The Cocos Ridge\index[places]{Cocos Ridge} serves as a border for the start of the wide continental margin characteristic of the \ac{MAT} \cite{Morgan1968}, \cite{Lemenkova2019108}. The Upper Jurassic-Cretaceous-Tertiary oceanic sediments occur in the slope of the GuatemalaTrench, a southern part of the \ac{MAT}, as well as in the Nicoya Complex of Costa Rica\index[places]{Costa Rica}. Deformation across a convergent tectonic plate boundary of \ac{MAT} correlate with gravity-driven and subduction-driven tectonic processes. 

Geochemistry of the sediments of the \ac{MAT} is presented by the olivine-plagioclase\index[concepts]{rock!igneous!olivine-plagioclase} phyric basalts\index[concepts]{rock!igneous!basalts, phyric}, the only igneous rocks is the study area, and plagioclase phyric high-alumina basalts\index[concepts]{rock!igneous!basalts, plagioclase phyric high-alumina}. Basalts with thickness layer of 7 m covered by 150 m of sedimentary layers were detected on the Cocos Plate section of \ac{MAT}. 

The petrographic features of \ac{MAT} basalts\index[concepts]{rock!igneous!basalt} are typical for the basalts of the ocean floor: subaphyric, with plagioclase phenocrysts dominant over olivine phenocrysts, composed of abundant plagioclase microlites\index[concepts]{rock!igneous!microlites}, olivine\index[concepts]{rock!igneous!olivine}, clinopyroxene\index[concepts]{rock!igneous!clinopyroxene} and rare spinel microcrysts, partly devitrified brown glass. \cite{Joronetal1982}. However, white some of the basalts are similar to the oceanic tholeiites\index[concepts]{rock!igneous!tholeiites magma series}, others have a composition transitional to island-arc tholeiitic basalts\index[concepts]{rock!igneous!tholeiites, island-arc}.  As described by \cite{Dmitriev1982}, this may indicate presence of magmatism in the zones of convergence of the oceanic and continental or island-arc crust. Petrochemical setting of the \ac{MAT} basalts show compositions typical for the oceanic tholeiites\index[concepts]{rock!igneous!tholeiites, oceanic} and transitional to island-arc tholeiites with advanced differentiation of high-alumina varieties in both types of rocks. Namely, such transitional-type basalts differ from rocks of the oceanic and island-arc basalts by values of TiO\textsubscript{2} concentration and the K\textsubscript{2}O/K\textsubscript{2}O/Na\textsubscript{2}O ratio, \cite{Lemenkova2019108}. 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=10cm]{Fig-MAT7.jpg}
	\caption{Middle America Trench: vertical gravity model map, \cite{Lemenkova2019108}}\label{map:MAT7}
\end{map}

Among other deep-sea trenches, chemically similar to \ac{MAT} tholeiitic basalts\index[concepts]{rock!igneous!basalt, tholeiitic} are detected in the transects of Philippine and Yap trenches. Young age of some basalts from the oceanward slopes of the \ac{MAT} may indicate evidences of the local magmatism\index[concepts]{Earth!magma!magmatism} caused by the zone of convergence of oceanic and continental crust located nearby. This explain the eruption of the transitional basalts as oceanic and island-arc tholeiites in the\ac{MAT}. Secondary minerals of the basalts of \ac{MAT} in studied transects include smectite and chlorite, as well as talc, calcite, phillipsite, mica, and mixed-layer chlorite-montmorillonite also fill veins in the basalts\index[concepts]{rock!igneous!basalt} of the trench \cite{KurnosovShevchenko}.

The \ac{MAT} runs parallel to the ancient structures that can be explained by the processes of paleosubduction. On the contrary, the Acapulco Trench, which is a northern part of the \ac{MAT}, intersects geologic structures of the southern Mexico. The differences in the south and northern parts of the \ac{MAT} are notable on the land- and oceanward sides. Thus, the impact of the  Tehuantepec and Cocos ridges differ on the seaward side, but very weak on the landward side of the \ac{MAT}. Thus, on the oceanward side, both ridges restrict the \ac{MAT} off the Middle America continental shelf, but on the continental side, the Tehuantepec Ridge produces only a slight offset in the geological structures, whereas the Cocos Ridge raises them as a whole \cite{Aubouinetal1982}. A system of major, active transform faults 'Motagua-Polochic system'\index[places]{Motagua-Polochic system} consists of the Motagua Fault\index[places]{Motagua Fault} and Polochic Fault\index[places]{Polochic Fault} (Map \ref{map:MAT5}, north), \cite{Lemenkova2019108}. 

The Motagua Fault runs across the Middle America, continuing offshore until it merges with the \ac{MAT} near the Acapulco forming part of the tectonic boundary between the North American Plate and the Caribbean Plate. The Polochic fault lies north and continues Motagua Fault in parallel, sharing motion between the North American and Caribbean Plates\index[places]{Caribbean Plate} \cite{LyonCaenetal2006}. The Motagua Fault caused several major earthquakes\index[concepts]{earthquake}, including the 7.5 Mw Middle America 1976 earthquake. The geologic structure of the \ac{MAT} includes Paleozoic crystalline basement rocks, Mesozoic\index[concepts]{Geologic time period!Mesozoic} intrusive igneous rocks and sedimentary rocks spanning the Cretaceous to the Pleistocene. The subduction of the Cocos Plate in the Paleogene\index[concepts]{Geologic time period!Paleogene} caused eruption of the volcanoes, which resulted in active melting and formation of magma. The volcanoes are mainly located along the Pacific Volcanic Chain\index[places]{Pacific Volcanic Chain} (Map \ref{map:GeolW}) that runs in parallel to the \ac{MAT}.

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=10cm]{Fig-MAT8.jpg}
	\caption{Middle America Trench: topographic surface map modeled by ASCII data, \cite{Lemenkova2019108}}\label{map:MAT8}
\end{map}

Northern part of the study area is distanced by the location of Chortis Block\index[places]{Chortis Block}, a group unit of the rocks originated as continental crust in Nicaragua\index[places]{Nicaragua}. The Nicoya Peninsula \index[places]{Nicoya Peninsula} (south-east on Map \ref{map:MAT5}) is formed by a volcanic-sedimentary series determined by radiolarian assemblages as Upper Jurassic and Cretaceous \cite{SchmidtEffing1979}. The distribution of the ophiolites and other geologic features is presented on Map \ref{map:MAT5}, \cite{Galli1979}. The schematic map of the geological setting of the study area is visualized on Map \ref{map:MAT5} by available data from the \ac{GMT} and \ac{USGS} sources: bathymetry, tectonic plates movement directions, distribution of volcanoes, ophiolites, ridges and tectonic slabs in the \ac{MAT} area.

Marine biological analysis of the samples taken from the \ac{MAT} \cite{Shipboard19824} shows calcareous benthic, planktonic foraminifers, and calcareous nannoplankton recovered within sediment cores along the trench axis that show relatively high faunal diversity. Their presence at great depth indicates rapid sediment transport and burial. The abundance of the biogenic silica throughout the sediment section indicated that continental slope of \ac{MAT} had high biological productivity environment. The conglomerates of the trench indicate rapid sedimentation of the deposits accumulated in a trench slope that proved a relatively quiet environment of sediment deposition \cite{Coulbourn1982}. The samples from the \ac{MAT} revealed areas composed of hemipelagic muds containing substantial biogenic components that proves a high degree of bioturbation: the samples consist of diatoms, radiolarians, sponge spicules, calcareous nannoplankton, and foraminifers of lower to middle bathyal benthic species \cite{Shipboard495}. 

The 'Glomar Challenger' expedition sampling shown that selected spots on \ac{MAT} predominantly consist of dark olive gray biogenic muds. The terrigenous component of the sediments is dominant and consists of clay minerals and minor contributions of volcanic glass. Biogenous contribution is secondary and consists of ca. 50\% diatoms and nannofossils and biogenic debris (foraminifers and sponge spicules), \cite{Lemenkova2019108}.

Brief sedimentation history of the \ac{MAT} area was summarized by \cite{Kurnosovetal1982}: the sedimentation on the continental slope was supplied by a smectite- and plagioclase-rich terrigenous land source area during the Late Cretaceous. In middle Miocene a volcanic source existed in \ac{MAT} area, as proved by the volcanic glasses followed by the persisted volcaniclastic constituents. Major sediment types of \ac{MAT} include turbidites, hemipelagic sediments, chalk, basalt\index[concepts]{rock!igneous!basalt} and slope sediments \cite{Ross1971}, \cite{Lemenkova2019108}. Sedimentation processes on the \ac{MAT} began in the early Miocene with an increased accumulation of the metalliferous foraminifer-nannofossil ooze above the basalt\index[concepts]{rock!igneous!basalt} basement. The basal chalk, a source of Fe and Mn, presents a typical mid-oceanic metalliferous sediment type, located near the spreading center of \ac{MAT}. Sedimentation time gap at \ac{MAT} in the middle Miocene\index[concepts]{Geologic time period!Miocene!Middle} to Early Pliocene\index[concepts]{Geologic time period!Pliocene!Early} refers to a radiolarian-bearing pelagic clay and weak supply of terrigenous sediment at this period, which in turn may indicate remote location of the site from the land masses. The Cocos Plate moved away from its spreading center toward the subduction zone in the \ac{MAT} during this period. 

During Pleistocene, the hemipelagic sedimentation was substituted for the turbidite accumulation that reached present location on the trench seafloor\index[concepts]{seafloor}. Sites located at the juncture of the Middle America slope and the \ac{MAT} seafloor\index[concepts]{seafloor} contain biogenic debris, including wood fragments. The sequence of the Quaternary\index[concepts]{Geologic time period!Quaternary} trench-filling turbidites is either faulted or deposited against a fault scarp in lower Miocene chalk on the detected sites of the \ac{MAT} \cite{Shipboard1982500}, \cite{Cowan1982}. 

Lithologic variation and differing compaction states at the \ac{MAT} show \cite{Faas1982} that sediments on the continental slope have identical compaction as sediments in the trench, yet their compaction curve is intermediate between pelagic clays and diatom ooze. The sediments in the \ac{MAT} have intermediate compaction level between the pelagic clays and terrigenous sediments, which is caused by the mix of pelagic sediments with terrestrially derived turbidites. Another particular feature of the \ac{MAT} consists in partly consolidated lower Miocene\index[concepts]{Geologic time period!Miocene} to Quaternary\index[concepts]{Geologic time period!Quaternary} hemipelagic muds that contain vertical-subvertical veins, <2 mm thick, spaced from one to several millimeters apart. Apart from the \ac{MAT}, such morphologically analogous structures were obtained from the inner slope of the Japan Trench\index[places]{Japan Trench}.

Ocean crust of the Cocos Plate subducting beneath the landward slope of the \ac{MAT} has a linear horst\index[concepts]{fault!horst} and graben\index[concepts]{fault!graben} topography of hundreds m relief. This passive assimilation of oceanic material occurs without disturbing topography shape. The subduction of the horst\index[concepts]{fault!horst} and graben\index[concepts]{fault!graben} in the \ac{MAT} area is largely passive and locally active. Mid-slope area has rugged topography covered by thick slope deposits, while lower slope is smooth except local brakes by benches. Upper and middle slope areas are correlated with strong magnetic anomalies. Rough midslope topography may reflect erosion following the Paleocene\index[concepts]{Geologic time period!Paleocene} uplift and local subsidence in the early Miocene. Slope deposits covered the \ac{MAT} landward slope, correlate with the increased arc volcanism\index[concepts]{volcanism} indicated by ash layers \cite{Hueneetal1982}. A thick sequence of the terrigenous sediments was discovered seaward of the \ac{MAT} \cite{Shipboard495}. More detailed insight to the origins of the geomorphology of the \ac{MAT} and factors affecting formation of its landforms, have been studied in relevant papers \cite{Shipboard6}, \cite{AlvarezGomezetal2008}, \cite{Shipboard1982499}, \cite{AzemaTournon}, \cite{AlvarezGomezetal2012}.

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\pagebreak
\section{Peru-Chile Trench}

\begin{multicols}{2}
The \ac{PCT}\index[places]{Peru-Chile Trench}, alternatively called Atacama Trench\index[places]{Atacama Trench} \cite{Gambietal2003}, is the longest hadal trench of the Pacific Ocean (Map \ref{map:PCT1}). It stretches on 5,900 km from Ecuador\index[places]{Ecuador} to Chile\index[places]{Chile}  $6^\circ 00^\prime$N, $81^\circ 50^\prime$W to $39^\circ 00^\prime$S, $75^\circ 00^\prime$W \cite{FischerRaitt}, Map \ref{map:PCT1}. 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=8cm]{Fig-PCT1.jpg}
	\caption{Peru-Chile Trench: bathymetric map, ETOPO1 \cite{Lemenkova2019107}}\label{map:PCT1}
\end{map}

In its total length, the trench covers an area of ca. 590,000 km2 \cite{Angel1982}. The \ac{PCT} is shallower comparing to other trenches of the Pacific Ocean (64 km width). Reaching maximal depth of 8,065 m, it is deepest trench of the South Pacific Ocean\index[places]{Pacific Ocean, south}. The \ac{PCT} is formed by the subduction of the Nazca\index[places]{Nazca Plate} and Antarctic plates\index[places]{Antarctic Plate} beneath the South American Plate\index[places]{South American Plate}. As followed by its name and location, \ac{PCT} can be logically divided into two parts: Peruvian and Chilean one. The Chilean part is longer than Peruvian, stretching in a NS direction along 4,300 km, from -18\degree S to -56\degree S. 
	
\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=8cm]{Fig-PCT2.jpg}
	\caption{Peru-Chile Trench: geological map \cite{Lemenkova2019107}}\label{map:PCT2}
\end{map}

Due to the specific geologic, geotectonic, geomorphic and climatic characteristics, the region of the \ac{PCT} is exposed to a wide variety of hazardous processes such as earthquakes\index[concepts]{earthquake}, local tsunamis, generated between the \ac{PCT} and Chilean\index[places]{Chile} coasts, volcanism\index[concepts]{volcanism} and continent-specific geological hazards, e.g. floods, landslides and mass movements, as briefly discussed below. 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=8cm]{Fig-PCT3.jpg}
	\caption{Peru-Chile Trench: geoid regional model \cite{Lemenkova2019107}}\label{map:PCT3}
\end{map}

Volcanism\index[concepts]{volcanism} intensity in the study area can be illustrated by recent documented tsunamigenic giant earthquakes: Valdivia\index[places]{Valdivia} earthquake, the largest ever recorded (Mw = 9.5), 1960 and the Maule\index[places]{Maule} earthquake (Mw = 8.8), 2010 \cite{Contrerasetal2010}. 

Bathymetry of the \ac{PCT} has following distinguished features. The continental margin of Southern Chile between 36\degree\space S and 39\degree\space S has intermediate shelf of width 20-30 km \cite{Voelkeretal2008}. Here the gradient of the continental slope is inclined at 2.5\degree-4.0\degree with frequent irregularities: plateaus, slope basins and escarpments as steep as 10\degree. South Chilean slope is dissected by four major and some minor meandering submarine canyons (Biobío\index[places]{Biobío Canyon}, Imperial\index[places]{Imperial Canyon}, Tolten\index[places]{Tolten Canyon} and Calle-Calle\index[places]{Calle-Calle Canyon}) connected to the large rivers flowing off the continent. 

The Biobío Canyon ends in submarine fans where it enter the trench \cite{ThornburgKulm}. Slightly sinuous axial channel is observed in the Chilean Trench, with a width of a 7-10 km and depth ca. 150 m \cite{Voelkeretal2008}. It originates from the Chacao Canyon at 41\degree S and connected to the exits of the largest submarine canyon systems of the continental margin (Calle-Calle, Tolten, Biobío and San Antonio Canyons\index[places]{San Antonio Canyon}). 

Nazca Plate\index[places]{Nazca Plate} forms oceanward boundary of the \ac{PCT}, elevating ca. 100-400 m comparing to the trench and showing parallel geological lineaments. Nazca Plate\index[places]{Nazca Plate} has chains of 2-km-high seamounts\index[concepts]{seamounts} aligned almost parallel to the complex subduction of the Valdivia\index[places]{Valdivia Fracture Zone} and Mocha Fracture Zones\index[places]{Mocha Fracture Zone} \cite{ContrerasReyesetal2013}, Map \ref{map:PCT2}. More details on the complexity of the processes in the oceanic fracture zones, affected by high degree of hydration in the crust and upper mantle is discussed by \cite{Contreras2008}. Geophysical setting of the study area in the Peru-Chile area are shown on Map \ref{map:PCT4}.
	
The \ac{PCT} is formed by the subduction of the Oligocene\index[concepts]{Geologic time period!Oligocene} oceanic crust of the Nazca Plate and Antarctic\index[places]{Antarctic Plate} tectonic plates beneath the South American Plate at the \ac{PCT}. The convergence rate of the Nazca Plate\index[places]{Nazca Plate} is 6.7–6.9 cm/a at an angle of ac. 80\degree\space \cite{Geersen2019}. The tectonic setting of the trench is complex, formed by a set of the processes of tectonic plates subduction, sediments accretion and their implications on the \ac{PCT}. The Mocha\index[places]{Mocha} crustal block, configured by the \ac{PCT}, Mocha and Valdivia fracture zones, lies on the Nazca Plate\index[places]{Nazca Plate} between ca. 37.5\degree\space and 40\degree\space S. It separates two segments Maule\index[places]{Maule} segment to the north and  Chiloé\index[places]{Chiloé segment} segment to the south \cite{Contrerasetal2010}. 

Subduction processes in a historical context of the evolution of the Nazca and Antarctic\index[places]{Antarctic Plate} tectonic plates and their formation along the western margin of South America\index[places]{South America} has been thoroughly described (e.g., \cite{Sarmientoetal2006}; \cite{Maneaetal2017}; \cite{Sdroliasetal2004}; \cite{Cande}; \cite{Scheuberetal1994}; \cite{Braz}; \cite{Schellartetal2006}. Central Andes\index[places]{Andes, Central} is a typical example of the convergent continental margin where the forearc, an important element directly reflects the interaction between the subducting and overriding plates \cite{Matheretal2014}. The dynamic interaction between the active Chile Ridge\index[places]{Chile Ridge} and the continental margin determines the Taitao Triple Junction\index[places]{Taitao Triple Junction} (46\degree-47\degree\space S). 

North of the triple junction, Nazca Plate\index[places]{Nazca Plate} is subducted beneath the South American Plate in an ENE direction at a rate of 8.4 cm/year. To the south, the Antarctic Plate\index[places]{Antarctic Plate} subducts along the E direction at only 2.4 cm/year \cite{CecioniPineda}. The Nazca Plate adjacent the \ac{PCT} is placed at the eastern boundary of the East Pacific Rise\index[places]{East Pacific Rise}, which is the fastest-spreading ocean ridge on Earth. The Nazca Plate is bordered by two microplates: Easter microplate\index[places]{Easter microplate} and the Juan Fernandez microplate\index[places]{Juan Fernández microplate}. In turn, these include three major seamount chains: the Easter Seamount Chain\index[places]{Easter Seamount Chain}, the Nazca Ridge\index[places]{Nazca Ridge}, and the Carnegie Ridge\index[places]{Carnegie Ridge} \cite{Bello-Gonzalezetal2018}. The huge earthquake\index[concepts]{earthquake} of 1960 resulted because of the subduction of the Mocha fracture zone\index[places]{Mocha Fracture Zone} which caused stress concentration and rupture of the Nazca Plate\cite{Cifuentes}; \cite{VitaFinzi}.

Main geomorphological structural units in Chile\index[places]{Chile} from west to east are Coastal Cordillera\index[places]{Coastal Cordillera}, Central Depression\index[places]{Central Depression} or Central valleys\index[places]{Central valley}, and Andean Cordillera\index[places]{Andean Cordillera}. The distinguished submarine geomorphological features of the \ac{PCT} area include submarine ridges: Juan Fernández Ridge\index[places]{Juan Fernández Ridge}, Copiapó Ridge\index[places]{Copiapó Ridge}, Taltal Ridge\index[places]{Taltal Ridge}, Nazca Ridge\index[places]{Nazca Ridge}, and Iquique Ridge\index[places]{Iquique Ridge}. The Iquique Ridge is composed of several seamounts\index[concepts]{seamounts} (b1.5 km high) surrounded by a broad region of shallow seafloor, swelling up to 250 km wide in some areas and >500 m in elevation. The submarine Taltal Ridge intersects the \ac{PCT} at 25\degree\space S offshore \cite{ContrerasReyesCarrizo2011}. Other geomorphic features are O'Higgins Guyot\index[places]{O'Higgins Guyot} and Eastern seamount chain\index[places]{Easter Seamount Chain} (Map \ref{map:PCT2}). The place of bending of the \ac{PCT} on two parts (Peru\index[places]{Peru} and Chile\index[places]{Chile}, respectively) is the Arica bend\index[places]{Arica bend} (Map \ref{map:PCT2}). 

The geomorphology of the subducted slab in the \ac{PCT} region changes from flat to the steep one along its path beneath the Andes\index[places]{Andes}. The region where the Juan Fernandez Ridge\index[places]{Juan Fernández Ridge} is subducting beneath the continental margin (27\degree-33\degree\space S) correlates with a flat-slab subduction zone \cite{CecioniPineda}. On the contrary, in north- and southwards direction from this area, the Wadati-Benioff zone, i.e. a zone of seismicity\index[concepts]{seismicity} corresponding to the down-going tectonic slab in a subduction zone \cite{Pronin1981}, is steeper. The flat-slab subduction zone is located northwards of the Taitao Triple Junction\index[places]{Taitao Triple Junction}, or Chile Tripe Junction\index[places]{Chile Tripe Junction} \cite{Behrmann} where geomorphological feature of the Chilean Andes is characterized by the transverse river valleys that separates the Coastal Cordillera\index[places]{Cordillera, Coastal} from the Andean Cordillera\index[places]{Cordillera, Andean} (27\degree-33\degree\space S). 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=8cm]{Fig-PCT4.jpg}
	\caption{Peru-Chile Trench: map of gravity surface model \cite{Lemenkova2019107}}\label{map:PCT4}
\end{map}

Southwards 33\degree\space S, the geologic and geomorphic features in the Peru-Chine Trench region change due to the dip change of the subduction zone \cite{CecioniPineda}. \cite{Matheretal2014} report existence of >60 individual large-scale landslides located within the hyper-arid coastal Cordillera\index[places]{} of the Atacama Desert\index[places]{Atacama Desert}, Northern Chile\index[places]{Northern Chile} adjacent to the \ac{PCT}\index[places]{Peru-Chile Trench}. The system of such giant landslides, developed as a combination of rock avalanches and multiple rotational failures, is located along the coastline within a distance of ca. 650 km and contributes to the sedimentation of the \ac{PCT}. Other sources of sediments can include earthquakes\index[concepts]{earthquake}, e.g. sediment thickness in the \ac{PCT} area in the region of 1960 earthquake ranged from ca. 300 to 700 m, thus having significantly increased sedimentation \cite{PrinceKulm}. 

Over 50\% of the world's deep-sea trenches are classified as sediment starved or partly sediment filled \cite{Geersenetal2018}. Northern part of the \ac{PCT}, that is Peruvian segment\index[places]{Peru-Chile Trench!Peruvian}, is classified as 'sediment starved', while southern part (Chilean segment\index[places]{Peru-Chile Trench!Chilean}) is 'partly sediment filled', as illustrated by \cite{Geersen2019}. As a response of sediment loading at the trench, the downward deflection of the trench plays a crucial role in determining its inlet sediments capacity (the amount of the subducted sediments). Weak and hot oceanic plates show more downward deflection at the trench due to sediment loading comparing to the old oceanic plates \cite{ContrerasOsses2010}. Thus, weak oceanic plate involves a larger space for sediment subduction. 

From the environmental aspect of view, the Peru-Chile is considered as eutrophic, that is having high biological productivity and organic matter on the seafloor of the trench \cite{Laceyetal2016}. \cite{Gambietal2003} illustrates the uniqueness of the biodiversity within the \ac{PCT} system as follows: high concentrations of the nutritionally-rich organic matter also at 7800-m depth, high nematode densities (>6000 ind. 10cm-2). The geomorphology of the trench is crucial for the deep-sea biology. Geographic location and geomorphic structure of the trench have affects on its biomass. Trenches located closer to the continents receive more inputs of the organic matter from the adjacent shelves. This creates more food supplies for the continental slope with steep angles and less food for the slopes with low gradients. 

The availability of the nutrients largely relies on the flux of the depositing organic-rich matter \cite{Turnewitschetal2014}. The content of the geological $CO_{2}$ burial rates varies in the seafloor, with the increase in the Pacific basin \cite{Cartapanisetal2018}. Two distinctly different domains of the seafloor exist, on which carbon burial takes place: surfaces located by the fringes of the continents and the constantly submerged surfaces of continental slopes and the deep sea. The mixture of the geochemical components located at various depths of the continental slope and trench implies that sediments from upper to lower slope incorporate into a single bed \cite{Voelkeretal2008}. Examples of the geochemical complex circulation, system of deposition and transport include \cite{SattarovaAksentov2018}; \cite{Yangetal2018}; \cite{Lehuetal2015}.  

Specifically for the \ac{PCT}, larger downward deflection of the plate is found along the Chiloé segment\index[places]{Peru-Chile Trench!Chiloé} than along the Maule segment\index[places]{Peru-Chile Trench!Maule} \cite{ContrerasReyesetal2013}. Hence, according to the sedimentation conditions, the \ac{PCT} can be divided into two main provinces: the main sediment-free province extending 8\degree-32\degree\space S and the sedimentary province extending 33\degree-57\degree\space S \cite{Hayes}. The margin off Isla de Chiloé\index[places]{Peru-Chile Trench!Chiloé} is characterized by a largely sedimented segment of the \ac{PCT}. This is caused by the extension of turbidite deposits seaward reaching 250 km from the trench axis \cite{Contreras2007}. The region close to the Taitao Triple Junction\index[places]{Taitao Triple Junction} is nearly devoid of sediments and has rapid narrowing of the forearc region \cite{Behrmann}. 

\end{multicols}
%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\pagebreak
\section{Kermadec Trench}
\ac{KT} is located in southwest Pacific Ocean\index[places]{Pacific Ocean}, eastwards from Australia\index[places]{Australia}. It is located 120 km off the New Zealand with axis continuing from ca. 26\degree-36\degree\space S. It is the \nth{5} deepest trench in the world with a maximum depth of 10,177 m \cite{Jamieson2015} m and a length of 1500 km \cite{Jamiesonetal2011}, Map \ref{map:TK1}. 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=13cm]{Fig-TK-1.jpg}
	\caption{Tonga and Kermadec trenches: bathymetric map, ETOPO1}\label{map:TK1}
\end{map}

Southward continuation of the \ac{KT} is Hikurangi Trough\index[places]{Hikurangi Trough} located eastwards off the North Island of New Zealand lying between the southern end of the Cook Strait\index[places]{Cook Strait} and the Chatham Rise\index[places]{Chatham Rise}. A convergence at maximal rate ca. 249 mm/yr along the Tonga-Kermadec island arc\index[places]{Tonga-Kermadec Arc} system makes it one of the most seismically active subduction zones in the world \cite{Bevis}. 

Detailed description of the environmental, geological and tectonic settings of the \ac{KT} is in \cite{Smithetal2003}; \cite{Anderson}; \cite{Regelous}; \cite{SmithPrice}. The majority of research on \ac{KT} focus on the biota communities of the deep-sea ecosystems \cite{Leduc2015}, \cite{Leduc2016}, \cite{Nunnally} or tectonic plates in the Pacific Ocean \cite{Duncan}, \cite{Tappin}. Example of the morphological heterogeneity affected by the geographic isolation of the \ac{KT} gives a case study of New Hebrides and \ac{KT}, SW Pacific Ocean \cite{Laceyetal2018}. The effects of the geographical proximity and geological ages of the \ac{KT}, bottom current patterns and bathymetry on the patterns of the biogenetic structure are explained by geographical isolation and demographic independency of the marine species which preserve species endemism \cite{Ritchieetal2017}.  

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\pagebreak
\section{Tonga Trench}
\ac{TT}\index[places]{Tonga Trench} is located in southwest Pacific Ocean\index[places]{Pacific Ocean}, eastwards from Australia\index[places]{Australia} northwards from the \ac{KT}. The study area is focused on two hadal trenches located in south-west Pacific Ocean\index[places]{Pacific Ocean} northwards from Australia\index[places]{Australia} and New Zealand\index[places]{New Zealand}: \ac{KT} and Tonga. Their brief geographic description can be characterized as follows. 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=13cm]{Fig-TK-2.jpg}
	\caption{Kermadec and Tonga trenches: transect segments; geologic and tectonic settings.}\label{map:TK2}
\end{map}

Its closeness to the Antarctic makes it to be one of the coldest trenches in the world \cite{Belyaev}. \ac{KT} runs parallel to the Kermadec Ridge\index[places]{Kermadec Ridge} (southern part of the Tonga-Kermadec Ridge\index[places]{Tonga-Kermadec Ridge}) with geomorphology of V- shape formed by tectonic subduction of the Pacific Plate under the Indo-Australian Plate\index[places]{Indo-Australian Plate} extending from approximately 26\degree-36\degree\space S near the northeastern tip of New Zealand's North Island\index[places]{North Island} \cite{LeducRowden2018}. The specific conditions of the tectonic setting in the study area is the subduction of the Pacific Plate at a rapid rate beneath the Indo-Australian Plate\index[places]{Indo-Australian Plate} along the Tonga-Kermadec Trench \cite{Castilloetal2009}, Map \ref{map:TK2}, right. 

Similar to \ac{KT}, \ac{TT} belongs to the \ac{SPSG} biogeochemical province and has the same primary productivity rate of 87 g C m-2 y-1 \cite{Xuetal2018}. \ac{TT} is adjacent, continuing \ac{KT} northwards with axis stretching from ca. 15\degree-26\degree\space S. Located in close proximity to the \ac{KT}, it is separated by a sill located on the Tonga Platform\index[places]{Tonga Platform} \cite{Wrightetal2000} and by the Louisville Seamount Chain\index[places]{Louisville Seamount Chain} \cite{Jamiesonetal2013} (Map \ref{map:TK2}, right). With maximum depths of 10,882 m, \ac{TT} is the \nth{2} deepest trench in the world \cite{Blankenship}. Both trench axes have roughly 30\degree\space slight from the longitude line. Due to the complex interaction of various factors (ocean currents, closeness to the Antarctica\index[places]{Antarctica}) the deep Pacific Ocean seafloor underlying the trenches is notable for variable surface productivity \cite{Linleyetal2017}. Further studies on \ac{TT}\index[places]{Tonga Trench}, its environment and communities can be found in relevant literature \cite{Tappin}; \cite{Ewartetal1998}; \cite{Hergt}.

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\pagebreak
\section{Vanuatu (New Hebrides) Trench}
The \ac{VT}\index[places]{Vanuatu Trench} (alternative former spelling: New Hebrides Trench\index[places]{New Hebrides Trench}) is located in Fiji Basin, East Australia. 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=13cm]{Fig-VV1.jpg}
	\caption{Vanuatu and Vityaz trenches: topographic map}\label{map:VV1}
\end{map}

The region is notable for complex geophysical settings which resulted in the formation of the trenches, high seismicity\index[concepts]{seismicity}, geodynamic complexity and seismic instability. Vanuatu (New Hebrides) trench-arc system divides the New Hebrides back-arc troughs and the active North Fiji marginal basin. The Vanuatu arc stretches ca. 1200 km in the SW Pacific Ocean located in the area with coordinates 10\degree-23\degree\space S, 160\degree-180\degree\space E  (Fig. \ref{map:VV1}). Narrow \ac{VT} is bordered by the Vanuatu volcanic archipelago (New Hebrides), which is a submersed part of an oceanic island arc that extends  between 12\degree\space S and 25\degree\space S, between the Torres to Hunter Islands \citep{JeanBaptisteetal2016}, 1000 km to the northwest of the \ac{KT}. Geomorphology of the \ac{VT} is reported \citep{HeezenHollister} to have masses of rubble, talus slopes and fragmented outcrops.

\ac{VT} is a shallower trench of ca. 7156 m. Relative to other neighbour trenches (\ac{KT}, \ac{MT}), it is warmest, and underlies intermediate bio-productivity \citep{Linleyetal2017}. \ac{VT} has developed in a situation of complex tectonic settings. More specifically, it is located in a double convergent boundary between the Australian and Pacific tectonic plates \citep{Beaumais}. The tectonic development of the \ac{VT} has two distinct subduction stages \citep{JeanBaptisteetal2016}: 1) subduction of the Pacific Plate under the Indian-Australian plate westward direction; 2) subduction of the Indo-Australian plate beneath the North Fiji back-arc basin at the margin of the Pacific Plate, north-eastwards  direction. Therefore, the convergence is marked by two opposite subduction zones, defined by the \ac{VT} to the west and the \ac{TT} to the east, resulting in an asymmetric opening of the North Fiji back-arc basin \citep{Auzende}. 

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\pagebreak
\section{Vityaz Trench}
\ac{VtT}\index[places]{Vityaz Trench} is located in the North Fiji Basin, East Australia. It belongs to the New Hebrides trench-arc system, caused by the Indo-Australian subducting tectonic plate and a subduction of the d'Entrecasteaux ridge \cite{Charvis}. This area is notable for the double convergent complex boundary where Pacific Plate subducts under the Indian-Australian plate westwards, and Indo-Australian plate beneath the North Fiji back-arc basin northeastwards.

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=13cm]{Fig-VV2.jpg}
	\caption{Vanuatu and Vityaz trenches: geologic map}\label{map:VV2}
\end{map}

Previous surveys on bathymetry, magnetism, seismicity\index[concepts]{seismicity} and focal mechanism\index[concepts]{earthquake!focal!mechanism} in the region between the southern New Hebrides Arc and North Fiji Basin enlighten the geodynamic complexity and  instability of the area \citep{Mailletetal1989}. An active volcano Mt. Yasur is located in Tanna Island of the Vanuatu arc \citep{Spina}. Discussions on the geologic setting of the \ac{VT} exist in literature \cite{Monzieretal1997}; \cite{Sorbadere}; \cite{Turtle}; \cite{Louat}; \cite{Bourrouilh}; \cite{PilletPelletier}, where particular attention is paid to geochemistry, mineralogy, magmatism, volcanology, seismicity\index[concepts]{seismicity} and tectonics of the region. The New Hebrides island arc consists of numerous volcanoes that frequently form islands situated in a tectonically complex arc-backarc system \cite{Beier}, \cite{Calmantetal2003}, Fig. \ref{map:VV2}. 

Three flat, mainly covered by forests, islands of Mar\'{e}, Lifou and Ouv\'{e}a may illustrate tectonic deformation at various distances from the \ac{VT}. Loyalty Islands serve as tension cracks\index[concepts]{slope!instability!tension cracks} resulting from the elastic bulging of the Australian plate before its subduction at the \ac{VT} \citep{Bogdanovetal2011}. The New Hebrides archipelago is a complex reversed-arc system that divided into four major volcanic provinces \citep{CarneyMacFarlane1982}: 

\begin{enumerate}
    \item Western Belt is an Early Miocene extinct volcanic arc in the frontal arc; 
    \item Eastern Belt formed in the Middle Miocene, a calc-alkaline arc volcanism;
    \item Marginal Province submerged in the Early Pleistocene is shown by volcanic activity;
    \item Central Chain is a present volcanic line continues Marginal Province volcanism;
\end{enumerate}

The anomalous morphology of the central New Hebrides arc is caused by the subduction of the D’Entrecasteaux aseismic ridge which enters the central part of the \ac{VT}. Besides, there is a deformation distributed over numerous spreading ridges in Lau\index[places]{Lua} and North Fiji back-arc basins. Geometry of Lau and North Fiji\index[places]{Fiji, North} back-arc basins and segmentation of the New Hebrides arc are significantly influenced by the subduction of aseismic ridges: Louisville, D'Entrecasteaux and Loyalty \citep{Pelletieretal1998} (Fig. \ref{map:VV2}). The basaltic rocks\index[concepts]{rock!igneous!basalt} from the central and southern islands of the New Hebrides (Aneityum, Tanna, Erromango, Efate, Emae, Tongoa and Epi), have geochemical features typical of island arc volcanics. The basalts\index[concepts]{rock!igneous!basalt} from Futuna Island\index[places]{Futuna Island} display characteristics typical of calc-alkaline rocks \citep{Dupuy}.  

Conversion of the two tectonic plates at the ocean margins causes sinks or subduction of the oceanic plates. The mechanism of faulting in the subducting plates at the trench is well described \cite{Zhouetal2015}; \cite{Zhengetal2013}. The terrestrial crust appearing at the top of this new layer is formed either by new masses of the terrestrial crust caused by the arc volcanism\index[concepts]{volcanism}, or because of the piled up sediment deposits coming from the subducting lower tectonic plate \cite{HueneScholl1991}. Therefore, ocean margins with high seismicity\index[concepts]{seismicity}, repeated earthquakes\index[concepts]{earthquake} and volcanism\index[concepts]{volcanism} indicate areas of the collision and movements of the ocean plates and active sedimentation, where trenches are located. Such and other geodynamic processes of the deep subduction take place of the Pacific Plate underneath cause extensional events and volcanism\index[concepts]{volcanism} \cite{Zhuetal2009}. Specific changes in the geochemical content of the underlying seafloor has also beed detected.

Factors affecting plate subduction\index[concepts]{tectonic!plate!subduction} include hydrothermal factors \cite{Yoshikawaetal2012}, buoyancy forces\index[concepts]{buoyancy}, rheology\index[concepts]{deformation!rheology} of mantle minerals, stabilities of hydrous minerals, partial melting, mechanisms and rates of melt migration, kinetics of phase transformations\index[concepts]{transformation!kinetics}\index[concepts]{transformation!phase}, mechanisms of deep earthquakes\index[concepts]{earthquake!deep}, geochemical recycling\index[concepts]{geochemical recycling}, and the processes of continental collision\index[concepts]{continental!collision} that result from the subduction \cite{Milleretal2005}. One fundamental problem concerns the depth to which subducted lithosphere\index[concepts]{Earth!lithosphere} penetrates into the mantle because this is related to the scale of mantle convection and the Earth’s evolution\index[concepts]{Earth!evolution}.

Finally, active sedimentation on the bottom of the seafloor leads to the accumulated amount at rifted margins which contributes to the formation of the \ac{VtT}. Sediments outflowing further to the ocean provide important geological bodies and resources. Natural hazards taking place in the ocean, strongly correlate with submarine earthquakes and volcanic eruptions during active rifting \cite{Brune2016}. Moreover, there is a certain correlation between the high oceanic features and thickness of the subduction channels and earthquake rupture segments \cite{ContrerasReyesCarrizo2011}. \ac{VtT} result from the complex geodynamic processes that continuously shape the surface of the trench' seafloor\index[concepts]{seafloor} \cite{BogolepovChikov1976}. Nowadays, the its seafloor\index[concepts]{seafloor} demonstrates ‘footprints’ of the many continuous steps of the seafloor\index[concepts]{seafloor} evolution. 

Tectonic plates act as giant radiators of the heat cooling, thickening, and gradually subsiding by progressing from ridge to trench. For example, it explains the appearance of the double seismic zone beneath the Mariana island arc\index[places]{Mariana Islands} \citep{SamowitzForsyth1981}. In such a way, tectonic plates form the large-scale pattern of the system of ridges and deep ocean basins\index[concepts]{deep-sea!basins}. The deformation of the upper plates together with surface migration of the ocean trenches of the Pacific Ocean\index[places]{Pacific Ocean} are the prevailing observables of the dynamics of the inter parts of the plates \citep{Milleretal2004}. 

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\pagebreak
\section{New Britain (Bougainville) Trench}
The \ac{NBT}\index[places]{New Britain Trench} (a.k.a. Bougainville Trench\index[places]{Bougainville Trench}) is located eastwards off Papua New Guinea\index[places]{Papua New Guinea} as northern borders of the Solomon Sea\index[places]{Solomon Sea}, Pacific Ocean\index[places]{Pacific Ocean} (Map \ref{map:NBT1}). The region is one of the world's most active subduction zones at the triple junction of the Pacific Plate, Indo-Australian Plate\index[places]{Indo-Australian Plate} and Solomon Sea Plate\index[places]{Solomon Sea Plate}. The Woodlark Basin\index[places]{Woodlark Basin} subducts beneath the Solomon Islands arc\index[places]{Solomon island arc} region, forming a double-sided subduction zone \cite{Yoneshimaetal2005}. As the region is situated in the complex zone of the convergence at the tectonic plates boundary, it is trapped between the converging Ontong Java Plateau\index[places]{Ontong Java Plateau} and Australia\index[places]{Australia}. Geologically, the area belongs to one of the most prospective for intrusion-related mineral deposits \cite{Holmetal2019}.

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=13cm]{Fig-NBT1.jpg}
	\caption{New Britain and San Cristobal trenches: topographic map}\label{map:NBT1}
\end{map}

\ac{NBT} is defined by the 6,000 m isoline. It is a narrow, 50-75 km wide trench extending northeasterly from the eastern end of the Huon Gulf\index[places]{Huon Gulf} along the south coast of New Britain\index[places]{New Britain Island} \cite{Mammerickx}. Its name is derived from the homonymous New Britain Island from the New Britain arc\index[places]{New Britain arc} (Map \ref{map:NBT1}). Its western extension are the north-dipping Ramu-Markham Fault zone\index[places]{Ramu-Markham Fault} and the West Bismarck arc\index[places]{West Bismarck arc} \cite{Abbott1994}. Northern part is characterized by the Bismark Sea\index[places]{Bismark Sea} back-arc basin (Fig. \ref{map:NBT2}) where active rifting and seafloor\index[concepts]{seafloor} spreading take place. The Bismark Sea back-arc basin\index[places]{Bismark Sea back-arc basin} comprises the South\index[places]{South Bismarck Plate} and North Bismarck\index[places]{North Bismarck Plate} microplates separated by the Bismarck Sea fault\index[places]{Bismarck Sea fault} \cite{Taylor1979}. The islands of eastern Papua New Guinea\index[places]{Papua New Guinea} and the Solomon islands\index[places]{Solomon island arc} are island arc terranes, surrounding \ac{NBT}, formed adjacent to the boundary between the Australia\index[places]{Australian Plate} and Pacific plates\index[places]{Pacific Plate} \cite{Baldwinetal2012}.

The subduction along the \ac{NBT} is crucial for the tectonic development in Papua New Guinea\index[places]{Papua New Guinea} because comparing to other Pacific trenches, it differs in its seismic structure \cite{Lindley2006}. Thus, it indicates a steeply dipping (>80\degree) structure to upper mantle depths (630 km), unlike other Pacific trenches where dips range between 45\degree-60\degree. Active volcanic arc along the north coast of New Britain Island (Fig. \ref{map:NBT2}, red triangles) correlates with the subduction curve along the \ac{NBT}\index[places]{New Britain Trench}. More discussion on \ac{NBT} tectonics and structure exist in existing papers \cite{Weibenga1973}; \cite{Cooperetal1989}. 

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\pagebreak
\section{San Cristobal Trench}
\ac{SCT}\index[places]{San Cristobal Trench} stretches in SE direction from the junction from the New Britain along the group of Solomon Islands\index[places]{Solomon island arc}: Bougainville\index[places]{Bougainville Island}, Choiseul\index[places]{Choiseul Island}, Santa Isabel\index[places]{Santa Isabel Island}, Malaita\index[places]{Malaita Island} until it reaches San Cristobal Island\index[places]{San Cristobal Island} from which it derived the name. The Woodlark Basin\index[places]{Woodlark Basin} is adjacent to the \ac{SCT}\index[places]{San Cristobal Trench} to the southwest and to the south of the Solomon Islands arc\index[places]{Solomon island arc} region (Fig. \ref{map:NBT2}). It is a young (ca. 5 Ma) oceanic basin that subducts beneath the \ac{NBT} \cite{Yoneshimaetal2005}. The eastern part of the Solomon Plate\index[places]{Solomon Sea Plate} bordering the \ac{SCT}, enters the front of the Pacific Plate\index[places]{Pacific Plate}. As a consequence, it results of significant earthquakes along the Solomon Plate \cite{Yangetal2018c}.

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=12cm]{Fig-NBT3.jpg}
	\caption{New Britain and San Cristobal trenches: geoid model map}\label{map:NBT3}
\end{map}

The environment of the hadal trench presents a complex system with highly interconnected factors affecting geomorphological structure, formation and development of the trench: slabs and tectonics plates, bathymetry, geographic location, geologic structure of the underlying basement and sediment thickness \cite{Lemenkova201871}. Further detailed explanation on structure and these factors can be found in existing literature \cite{Daviesetal1987}; \cite{FinlaysonCull}; \cite{Tregoningetal1998}; \cite{Johnson1976}; \cite{Lindley1988}; \cite{Ripper1970}; \cite{Honzaetal1987} describing bathymetry, geomorphology, sedimentation, submarine canyons and terraces of the \ac{NBT}\index[places]{New Britain Trench}. However, these work are mainly restricted to the western part of the Solomon Sea\index[places]{Solomon Sea} and are based on the existing data at that time. Current study proposes a comparative analysis of the \ac{NBT} with its contiguous \ac{SCT}\index[places]{San Cristobal Trench}. Moreover, this study is supported by modern datasets (\ac{SRTM}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!SRTM} 15 sec \ac{DEM}, gravity and geoid grids\index[concepts]{geoid}) using fine cartographic toolset \ac{GMT}.

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=12cm]{Fig-NBT2.jpg}
	\caption{New Britain and San Cristobal trenches: geological and tectonic map}\label{map:NBT2}
\end{map}

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=12cm]{Fig-NBT4.jpg}
	\caption{New Britain and San Cristobal trenches: gravimetry model map}\label{map:NBT4}
\end{map}

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\pagebreak
\section{Philippine Trench}

\ac{PT}\index[places]{Philippine Trench} is located in the axe of the collision of the \ac{PSP} and Sunda Plate, west Pacific Ocean. 
	
\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
 		\includegraphics[width=13cm]{Fig-PM1.jpg}
	\caption{Philippine Sea Basin: GEBCO based bathymetric map}
\label{map:PM1}       
\end{map}

\ac{PSB} is marked by the complex interaction of three lithospheric plates: Eurasian, Australian and the \ac{PSP}, which includes collision, subduction and accretion. Old, heavy and large (103,300,000 $km^{2}$) Pacific plate plays the major role comparing to the Australian Plate (47,000,000 $km^{2}$) and the \ac{PSP} 5,500,000 $km^{2}$\cite{Alden2019}. The morphology of the Pacific plate has a low dip angle at shallow depths. The \ac{PSP}, a large and tectonically complex region of the western Pacific located between the Pacific, Eurasian and Australian plates, is the world’s largest marginal basin plate \cite{Sdroliasetal2004}. The \ac{PSP} has two back-arc basins formed in Oligocene\index[concepts]{Geologic time period!Oligocene} to Miocene\index[concepts]{Geologic time period!Miocene} period: Parece Vela and Shikoku Basins (Fig. \ref{map:PM2}). 

Slab dynamics\index[concepts]{slab!subduction!dynamics} is one of the important driving forces for the trench formation affecting the mechanisms of its migration (retreat\index[concepts]{slab!subduction!retreat} or advance\index[concepts]{slab!subduction!advance}). It is therefore crucial to characterize the origin of the subducting\index[concepts]{slab!subduction} slab morphology\index[concepts]{slab!morphology} in the deep mantle identifying the features of subduction zones, which are among the fundamental issues of solid Earth \cite{Yoshida2017}. Effects of slab mineralogy and phase chemistry on the subduction dynamics\index[concepts]{slab!subduction!dynamics} (buoyancy\index[concepts]{slab!subduction!buoyancy}, stress field\index[concepts]{slab!subduction!stress field}) kinematics (rate of subduction\index[concepts]{slab!subduction!rate} and plate motion), elasticity (deformation and seismic wave speed\index[concepts]{wave!seismic}), thermometry (effects of latent heat, isobaric superheating) and seismicity\index[concepts]{seismicity} (adiabatic shear instabilities)\index[concepts]{seismicity!adiabatic shear instabilities} are discussed previously \cite{Binaetal2001}. 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
 		\includegraphics[width=13cm]{Fig-PM2.jpg}
	\caption{Philippine Sea Basin: Geologic map. }
\label{map:PM2}       
\end{map}

The geologic map shown on Fig. \ref{map:PM2} indicates boundaries between the lithospheric plates (solid red lines). Yellow fronts denote trench axes. Tectonic slab contours are depicted by the red dashed lines. Green arrows and relevant numbers indicate the convergence rates (mm/year) along the trenches. Magenta triangles denote hydrothermal areas mostly located along the trench. The morphology and development of the subducted slabs are complex \cite{Christensen1996}, on either mantle flow or convective layering at 660 km depth. Slabs can deflect horizontally in the upper mantle transition zone beneath some convergent margins whereas penetration to lower mantle depths occur beneath other island arc segments. As a result, different styles of the subduction across the upper mantle transition zone affect trench formation and are reflected in its geomorphology. 

Double seismic zone beneath the Mariana Island arc is explained \cite{SamowitzForsyth1981} by the following conceptual scheme of the processes of the global plate tectonics. Subduction of the cooled plate into the mantle causes formation of the deep ocean trenches where, as a consequence, earthquakes and tsunamis originate. Active volcanism\index[concepts]{volcanism} and seismic situation around Mariana arc show recorded events at the cross-sections of the earthquake transects. Subducting lithospheric plates act as giant radiators of the heat cooling, thickening, and progressively subsiding from ridge to trench. As a result, spreading seafloor\index[concepts]{seafloor}, affected by moving plates, creates an axial rift, corrugated hills, and ridges, formed by the nearby faults. 

Plate subduction along the \ac{IBM} trench caused trench roll-back, arc rupture and back-arc rifting of two interconnected back-arc basins: Parece Vela and Shikoku Basins. The \ac{KPR}, a remnant arc of the active \ac{IBM} system results from the spreading of the Shikoku Basin (Fig. \ref{map:PM2}). It plays a key role in the subduction process of the west Philippine Sea Plate: the subduction zone here is characterized by the \ac{KPR} subducting beneath the Kyushu Arc (Fig. \ref{map:PM2}) \cite{Caoetal2014}. A set of various geo dynamic processes including subduction-interface rheology, phase-transition buoyancy, slab stagnation\index[concepts]{slab!subduction!stagnation}, rollback of mantle and ridge-push effects, cause significant trench motion of the \ac{IBM} detected as advance by global plate-motion observations \cite{CizkovaBina2015}. 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
 		\includegraphics[width=13cm]{Fig-PM6.jpg}
	\caption{Geoid model of the \ac{PSB}}
\label{map:PM6}       
\end{map}

Seismic\index[concepts]{seismicity} phenomena is the \ac{PSB} (Fig. \ref{map:PM3}) can be explained as the result of tectonic processes at the edges of the lithospheric plates that spread apart at the ocean ridges along the large strike-slip faults and converge at the hot and weak volcanic island arcs of \ac{IBM} and the Philippines. Seismicity\index[concepts]{seismicity} map (Fig. \ref{map:PM3}) in the \ac{PSB} area shows that most earthquakes are confined to the marginal areas presenting a narrow, continuous belts around large stable areas of the \ac{PSP}. The activity differs by divergence and convergence zones being moderate in the zones of plate divergence and including deep shocks at shallow depths in the zones of plate convergence. Seismic data on focal mechanisms\index[concepts]{earthquake!focal!mechanism} are presented at \ac{CMT} catalogue which gives a relative direction of tectonic plates motion throughout active belts. Focal mechanisms\index[concepts]{earthquake!focal!mechanism} point at relative motions of the lithosphere\index[concepts]{Earth!lithosphere} plates determined from magnetic and topographic data associated with the zones of plate divergence. 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
 		\includegraphics[width=13cm]{Fig-PM7.jpg}
	\caption{Gravity model of the \ac{PSB}}
\label{map:PM7}       
\end{map}

A phenomena of the deep-sea trench includes its migration that depends primarily on the age at the trench \cite{Faccennaetal2009}, \cite{Gutscher}: located on the place of the subduction plate boundary at a given time, the trench may change its location over time as a result of the complex processes of the global plate tectonics. As proved by \cite{Lallemandetal2008}, trench migration and hence, geomorphic fluctuations, depends on the lower plate parameters and controlled by the subducting plate velocity $V_{sub}$. From the more recent publications on \ac{PT} dynamics it has been noted \cite{Yoshida2017} that fluctuations in the trench dynamics affect slab morphology and character. Another particular feature of the \ac{PT} regions is tectonic plate tearing in the northwestern corner of the subducting \ac{PSP} discussed in detailed by \cite{Linetal2013a}.

\cite{YuChang1991} keep track of the lateral fluctuations in the upper mantle structure of the \ac{PSB}, which is later more detailed studied by \cite{Yuetal2000} with a more close focus on the geophysics across the Philippine Sea. In some previous case studies \cite{Menard1955}; \cite{Macdonald2001}; \cite{NelsonKulm1973}, using available methods of the oceanographic mapping existing at that time, the geomorphic patterns of the deep-sea channels, submarine fans and their topography were analyzed with regards to the deep-water sedimentation \cite{Divins2003}, mid-ocean ridge tectonics and volcanism\index[concepts]{volcanism}, dynamics of the Philippine back arc \cite{Changetal2007}; \cite{Fujiokaetal1999}; \cite{Halletal1995b}, eaqthquakes and gravitation \cite{Lin-Lo2013}. 

There are proven relationships between the geomorphic structure of the submarine landforms and sedimentation in the selected parts of the Ocean \cite{Dolotov2010}. Other factors include oceanology as underwater currents \cite{Gilleetal2004}, geology \cite{Harrisetal2014}, tectonics \cite{HarrisWhiteway2011}, earthquakes in the Western \ac{PSP} \cite{Linetal2013b}, accumulation of the sedimentation that goes into the hadal trenches through the canyon-channel systems \cite{Covault2011}. 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
 		\includegraphics[width=12cm]{Fig-PM13.jpg}
	\caption{3D relief model: Philippine Trench (south segment)}
\label{map:PM13}       
\end{map}

\ac{PT} is located along the eastern edge of the Philippine archipelago\index[places]{Philippine Archipelago}, which is a deformed orogenic belt resulting from the collision of oceanic and continental blocks \cite{Karig1983}. Here, the \ac{PSP} subducts under the Philippine archipelago  (4\degree-15\degree\space N), which is a part of a convergence zone between the Eurasian plate (Sunda block) and the \ac{PSP} \cite{PerezTsutsumi2017}. Subducting \ac{PSP} cause a high-velocity anomaly in the \ac{KPR} area. The \ac{PSP} tears and forms a ‘slab window’ corresponding to the \ac{KPR} subduction \cite{Caoetal2014}. Tearing of the tectonic plate in the northwestern corner of the subducting \ac{PSP} is further discussed by \cite{Linetal2013a}. The \ac{PSB} is a complicated region, notable for complex bathymetry and tectonic plate shape \cite{Yamazakietal2010}, consisting of many different patchworks of the seafloor\index[concepts]{seafloor} ages \cite{CizkovaBina2015}. 

Because of the location in the Philippine and Sunda tectonic slabs collision, the Philippine Fault is proven to have motion in its dynamics \cite{Hiranoetal1986}; \cite{Yu1982}; \cite{Yehetal2013}; \cite{Salahetal2009}; \cite{KatoJordan1999}; \cite{Isseetal2010}. The northwestward motion of the Philippine Sea Plate is partially absorbed by the subduction of first, the \ac{PT} moving westwards, second, the East Luzon Trough \index[places]{East Luzon Trough} located in the north-east, and third, by the Philippine Fault\index[places]{Philippine Fault}. Along with geologic factors, tectonic instability of the Philippine Sea Trench necessarily causes variations in the structure of its geomorphology, which is studied in this paper.  

\ac{PT} has been studied with respect to the geophysical processes and geological settings at selected sample points margins. This interest is in part due to correlation that exist between tectonic plate movements and historical evolution of the geological processes \cite{HildeLee1984}; \cite{SenoMaruyama1984}; \cite{Ramosetal2012}; \cite{DeschampsLallemand2003}; \cite{Chadwicketal2018}. There is also evidence of deep-sea species existing on the seafloor\index[concepts]{seafloor} of the \ac{PT} which provides a habitat to a certain examples of the hadal crustaceans shrimps such as amphipods \cite{Hessleretal1978}. \ac{PT} supports geomorphic evidence of the tectonic deformation resulting from the westward \ac{PSP} subduction  along the trench, as reconstructed by \cite{Ramosetal2012} who detected a raised marine platforms and terraces along the 80-km-long coastline of the Philippine coasts. 

Heterogeneous nature of its submarine geomorphology resulting in depths and steepness variations at the local level (ca 1200 km) is caused by crustal deformation along the Philippine Fault, Luzon originated from Eocene\index[concepts]{Geologic time period!Eocene} \cite{Yuetal2013}, \cite{Suzukietal2017}. Specifically, the fault reveals left-lateral slip of the Philippine archipelago from the northwest corner of Luzon to the southeast end of Mindanao.  Therefore, it is often challenging to distinguish the multiple spatial variables of the tectonic and geological factors with those of depth distribution and gradients in deep-sea trenches.

Simulated tectonic evolution of the southern Japan \cite{Hashimaetal2016} shown that it was caused by the subduction of the Pacific and \ac{PSP} and the collision of the \ac{IBM} arc: geomorphological and geological data on plate movements suggest that subsidence affected the entire \ac{PSB} after which the area of subsidence narrowed until the basin's uplift. \cite{FanZhao2019} identified the geodynamic processes in the crust and mantle of the Philippines archipelago which induce geographic differences in azimuthal tomographic anisotropy between the central and southern Philippines. Recorded earthquake events at the 11 cross-sections of the earthquake transects visualized at three groups of depths: 0-60, 60-350 and $>$ 350 km, demonstrating active volcanism\index[concepts]{volcanism} and seismicity\index[concepts]{seismicity} around the Philippine archipelago and trench.

Further reading on tectonics and geologic settings of the \ac{PT} can be found in relevant papers, e.g. with more detailed discussions on spatial variation in seismic earthquake data \cite{Besanaetal1997}; shape form of the subducting slabs \cite{Bautista}; tectonics of the Parece Vela Basin \cite{Okinoetal2009}; subduction system of the \ac{PSP} \cite{Holtetal2018}; questions of fault topography patterns and quaternary\index[concepts]{Geologic time period!Quaternary} faulting along the Philippine Fault zone, Central Luzon \cite{Hiranoetal1986}; highlighted accreted terranes in the northern part of the Philippine archipelago \cite{Karig1983}. Recent surveys revealed the presence of peridotites, gabbros, and massive and pillowed flows of the Mesozoic\index[concepts]{Geologic time period!Mesozoic} ophiolite belt. Major, trace and rare earth elements of the volcanic rocks show subduction-related influences on formation of the island arc \cite{Guotanaetal2017}. Mixed sedimentary consolidated rocks were formed by the submarine erosion from the abyssal plain area located near the \ac{PT} and transported to the trench, as well as submarine canyons followed by water currents \cite{Lemenkova201990}. 

Some more studies of the \ac{PSP} have investigated its geophysical settings with a special focus on magnetism and reconstruction models highlighting its complex tectonic history, origin and motion development, seismicity and earthquakes \cite{Halletal1995a}; \cite{Halletal1995b}; \cite{Yu1982}, \cite{Lemenkova202002}. Other efforts have been focussed on interpreting the geophysical and tectonic settings of the \ac{PSP}. Thus, \cite{YuChang1991} discussed lateral fluctuations in the upper mantle structure of the \ac{PSB}, studied more detailed by \cite{Yuetal2000} with a close focus on the geophysics across the \ac{PSB}. 

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\pagebreak
\section{Manila Trench}
\ac{MnT}\index[places]{Manila Trench} is located in South China Sea\index[places]{South China Sea}, west Pacific Ocean, west of the islands of Luzon\index[places]{Luzon Island} and Mindoro\index[places]{Mindoro Island} in the Philippines (Map \ref{map:M1}). 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=12cm]{Fig-M1.jpg}
	\caption{Manila Trench: bathymetric map, SRTM 15 sec grid}\label{map:M1}
\end{map}

The area of the \ac{MnT} is located in the Manila subduction zone at the Philippine Sea plate boundary where it moves in the northwest direction toward the Eurasian plate with a high convergence rate \cite{Lo2017}. The \ac{MnT} with its maximal depth of the trench about 5,400 m \cite{Liuetal2007} and stretching in almost vertical North-South direction is created by the subduction of the Eurasian Plate (through its part Sunda Plate\index[places]{Sunda Plate}) under the Philippine Sea Plate\index[places]{Philippine Sea Plate}. Specific geological structure of the \ac{MnT} causes potential repetitive earthquakes and seismicity\index[concepts]{seismicity} \cite{Wu2009}. 

Another frequent hazard caused by the subducting slab of the Eurasian plate beneath the \ac{MnT} (Map \ref{map:M3}, left) is destructive tsunamis causing catastrophic damages in the source area and along the coastlines of the Luzon Island\index[places]{Luzon Island}. The hypocenters\index[concepts]{earthquake!hypocenter} of the tsunami of the \ac{MnT} are located at the depths <100 km \cite{Ruangrassamee}. The tsunami hazard from the \ac{MnT} source has been assessed in more details in several research papers \cite{Nguyen}, \cite{Bautista}, \cite{Soloviev}. The zone of the Eurasian Plate\index[places]{Eurasian Plate} subduction explains the belt of volcanoes in the \ac{MnT} area (Map \ref{map:M3}, left), on the west side of the Philippine island of Luzon\index[places]{Luzon Island}. The area between the northernmost Manila subduction zone and southern Taiwan\index[places]{Taiwan Island} is considered a transition from subduction to initial collision and a weakly coupled nature for the northern part of the \ac{MnT} \cite{Chin}. 

The geological complexity of the \ac{MnT} is also expressed by the connection with the \ac{PT} where a horizontal mantle flow exists between the \ac{MnT} and the \ac{PT}. It is caused by the collision between the Palawan block and the Philippine Mobile Belt, and movement off the South China Sea\index[places]{South China Sea} slab \cite{FanZhao2019}. Various factors cause the formation of the \ac{MnT}. Being a hadal trench, it presents a special area of the ocean seafloor\index[concepts]{seafloor} with distinct geomorphological structures characterized by the notable depths and steep gradient angles, located in the zones of the continental margin tectonic plates bending. 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\subfloat[Geologic map]{\includegraphics[width=6.8cm]{Fig-M2.jpg}}
			\hspace{3mm}
		\subfloat[Geoid model]{\includegraphics[width=7.5cm]{Fig-M3.jpg}}
			\hspace{3mm}
	\caption{Manila Trench \cite{Lemenkova201995}}\label{map:M3}
\end{map}

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=10cm]{Fig-M4.jpg}
	\caption{Manila Trench: marine free-air gravity map}\label{map:M4}
\end{map}

The convergence between the two plates forming the \ac{MnT} is roughly northwestward. There is a high-velocity zone present in the crust and upper mantle beneath the Luzon Arc\index[places]{Luzon Arc} where the \ac{MnT} is stretching \cite{Doo2016}. All these factors briefly mentioned to illustrate the tectonic settings of the study area indicate high seismicity\index[concepts]{seismicity} and special tectonic zone where the \ac{MnT} has been formed. The seafloor\index[concepts]{seafloor} of the hadal trench presents a complex structure combined of of various landforms: mid-ocean ridges, transform faults, ocean plains complicated by chains of seamounts\index[concepts]{seamounts}, minor ridges, trenches and plateaus. 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=10cm]{Fig-M5.jpg}
	\caption{Manila Trench: 3D mesh map of the geomorphology}\label{map:M5}
\end{map}

Factors influencing geomorphic development, structure and bathymetric patterns of the trench are diverse. To mention some of them: geological, hydro-chemical, geothermal, climatic, tectonic and bathymetric determinants. The steepness of the Manila geomorphology varies: the dip angles along the \ac{MnT}\index[places]{Manila Trench} increasing gradually southwards from ca. 25\degree\space at the latitudes between 18\degree\space and 21\degree\space N, 32\degree\space at the latitude 17\degree\space N, and nearly vertical at the latitude 14\degree\space N \cite{Chengetal2019}. The tectonic front of the \ac{MnT} continuing northward into the frontal thrust faults in the western Taiwan shows active plate boundary between the Eurasian and Philippine Sea plates in the Luzon-Taiwan region \cite{Yu2004}. 

The structure of the northern \ac{MnT} has been studied in various papers focused on the problems of crustal structure and deformation in the north of the \ac{MnT} \cite{Lietal2013}, \cite{He2016}, \cite{Ku2009}. Among other findings, the increasing dip angle of the \ac{MnT} from north to south has been discovered. The \ac{MnT} loses its sharp bathymetric pattern in the early collision zone and gradually becomes a less well-defined deformation front \cite{Lester2013}. However, the comparative analysis of its southern and northern parts is still missing. Therefore, current paper contributes to better understanding of the \ac{MnT} by comparative analysis of its geomorphology in the northern and southern segments.

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\pagebreak
\section{Ryukyu Trench}
The \ac{RT}\index[places]{Ryukyu Trench} is a 1400 km long hadal oceanic trench, stretching along the southeastern edge of the Ryukyu island arc\index[places]{Ryukyu island arc}, between the Philippine Sea, Taiwan Island\index[places]{Taiwan Island} and Kyushu Island\index[places]{Kyushu Island}, southern Japan\index[places]{Japan}, Pacific Ocean \cite{Ando} (Map \ref{map:R1}). The trench was formed as a result of the subduction of the oceanic crust of the Philippine Sea Plate beneath the continental crust of the Eurasian Plate\index[places]{Eurasian Plate} \cite{Doo}. The aim of the research is to analyze differences in the geomorphology and bathymetry of the northern and southern parts of the trench using \ac{GMT} \cite{Wesseletal2013}.

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=13cm]{Fig-R1.jpg}
	\caption{Ryukyu Trench and Arc, East China Sea and Philippine Sea: bathymetric map}\label{map:R1}
\end{map}

The bathymetric pattern of the \ac{RT} varies in its northern and southern parts which reflects the correlation between the geomorphic shape of the hadal trench, steepness of its gradient slope angles and tectonic settings in the area. Thus, in the northern part of the trench, the subduction of the Philippine Sea Plate is notable for shallow depths and steepness, reaching only ca. 11\degree. On the contrary, the angle becomes steeper, reaching 70\degree\space below ca. 70 km. The region located to north-west of the Ryukyu volcanic island arc\index[places]{Ryukyu island arc} is a \ac{BAB}, a depression of the Okinawa Trough\index[places]{Okinawa Trough} (Map \ref{map:R2}).

The subduction of the Philippine Sea tectonic plate causes eruption of the approximate 34 volcanoes (Map \ref{map:R2}) and earthquakes producing tsunami. In contrast, the slab dip in the central and southern parts of the \ac{RT} is more gentle, reaching 40-50\degree\space at 70 km depth \cite{Kodaira} \cite{Long}. There are three groups of islands on the forearc basement stretching over the Ryukyu arc \cite{Kizaki}. The Kerama Fault\index[places]{Kerama Fault}, about 250 km wide (Map \ref{map:R2}), separates the southern from the central group of islands.

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=13cm]{Fig-R2.jpg}
	\caption{Ryukyu Trench: geologic map, ETOPO1}\label{map:R2}
\end{map}

The \ac{RT} reaches depths of 7000 m \ac{BSL} and the forearc slope between the forearc island chain and the trench is between 130 to 160 km wide. The northern group has Yaku-shima\index[places]{Yaku-shima} and Tanega-shima\index[places]{Tanega-shima} and a 200 km long Tokara Channel\index[places]{Tokara Channel} gap between the island groups \cite{Okamura}. The tectonics of the hadal trench reflects evolutional processes of the building seamounts\index[concepts]{seamounts} and the structure of the Earth’s crust\index[concepts]{Earth!crust}. Therefore, it causes variations in the lithological patters, geological conditions of the rocks formation, stratigraphy and sedimentary settings of the ocean seafloor formation during Jurassic\index[concepts]{Geologic time period!Jurassic}, Mesozoic\index[concepts]{Geologic time period!Mesozoic} and Cretaceous\index[concepts]{Geologic time period!Cretaceous}. Double subduction system of the \ac{RT} reflects the complex trench motions as an expression of the interaction between the subducting plates and the underlying mantle of the \ac{RT}. The Pacific plates subducts beneath the Philippine Sea Plate, which is in turn subducting beneath the Eurasian Plate along the \ac{RT} \cite{Faccennaetal2018}.

Geomorphology of the ocean seafloor results from the complex geodynamic processes. These demonstrate traces of the continuous evolution and shape seabed surface as a system of the mid-ocean ridges, transform faults, vast ocean plains complicated by chains groups, ridges and plateaus as well as minor forms such as terraces, seamounts and hills, 3D\index[concepts]{plot!3D} Map \ref{map:R5}. Various factors affect the trench geomorphology and in turn, are reflected in its complex structure and functionality of the submarine environment. To mention some of them: effects of the magmatism \cite{Kiminami}, seismicity\index[concepts]{seismicity} \cite{Theunissen}, \cite{Gutscher}, volcanism\index[concepts]{volcanism} and hydrothermal system \cite{Minami2018}, \cite{Minami2016}.

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=10cm]{Fig-R3.jpg}
	\caption{Ryukyu Trench: geoid map}\label{map:R3}
\end{map}

Another example is the effect of the thickness of the subduction channels and earthquake rupture segments on the geomorphology of the oceanic landforms. Thus, the spatial differences of the \ac{RT} geomorphology is reflected in its sediment composition that change systematically from lighter off northern Ryukyu arc to heavier along the central part of the Ryukyu arc. This is explained by the dominance of the terrigenous and pelagic sedimentation off of the northern and central Ryukyu arc, respectively \cite{Shu}.

\ac{RT} presents a structural trap in the continental margins of the Pacific Ocean since the sediments are carried by the ocean currents passing through the system of trenches on the west of the Pacific. The sediment types of the \ac{RT} include among others mud diapirs, hemipelagic sediments, mud-supported breccia, fossil planktonic foraminifera, calcareous nannofossils \cite{Ujiie}. The content of the planktonic foraminifera varies according to the seasonal warming of surface waters and vertical mix as a result of the NW monsoon \cite{Xu}. 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=10cm]{Fig-R4.jpg}
	\caption{Ryukyu Trench: gravimetry map}\label{map:R4}
\end{map}

Seismic investigations of the \ac{RT} area show that the sedimentary thickness is ca. 2-3 km on the oceanic plate, oceanic crust thickness is 5-6 km. The upper mantle material underlying the oceanic plate has low seismic velocities, explained by the geochemical processes of the mantle peridotites \cite{Klingelhoefer}. Submarine margins of the continents can be divided into two types: the passive and active ones, depending on the features of the geomorphology and geology. The active margin in the Ryukyu area is located off northern Taiwan in the southern Ryukyu forearc (Map \ref{map:R2}). A mega-splay fault system associated with great earthquakes in the south of the Ryukyu region is described by \cite{Hsu}. Indeed, the southwestern Ryukyu system is characterized by the edge regime that is heavily influenced by the juxtaposing continental lithosphere\index[concepts]{Earth!lithosphere} \cite{Kuo}.

Although the seafloor of the \ac{RT} is separated vertically from the euphotic zone, is serves as a depositional center for the organic carbon accumulation. This is caused by the geomorphological factors: steep slopes enable lateral transport from the adjacent continental margins \cite{Itohetal2011}. Studies on the spatial biodiversity of the \ac{RT} show \cite{Kitahashietal2014b} low values at shallow depths, but increased with water depth reaching maximum between the trench floors and abyssal plains. This proves that bathymetric patterns of the deep-sea biomass of the \ac{RT} spatially differ in various regions of the trench. 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=10cm]{Fig-R5.jpg}
	\caption{Ryukyu Trench: 3D mesh geomorphological modelling}\label{map:R5}
\end{map}

Such discrepancies reflect differences in the environmental conditions, e.g. primary productivity. level. This is also supported by the discoveries of the migration patterns in the \ac{RT} area where reefs and geomorphic landforms serves as a land bridge for the migration of terrestrial organisms from Okinawa-jima\index[places]{Okinawa-jima (Island)} to Miyako-jima\index[places]{Miyako-jima (Island)} \cite{Arai}.

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\pagebreak
\section{Palau Trench}
The \ac{PaT}\index[places]{Palau Trench} is located in the Philippine Sea\index[places]{Philippine Sea}, northwards from the Papua-New Guinea\index[places]{Papua New Guinea} and eastwards from the Philippines, west Pacific Ocean (Map. \ref{map:YPT1}).The geological settings of the \ac{PaT} area is shown on Map \ref{map:YPT2}. \ac{PaT} with the greatest depth at 8,080 m (situated in its southern part) has a straight shape of the geometric form, stretching from south-west to north-east (Map {map:YPT1} and Map {map:YPT6}), located within a square 133\degree-136\degree\space E and 6\degree-8\degree\space N, east from the Yap Islands\index[places]{Yap Islands}. 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=13cm]{Fig-YPT2.jpg}
	\caption{Yap and Palau trenches: geological map \cite{Lemenkova2019106}}\label{map:YPT2}
\end{map}

One of the most unique characteristics of the \ac{PaT} is its short distance (35 km) between the trench axis and the coast of the Palau Islands\index[places]{Palau Islands} westwards \cite{Kobayashietal1997}. The geology of the \ac{PaT} includes sequences of serpentinized peridotite, basalt\index[concepts]{rock!igneous!basalt} and limestone on its landward slope and a huge block of coral-reef limestones at depths 4,900 m to 6,500 m \cite{Kobayashietal1997}. \ac{PaT} is connected with the Kyushu-Palau Ridge\index[places]{Kyushu-Palau Ridge} in the north and Ayu Trough\index[places]{Ayu Trough} in the south. Yap and \ac{PaT} are distinguished from the flat-bottomed Ayu Trough by their more similar to V shape in the cross section. 

The Islands of Palau located westwards to the \ac{PaT} are composed of volcanic rocks capped by coral reef limestone. This indicates that the Palau islands were located along the volcanic front earlier \cite{Kobayashietal1997}. The tectonic situation of Yap and \ac{PaT} is on the \ac{PSP}\index[places]{Philippine Sea Plate}. The \ac{PSP} is the tectonic plate completely surrounded by convergent boundaries of the tectonic plates \cite{Yehetal2013}. The tectonic settings of Yap and \ac{PaT} presents a complex region of convergence among three tectonic plates: the \ac{PSP}, the Pacific and the Caroline plates\index[places]{Caroline Plate}. Hence, Yap and \ac{PaT} belong to the subduction zones surrounding \ac{PSP} along its eastern boundary.

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=13cm]{Fig-YPT3.jpg}
	\caption{Yap and Palau trenches: geophysical gravity map \cite{Lemenkova2019106}}\label{map:YPT3}
\end{map}

Increasing developments of the ocean resource exploration caused concerns on ocean areas and \ac{PaT} geomorphology, formed as a result of complex set of processes.
