The data analysis revealed deep interconnections between its submarine geomorphology and impact factors: geology and bathymetry. The structure of the submarine geomorphology of the \ac{PaT} is a complex interplay of these processes. Various factors affect its geomorphic structure, geometry of shape, formation and development. The most important include the following: sediments, tectonics and stratigraphy, bathymetry, geographic location, slope aspect degree; geologic structure of the underlying basement, ocean streams determining local geomorphological conditions that depend on the sediment deposition processes. 

Main morphostructure elements of the seafloor\index[concepts]{seafloor} of the \ac{PaT} are the outskirts of the continents, tectonic plates and ocean bedrock. The structure of the trench and the nature of its geomorphic shape are therefore greatly complicated by the multiple secondary tectonic disturbances: system of faults, displacements of grabens\index[concepts]{fault!graben}, horsts\index[concepts]{fault!horst} and lateral geologic shifts. The interconnection of these complex processes is also illustrated by recent findings \cite{HeuretLallemand2005} that demonstrates correlation between the back-arc deformation and upper continental tectonic plate velocity. 

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\pagebreak
\section{Yap Trench}
\ac{YT}\index[places]{Yap Trench} is located in the Philippine Sea, northwards from the Papua New Guinea\index[places]{Papua New Guinea}, eastwards from the Philippines, west Pacific Ocean (Map. \ref{map:YPT1}). \ac{YT} with the greatest depth at 8,292 m is ca. 700 km long and 50 km wide \cite{Satoetal1997}. It stretches from the trench axis to Yap islands arc with a characteristic 'J' form \cite{Lee2004}. It is morphologically divided into the northern and southern segments with the the boundary between the two segments at $8^\circ26^\prime$N \cite{Fujiwaraetal2000}. 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=13cm]{Fig-YPT1.jpg}
	\caption{Yap and Palau trenches: topographic map \cite{Lemenkova2019106}}\label{map:YPT1}
\end{map}

The Yap-Palau subduction zone\index[places]{Yap-Palau subduction zone} lies southwards off the Mariana arc-trench system. The Caroline Plate\index[places]{Caroline Plate} subducts here northwestwards beneath the Philippine Sea Plate. The most prominent geological structures in the Caroline Plate\index[places]{Caroline Plate} are Caroline Ridge\index[places]{Caroline Ridge} (Map \ref{map:YPT2}), a buoyant oceanic plateau, and Sorol Trough\index[places]{Sorol Trough}, spreading in the middle of the ridge \cite{Dongetal2018}.

\ac{YT} and \ac{PaT} are formed in the place of the \ac{PSP}\index[places]{Philippine Sea Plate} subduction and oceanic lithosphere\index[concepts]{Earth!lithosphere} sinking to the mantle. As sinking \ac{PSP} moves deeper into the mantle, the fluids are released from the mantle rock. This cause melting of the overlying mantle. New magma (molten rock) rises to the surface and erupts violently forming volcanoes and arcs of islands along the convergent tectonic plate boundary. Therefore, earthquakes are common in the subduction zone of the \ac{PSP}\index[places]{Philippine Sea Plate} (Map \ref{map:YPT1}, red triangles). 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=13cm]{Fig-YPT4.jpg}
	\caption{Yap and Palau trenches: geoid model \cite{Lemenkova2019106}}\label{map:YPT4}
\end{map}

\ac{YT} and \ac{PaT} form a typical trench-arc-basin system which includes deep trench, island arcs, and back-arc basin, a Parece Vela Basin\index[places]{Parece Vela Basin}, located in the eastern side of the Philippine Sea Plate (Map. \ref{map:YPT2}). Such complex tectonic structure of the region is reflected in notable heterogeneity of the crustal and upper mantle velocity structures beneath the Philippine Sea Plate \cite{OdaSenna1994}. Necessarily, it affects the submarine seafloor\index[concepts]{seafloor} topography and its geomorphic shape. Environment of the \ac{YT} and \ac{PaT} (biochemical and ecological setting) are described in relevant papers \cite{Yangetal2018}; \cite{Xuetal2018}; \cite{Liuetal2018a}. 

Syntheses of the tectonic and magmatic evolution of the region of the Philippine Sea, \ac{YT} and \ac{PaT} can be found in further works \cite{Beccaluvaetal1986}, \cite{Pilletetal1999}, \cite{Setonetal2012}, \cite{Oharaetal2002}, \cite{Tsuboietal2014}. Water mass properties and deep currents in the northern \ac{YT} were observed and discussed recently \cite{Liuetal2018a}.

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\pagebreak
\section{Mariana Trench}

\paragraph{Geographic Location}
\ac{MT}\index[places]{Mariana Trench} is the deepest part of the World Ocean\index[places]{World Ocean} with a maximal depth of 10,984$\pm$ 25 m (95\%) at 11.329903\degree\space N / 142.199305\degree\space E at the Challenger Deep\index[places]{Challenger Deep} \cite{Gardneretal2014}. As the deepest place on the Earth, it is constantly deserving attention of the scientific world \cite{Chadwicketal2018}; \cite{CurtisMoyer2005}; \cite{Faccennaetal2018}; \cite{Fujiokaetal2002}; \cite{Theberge2008}; \cite{Tianetal2018}. The arc of the \ac{MT} can be fitted into a square with coordinates 140\degree E-148\degree E, 11\degree N-25\degree N (Fig. \ref{map:BathyMT}). Geographic location of the edge of the \ac{MT} along the coasts of the continents or island arcs is explained by the subduction of tectonic plates at the boundaries of their collision: Mariana\index[places]{Mariana Plate}, Pacific\index[places]{Pacific Plate}, Philippine\index[places]{Philippine Sea Plate}, Caroline\index[places]{Caroline Plate} tectonic plates \cite{Ishibashietal2015}. 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=14cm]{Bathy-MT.jpg}
	\caption{Mariana Trench: bathymetric map}\label{map:BathyMT}
\end{map}

\ac{MT} is one of the 37 known deep-water trenches of the World Ocean, 28 of which located in the margin areas of the tectonic plates of the Pacific Ocean \cite{PushharovskijNeprochnov1984}, and the rest is as follows: 5 are located in the \index[places]{Atlantic Ocean} Atlantic Ocean \cite{Bogdanov1997} and 5 in Indian Ocean\index[places]{Indian Ocean} \cite{Gurvich1998}. With a total length of about 2,550 km, \ac{MT}  creates a complex of the deeply interrelated factors and processes of tectonic plates. \ac{MT} is a strongly elongated, arched in plan and lesser rectilinear, narrow topographic depression of the seafloor, crescent-like in its geometric plain shape. It is located in the west Pacific Ocean\index[places]{Pacific Ocean}, 200 km to the east of the Mariana Islands\index[places]{Mariana Islands}, eastwards of the Philippine Islands\index[places]{Philippine Archipelago} and China\index[places]{China}, Fig. \ref{map:BathyMT}. Its transverse profile\index[concepts]{trench!profile!transverse} is asymmetric\index[concepts]{trench!profile!asymmetric}: the slope\index[concepts]{slope} is higher on the side of the Mariana island arc\index[places]{Mariana Islands}, dissected by the deep underwater canyons. 

Seafloor bathymetry varies seaward of the trench axis: the Challenger Deep area, southwest of Guam, is associated with the deepest relief sounding of $10,984 \pm 25$ m (95\%) at 11.329903\degree N / 142.199305 \degree E, and axial vertical loading \cite{Gardneretal2014}. The Challenger Deep is up to 2 km deeper than the average depth along the axis of the \ac{MT} \cite{Fryer2003}. \ac{MT} has complex geomorphic structure and unevenness in profiles stretching SW where its geomorphological structure varies. The geomorphology is subjected to various phenomena that affect its shape. These include bathymetry (depths), tectonics plates, geology (sedimentation) and geometry (slope steepness). Stretching for hundreds kilometers, \ac{MT} is formed as a result of the subduction of the Pacific Plate beneath the \ac{PSP}. The subduction boundaries have a classified 'Mariana type' where the coupling between the two plates is either weak or non existent \cite{UyedaKanamori1979}. Westward slope of the \ac{MT} presents a barren trench wall with no uplift, while eastern part is the border along which western edge of the Pacific Plate is subducting beneath the smaller Mariana Plate. As a result, the geomorphic feature of the \ac{MT} is its very steep slopes and depths of 3-5 km width of the bottoms rarely exceeding 5 km in breadth \cite{NakanishiHashimoto2011}. 

\ac{MT} has complicated steps of various shapes and sizes, caused by active tectonic and processes of sedimentation\index[concepts]{sedimentation}. The steepness of its geomorphic depth averages in 4-5\degree, but its individual parts can be limited to steeper slopes as subjects to the gravitational flow system\index[concepts]{gravitation!flow system} of the submarine canyons\index[concepts]{submarine!canyons} and valleys\index[concepts]{submarine!valleys}\cite{Kawabe1993} \cite{NormarkCarlson2003}; \cite{HarrisWhiteway2011}. Complex distribution of the various geomorphic material on the adjacent abyssal plains\index[concepts]{abyssal!plain} of the ocean contributes to the formation of the geomorphic features of the particular region of the \ac{MT}. The main area of the seafloor of the trench has prevailing depths of >3000 m, a seabed being the most ancient parts of the ocean floor formed in the late Jurassic\index[concepts]{Geologic time period!Jurassic}. To date, research on \ac{MT} and \ac{IBM} arc has focused on aspects of the tectonic processes of plate subduction \cite{Faccennaetal2018}, faulting and deformation \cite{Zhouetal2015}, \cite{ZhouLin2018}, geometry of the lithospheric gradient dips \cite{BodineWatts1979}, plate motion, geologic structure and depths of slabs \cite{HilstSeno1993}. \cite{Beccaluvaetal1986}, petrogenetic studies on geochemic traces of volcanic rocks from the \ac{MT} pointed at its tectono-magmatic evolution.

\paragraph{Bathymetry}
Recent bathymetric and topographic analyses \cite{Michibayashietal2007} indicate that thin lithosphere\index[concepts]{Earth!lithosphere} in the \ac{IBM} region (20 km) might have been intensely deformed during tectonic evolution of the region. It may explain notable variation in microstructure of samples taken from the ground on the \ac{MT}, which range from coarse granular and intensely elongated texture to fine porphyroclastic and fine-grained equigranular textures. Submarine relief of the \ac{MT} is marked by complicated steps of various shapes and sizes, caused by active tectonic and processes of sedimentation. NW Pacific Ocean is specially characteristic for the vast areas of the bottom of the basins occupied by depressions >6000m. The structure of the \ac{MT} has following general pattern: a major total area is being occupied by the abyssal depths (3-6 km), while the extreme depths >6 km, are smaller in comparison to the previous areas \cite{UyedaKanamori1979}. The abyssal make up relatively small part of the total area of the \ac{MT}, while the \nth{2} ones cover about 2/3 of the whole. 

Depths >6000 m are confined mainly to the deepest part of the trench located in its SW part, although individual depressions to depths of 6-7 km, rarely up to 7.5 km, occur in the central part of the basins along the trench \cite{Morgan1974}. Typical depths of the Mariana seafloor are 4000-5000 m where mid-ocean ridges\index[concepts]{mid-ocean ridge} and deep-sea basins\index[concepts]{deep-sea!basins} are formed by numerous seamounts\index[concepts]{seamounts} and deep-sea depressions\index[concepts]{deep-sea!depressions} are located. There are differences in the velocity of the longitudinal seismic waves\index[concepts]{wave!seismic} in the geological layers of the \ac{MT} structure: water and sedimentary layer have speed of 3.5-6.2 km/s, basalt\index[concepts]{rock!igneous!basalt} layer has speed of 6.5-7.0 km/s. The layer of gabbro\index[concepts]{rock!igneous!gabbro} and banded gabbro are located beneath. The boundary of the crust-mantle\index[concepts]{Earth!crust} is made by a sharp increase in the velocities from 7 to 8 km/s (the boundary of the Mohorovi\v{c}i\'{c} (Moho)\index[concepts]{Earth!Mohorovi\v{c}i\'{c} (Moho) boundary}). Upper mantle is the region with velocities of 8.0-8.2 km/s \cite{Garfunkeletal1986}. Analogues of the oceanic crust on the ground are ophiolites\index[concepts]{plate!ophiolites}. The average thickness of the crust of this type under the platforms is ca. 40 km \cite{Gurvich1998}. The regions with bark continental type in the ocean are fragments of continental mass formed as a result of the formation of the modern ocean floor\index[concepts]{seafloor} causing migration and slow movement of the trench \cite{Husson2012}. \ac{MT} has a a distinctive morphological feature of the convergent plate boundaries, along which lithospheric plates\index[concepts]{plate!lithospheric} move towards each other, located about 200 km parallel to a volcanic island arc\index[concepts]{volcanic!island arc} \cite{Karatoetal2001}. 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
 		\includegraphics[width=12cm]{Fig-PM15.jpg}
	\caption{3D relief model: Mariana Trench, south segment}
\label{map:PM15}       
\end{map}

The formation of the trench is caused by the complex interconnection of various factors \cite{Gardneretal2014}. A trench marks the position at which the subducting slab\index[concepts]{subduction!slab} descends beneath another lithospheric slab \cite{DeschampsLallemand2003}. General form of the bathymetry of the \ac{MT} is inclined toward the margin plate being connected with the ocean basic zone. It has high and steep island or continental slopes and more low and gentle slopes from the south-eastern side. The trench has a distinctive morphological feature of the convergent Pacific plate boundaries, along which lithospheric plates move towards each other. Various narrow steps are often found on the slopes of the trench. On the longitudinal profiles of the bottom of the \ac{MT} there are many deepwater detected transverse steps and thresholds\index[concepts]{thresholds} connected by movements of the Earth's crust along transverse faults\index[concepts]{fault} \cite{Zhouetal2015}. These thresholds can play important role in the evolution of the deep water trough\index[concepts]{trough}. For example, they control the intensity of its filling by sediment precipitation\index[concepts]{sediments!precipitation}. In this process very important are the streams of the precipitation moving along the bottom from the part of the trough which is closer to the continent \cite{Tairaetal2004}. At the bottom of the trough there is a transverse threshold, which can present kind of a dam, in front of which there is accumulation of a thick layer of sediment precipitation.

%---------------------------------------------
\paragraph{Geologic development}
Geologic development of the \ac{MT} took place in several phases shaped by the influence of various factors: geologic conditions, volcanic submarine areas, hydrological settings of the currents and sedimentation. Seafloor bathymetry of the trench varies significantly in seaward axis. The Caroline Ridge is located off the trench axis at a distance of ca. 250 km. A prominent trench-parallel belt of seamounts with trench-perpendicular width of ca. 250 km, is located <1350km. Another prominent group of wide seamounts intersects the trench at distance of 1600-2000 km \cite{Zhangetal2014}. The effects of the volcanism\index[concepts]{volcanism} on the biological communities \cite{Kitahashietal2014} proved correlation between earthquakes and the assemblage structures. Because rapid sedimentation is induced by turbidity currents, the organic matter content increased in the topmost layers which indicate that disturbances to the deep-sea sediments affects vertical distribution structure of meiofauna in the trench.

The seabed structure of the \ac{MT} is composed in the following way from top to bottom: a sedimentary cover, a basalt\index[concepts]{rock!igneous!basalt} of the leitic composition, a complex of parallel dykes of diabases\index[concepts]{rock!igneous!diabase}, an isotropic gabbro\index[concepts]{rock!igneous!gabbro}, a banded gabbro-ultrabasic complex\index[concepts]{rock!igneous!ultrabasic complex} lying on the mantle\index[concepts]{Earth!mantle} ultrabasites\index[concepts]{rock!igneous!ultrabasites} \cite{Butuzova2003}. The trench advances toward the upper plate corresponding to the subduction of very old, Mesozoic\index[concepts]{Geologic time period!Mesozoic}, oceanic lithosphere\index[concepts]{Earth!lithosphere}. Mariana Arc lavas are enriched in Molybdenum (Mo) and have $\delta^{98/95}$ Mo >\ac{MORB}. This implicates the addition of a Mo-rich fluid with $\delta^{98/95}$ Mo \texttildelow{} +0.05\% to +0.05\% to the mantle wedge beneath most islands \cite{Freymuthetal2015}. Thin Quaternary\index[concepts]{Geologic time period!Quaternary} clayey sediments overlie a 400 m thick alternating sequence of the Early Cretaceous\index[concepts]{Geologic time period!Cretaceous!Early} quartz-trachyte\index[concepts]{rock!igneous!trachyte}\index[concepts]{rock!igneous!quartz-trachyte}\index[concepts]{rock!igneous!quartz} pillow lava\index[concepts]{Earth!mantle!pillow lava} and Early to Late Cretaceous\index[concepts]{Geologic time period!Cretaceous!Late} radiolarian cherts in the oceanward slope of the \ac{MT} \cite{Hiranoetal2002}. \ac{MT} belongs to the one of the deepest 45\% of the ocean’s depth range, and its unique topography disrupts the continental shelf-slope-rise to abyssal plain continuum, resulting in an array of deep isolated habitats. The often abrupt and distinct topography is likely to further promote speciation through high hydrostatic pressure, remoteness from surface derived food sources and geographic isolation \cite{Eustaceetal2016}. 

%---------------------------------------------
\paragraph{Lithology}
Lithological structure and sediment thickness\index[concepts]{sediments!thickness} are among other factors affecting \ac{MT} formation. Relatively few other trenches, comparing to \ac{MT}, also formed on the boundaries of the tectonic plates\index[concepts]{tectonic!plate}, but usually at a greater distance from the continents in the rift zones\index[concepts]{rift}. Therefore, sedimentation processes within \ac{MT} are deeply impacted by the location near the rift zones that are mainly associated with the formation of the underwater mountain ranges\index[concepts]{mid-ocean ridge} and spreading\index[concepts]{seafloor!spreading}. Their expansion in the sides of the neighboring lithospheric plates is a result of the rise towards \ac{MT}. The outflow to the surface of the ocean floor of the substance of the upper layer of the Earth's mantle\index[concepts]{Earth!mantle}. As a result of the unevenness of this process, the transversed faults are formed that have excellent conditions for sedimentation accumulation\index[concepts]{sedimentation!accumulation}. According to available geophysical data \cite{DubininUshakov2001}, the oceanic crust\index[concepts]{Earth!crust!oceanic} of the tectonic plates surrounding \ac{MT} is composed by a number of layers. Therefore, the impact of the sediment subduction\index[concepts]{subduction!sediment} on the trench dynamics\index[concepts]{trench!dynamics} is relatively high \cite{HorlestonHelffrich2012}.

\ac{MT} is the largest structural trap located in the continental margins\index[concepts]{continental!margins} of the Pacific Ocean: the sediments are being carrying by the ocean waves\index[concepts]{wave!ocean} in a clockwise direction, passing through the trenches on the west of the Pacific (i.e. Kermadec Trench and the \ac{TT} through the Samoan Passage\index[places]{Samoan Passage}) flowing northwest across the equator to the \ac{MT}. Its geomorphic conditions create ideal conditions for the sedimentation intercepting almost all clastic material\index[concepts]{rock!sedimentary!clastic} originating from the adjacent land. Accumulation\index[concepts]{sedimentation!accumulation} of the terrigenous sedimentation\index[concepts]{sedimentation!terrigenous} on the \ac{MT} seafloor\index[concepts]{seafloor} depends on various factors: climatic and hydrodynamic local settings, tectonics, seafloor\index[concepts]{seafloor} bathymetry, geomorphiology, type and size of particles. Large areas of active sedimentation can be distinguished where speed and volumes are very high, as well as local areas of active sedimentation\index[concepts]{sedimentation!active}, or on the contrary, dominating erosion areas. Sedimentation occur in various sediment traps\index[concepts]{sediments!trap}, with some areas noted by areal sedimentation\index[concepts]{sedimentation!areal}. The processes of the sedimentation within the \ac{MT} are highly uneven.

%---------------------------------------------
\paragraph{Geomorphic structure}
\ac{MT} has a complicated geomorphic structure\index[concepts]{geomorphology} with various types of the continental tectonic plate\index[concepts]{tectonic!plate} boundaries producing a unique type of its seafloor\index[concepts]{seafloor!bathymetry} bathymetry\index[concepts]{bathymetry}. The seafloor\index[concepts]{seafloor} of the trench is a background, on which all the processes are reflected \cite{Dic1974}. Main morphostructure elements of the seafloor\index[concepts]{seafloor} are the outskirts of the continents and of the ocean bedrock\index[concepts]{ocean bedrock}. The structure of the trench and the nature of its relief are greatly complicated by the multiple secondary tectonic disturbances, i.e. by the occurrence of faults and displacements on of graben\index[concepts]{fault!graben}, horsts\index[concepts]{fault!horst} and lateral geologic shifts\index[concepts]{lateral!geologic shifts} \cite{Lisicynetal1990}. 

Among other trenches, \ac{MT} is distinct for its edge type associated with the marginal tectonic plate subduction processes \cite{Boutelieretal2014}. It is formed in the process of plates subduction. Its submarine terrain has a complex structure, the scale of which sharply exceeds the dimensions forms of the Earth's surface on the margin continents. These include the system of the mid-ocean ridges\index[concepts]{ridge}, crossing transform faults, deep-sea ridges\index[concepts]{deep-sea!ridges}, vast ocean plains complicated by chains and mountain groups and plateaus\index[concepts]{plateau} \cite{Jarrard1986}. The width of the continental shelf\index[concepts]{continental!shelf} of the margin plates connecting to the \ac{MT} occupies varies from a few tens to thousands km with average bottom slope 17-25\degree\space \cite{PushharovskijMelanholina1992}.

Areas with seamounts at the trench axis are marked by gentle trench geomorphology\index[concepts]{geomorphology}, smaller axial vertical loading, and bulgy topography at the outer-rise. Mariana Arc and West Mariana Ridge border the trench on the west side. The Caroline Ridge and a group of Caroline Islands are located southwards off the trench axis at distance of about 0-250 km. A prominent trench-parallel belt of seamounts with trench-perpendicular width of about 250 km, is located at around 1350km \ac{BSL}. Another notable group of wide seamounts, intersects the trench at a distance of 1600-2000 km \ac{BSL} \cite{Zhangetal2014}. Morphological structure of the of the \ac{MT} has following pattern: a majority of the seafloor is occupied by the abyssal depths (3-6 km) followed by the extreme depths ($>6$ km). Depths of $>6$ km are restricted mostly to the deepest part of the trench located in the south-western part of the trench (area of Challenger Deep), although depressions of 6-7.5 km \ac{BSL}, occasionally occur in the central part of the trench crescent \cite{Morgan1974}. Typical depths of the \ac{MT} seafloor are 4-5 km \cite{Lemenkova201991}, \cite{Lemenkova201989}.

Seafloor of the \ac{MT} is composed by the oceanic crust forming rift zones of the mid-ocean ridges with a capacity of 5-10 km \cite{Morgan1974}. \ac{MT} is complicated and constituent of the interrelated factors forming its tectonic structure. Deformations of the trench respond to the coupling between the upper and lower plates: coupling relating to the continental slab age-buoyancy\index[concepts]{continental!slab age-buoyancy} \cite{RuffKanamori1980}. Back-arc deformation roughly correlate with upper continental tectonic plate velocity\index[concepts]{tectonic!plate!velocity} \cite{HeuretLallemand2005}. Trench migration rates are chiefly controlled by lower continental tectonic plate velocity \cite{Lallemandetal2008}, which depends on the tectonic slab age buoyancy \cite{Chase1978}. Main ridge axes of the \ac{MT} represent a place where the upwelling magma encounters cold seawater in the location of tectonic plates. Hydrothermal cooling of magma at the ridge axes causes recycling of the entire volume of the water in the trench column. In this way it provides nutrients for biological communities\index[concepts]{biological communities} in the abyssal zone of trench\index[concepts]{abyssal!zone} \cite{Langetal2014}, \cite{Luoetal2017}, \cite{Ichinoetal2015}. Examples of interconnection of the system are illustrated by biological communities \cite{Galloetal2015}, \cite{Laceyetal2016}. 

Geomorphic structure\index[concepts]{geomorphology} of the \ac{MT} is complicated by the longitudinal ridges\index[concepts]{ridge!longitudinal}, steps\index[concepts]{canyons!underwater!steps}, large landslide\index[concepts]{landslide}, bodies and ledges\index[concepts]{ledges}. Transverse profile of the \ac{MT} is strongly asymmetric: slopes are higher on the side of the island arc, dissected by deep underwater canyons with narrow steps\index[concepts]{canyons!underwater!steps}. \ac{MT} has complicated steps of various shapes and sizes, caused by active tectonic and sediment processes. Continental slopes are high, reaching up to several thousand m and inclining to 3-6\degree\space (in the SW part up to 30-40\degree), upper boundary of which coincides with the edges of the shelf (depths 150-200 m). Slopes of the passive margins\index[concepts]{passive margins} are strongly complicated by the terraces\index[concepts]{terrace}, ledges, marginal plateaus\index[concepts]{plateau!marginal} and canyons\index[concepts]{canyons!underwater}. Slopes\index[concepts]{slope} of the margins are steeper, reaching can 5-7 km in height. Seafloor geomorphology\index[concepts]{geomorphology} with very steep escarpment of the narrow \ac{MT} does not play a crucial role for ocean water mixing \cite{Harenetal2017}. Interplay between topography and turbulence of the ocean waves\index[concepts]{wave!ocean} includes impacts of the inertial and meridionally directed tidal inertio-gravity waves, responsible for turbulence and variation of water masses mix according to the depth stratification. At the base of the continental slope of passive margins formed by the adjacent tectonic plates, continental foot forms accumulative body. In turn, it is formed by merged cones of the removal and plumes of the suspension flows\index[concepts]{suspension flows} and submarine landslides\index[concepts]{landslide!submarine} with abyssal sedimentation\index[concepts]{sedimentation!abyssal}. 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
 		\includegraphics[width=12cm]{Fig-PM5.jpg}
	\caption{Earthquakes event map at \ac{PSB} area: prime hypocentres and magnitude values}
\label{map:PM5}       
\end{map}

Submarine margins of continents\index[concepts]{submarine!margins of continents}, or transition zones, depend on the features of the relief and geology. Accordingly, they are divided into 2 types: the passive and active ones. The \nth{1} ones include the shelf\index[concepts]{shelf}, that is mainland bank\index[concepts]{mainland bank}, the slope and the mainland foot. Having complicated relief structure with marginal seas\index[concepts]{marginal seas}, island arcs\index[concepts]{volcanic!island arc} and deep-sea gutters, \ac{MT} belongs to the active type of trench. Due to the geodynamic reasons, \ac{MT} motion, migration and upper plate deformation\index[concepts]{plate!deformation} can be described as the result of the response to the mantle flow and imbalance between the forces exerted by lower and upper continental plates at the plate interface.

\paragraph{Cracks}
\ac{MT} is notable for well-developed systems of cracks\index[concepts]{slope!instability!tension cracks} in the structure. Cracks\index[concepts]{slope!instability!tension cracks} of the \ac{MT} are abundantly developed at the edge, in the middle and upper parts of the terrace \cite{HeuretLallemand2005}. The formation of these cracks\index[concepts]{slope!instability!tension cracks} is caused by gravitational instability\index[concepts]{gravitation}\index[concepts]{gravitation!instability}: horizontal extension is due to the trench-ward increase of the dip angle of the seafloor surface. Besides, it cause slope instability due to gravity pull\index[concepts]{gravity!pull}. The cracks\index[concepts]{slope!instability!tension cracks} on the surface of the seafloor\index[concepts]{seafloor} on the oceanward slopes of the \ac{MT} are attributed to tensile rock failure. 
Cracks are elongated nearly parallel to the strike of the cliffs\index[concepts]{cliffs}, some are aligned, branching or merging have no vertical nor lateral displacement\index[concepts]{lateral!displacement}. This can be explained by open tension fractures\index[concepts]{tension fractures}. Surface edges of the \ac{MT} cracks are sharp, indicating young origin with a noticeable pressure ridge caused by mud overflow during closure of cracks. Crack\index[concepts]{slope!instability!tension cracks}s are formed at topographically specific areas, at the horizontally stretched surface of the down-going plates under tensional stress, on the horizontal or very gentle slopes above steep cliffs. Cracks are caused by a combination of gravitational slope instability\index[concepts]{slope!stability} at the edges of the slope or top of the ridge \cite{Ishizukaetal2010} and additional inertia during earthquake shaking\index[concepts]{earthquake} \cite{Demetsetal1990}, \cite{Schellart2007}.

\paragraph{Submarine earthquakes}
Deep earthquakes are important factors causing system of cracks of the \ac{MT}. Transformation faulting is the mechanism for deep earthquakes \cite{Kirbyetal1996}. Experimental observations \cite{GreenBurnley1989}, \cite{BurnleyGreen1991}, \cite{Kirbyetal1991} indicate a shear instability that develops during the incipient transformation of metastable olivine\index[concepts]{rock!igneous!olivine} to wadsleyite\index[concepts]{sediments!wadsleyite} or ringwoodite\index[concepts]{sediments!ringwoodite}. Horizontal tensional stress during the earthquake shaking triggers development of the system of cracks in the \ac{MT}, as stress difference under static conditions is caused by the slope inclination\index[concepts]{slope!inclination}. The majority of the cracks\index[concepts]{slope!instability!tension cracks} within the \ac{MT} are open tensile failures and are situated in areas of very gentle slope or horizontal parts of the seabed, in most cases near the edges of steep cliffs which delimit the terraces. There is no lateral nor vertical dislocations\index[concepts]{dislocations!lateral}\index[concepts]{dislocations!vertical} on both sides of the cracks, so they are open fractures\index[concepts]{fracture}.

\paragraph{Igneous volcanic areas}
\ac{MT} is important integral feature of the active continental margins that naturally includes deep plate subduction. Subduction of slabs of oceanic lithosphere\index[concepts]{Earth!lithosphere} into the deep mantle involves a wide range of the geophysical and geochemical processes and is of major importance for the physical and chemical evolution of the Earth \cite{Milleretal2004}. Thus, it is noticed \cite{IsacksMolnar1971} that the continuity of the slab across gaps in the Wadati-Benioff seismic zone\index[concepts]{Wadati-Benioff zone} between deep earthquake clusters and shallow seismicity\index[concepts]{seismicity}\index[concepts]{seismicity} and they argue that only the deep earthquakes beneath the north Fiji\index[places]{Fiji} basin and the deep events beneath New Zealand\index[places]{New Zealand} may occur as detached events with no mechanical connection to the surface. Using the observation and triggering mechanism of high-frequency oceanic T-waves\index[concepts]{wave!oceanic (T-waves)}, \cite{Barazangietal1972}, it has been argued \cite{GubbinsSnieder1991}, \cite{HilstSeno1993} for the mechanical continuity of most slabs across the deep seismicity\index[concepts]{seismicity} gap.

Recent studies revealed \cite{Faccennaetal2009} that \ac{MT}\index[places]{Mariana Trench} advance toward the upper plate corresponding to the subduction of very old, Mesozoic\index[concepts]{Geologic time period!Mesozoic} oceanic lithosphere\index[concepts]{Earth!lithosphere}. The Mariana Arc lavas are relatively enriched in Molybdenum (Mo) and have $\delta^{98/95}$ Mo significantly greater than \ac{MORB}\index[concepts]{mid-ocean ridge!basalts} (apart from the samples from the island of Agrigan\index[places]{Agrigan, Island}). This implicates the addition of a Mo-rich fluid with $\delta^{98/95}$ Mo ca. -0.05\% to +0.05\% to the mantle wedge beneath most islands \cite{Freymuthetal2015}. Recently updated analyses of global plate motions indicate that significant trench advance is also rare on Earth, being largely restricted to the Marianas-Izu-Bonin arc\index[places]{Izu-Bonin-Mariana Arc} \cite{CizkovaBina2015}. The effects of trench migration on the descent of subducted slabs\index[concepts]{slab} are discussed by \cite{Griffithsetal1995}. The global plate tectonics\index[concepts]{tectonic!plate!movements} movements shaping the seafloor bathymetry can be described as follows. 

The seafloor spreading fabric\index[concepts]{seafloor!spreading fabric} in the \ac{MT} is obscured by the absence of magnetic anomaly lineations\index[concepts]{magnetic anomaly lineations}. Elongated topographic structures accompanied by plate bending exist in the oceanward slope of the Challenger Deep\index[places]{Challenger Deep} \cite{Zhangetal2014}. The Japanese Lineation set is a Mesozoic\index[concepts]{Geologic time period!Mesozoic} magnetic anomaly lineation, it exists in the East Mariana Basin\index[places]{East Mariana Basin}, east of the \ac{MT} \cite{Nakanishietal1992}, which means that the subducting plate along the southern part of the \ac{MT} is a part of the Pacific plate formed in the Mesozoic\index[concepts]{Geologic time period!Mesozoic}. 

Other remarkable topographic feature of the \ac{MT}\index[places]{Mariana Trench} includes a set of many elongated ridges and escarpments accompanied by the plate bending on the outer slopes with two existing distinct strikes for these structures. The elongated structures have two strikes: N85\degree E and the same as the trench axis; N70\degree\space E. Most bending-related structures except those near the trench axis, were formed by the reactivation of the seafloor spreading fabric. The presence of cracks\index[concepts]{slope!instability!tension cracks} is another distinguished geological feature of the \ac{MT} \cite{Ogawaetal1997}. \ac{MT} has many open prominent cracks on its surface, up to a few meters deep and a few hundred meters long on the diatomaceous clayey sediment surfaces of its oceanward slopes. The shape of the cross-section is Y-shaped. More precisely, the bottom is a very narrow (1-3 km), yet in some places wide (several tens of kilometers) flattened.

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\pagebreak
\section{Izu-Bonin Trench}
The \ac{IBT}\index[places]{Izu-Bonin Trench}, also known as Izu-Ogasawara Trench\index[places]{Izu-Ogasawara Trench} is a hadal trench located in west Pacific Ocean and lies south of Japan (Map \ref{map:IBT4}). 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=14cm]{Fig-IBT1.jpg}
	\caption{Izu-Bonin Trench: bathymetric map}\label{map:IBT1}
\end{map}

Longitudinally, it extends from near Tokyo\index[places]{Tokyo}, Japan to Guam\index[places]{Guam} \cite{BarnesStraub}. In its structure, the trench continues the Japan Trench\index[places]{Japan Trench}. The \ac{IBT} is formed in the area of active tectonic subduction of the Cretaceous (135 Ma) Pacific Plate beneath the Philippine Sea Plate \cite{Sanoetal2016}. As a consequence of this subduction the following structured are formed: a volcanic arc of the Izu\index[places]{Izu Islands} and Bonin Islands\index[places]{Bonin Islands} on the Izu-Bonin-Mariana Arc system and the Shikoku Basin\index[places]{Shikoku Basin} as a backarc basin (Map \ref{map:IBT2}). The \ac{IBT} is boarded on the east by the Ogasawara Plateau\index[places]{Ogasawara Plateau} \cite{Nishizawaetal2006}.
In its historical development, the Izu Bonin arc has two major periods of arc formation that differ in duration, composition, and volcanology. The first lasted \textasciitilde 5 to 6 million years and produced voluminous boninitic-tholeiitic magmas. The second post-42 Ma Izu Bonin arc formation period produced a classical volcanic arc with stratocones \cite{BarnesStraub}. The formation of the Shikoku backarc basin took place in the early Miocene, ca. 30-15 Ma \cite{Taylor1992}. A new period of along-strike arc rifting began about 2.5 Ma ago \cite{Ishizuka2003}. The historical development of the oceanic trench is mirrored in its seabed structure, which is is well structured, including valious layers: a sedimentary cover, a basalt of the leitic composition, a complex of parallel dykes of diabases, gabbro and gabbro-ultrabasic complex lying on the mantle ultrabasites. 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=14cm]{Fig-IBT2.jpg}
	\caption{Izu-Bonin Trench: geologic map}\label{map:IBT2}
\end{map}

The trench extends to nearly 10 km depth. The 200 km wide forearc region includes inner trench slope with a basal terrace and seamounts, outer arc connected with the Bonin Islands\index[places]{Bonin Islands} and a thick forearc sedimentary basin east of the active arc volcanoes. The backarc region encompasses active rift basins and cross-chains of submarine arc volcanoes extending into the Shikoku Basin\index[places]{Shikoku Basin} \cite{DeBarietal1999}. Northern end of the Izu-Bonin arc is a juvenile oceanic arc colliding with the Honshu arc\index[places]{Honshu island arc}, a mature island arc since Middle Miocene Saito and Tani, 2017). 

A chain of serpentine seamounts is exposed at the forearc slope 40-50 km west of the \ac{IBT} axis. Seismically, few large earthquakes ($>M7.0$) \cite{Kamimuraetal2002} are detected at depths $<100$ km (red circles on Map \ref{map:IBT2}) and many large earthquakes at depths 100-300 km (green circles on Map \ref{map:IBT2}). The Boso triple junction\index[places]{Boso triple junction} located on the north of the Izu-Bonin Collision Zone\index[places]{Izu-Bonin Collision Zone} (Map \ref{map:IBT2}) is the northern end of the arc colliding with the Honshu Island arc\index[places]{Honshu island arc} as a consequence of the northwestward migration of the Philippine Sea Plate\index[places]{Philippine Sea Plate} \cite{Ogawaetal1989}.

The \ac{IBT}\index[places]{Izu-Bonin Trench} is boarded by the Philippine Sea tectonic plate in the west. The Philippine Sea\index[places]{Philippine Sea} is a geologically complicated region notable for complex bathymetry and plate shape \cite{Yamazakietal2010} and a complex patchwork of seafloor ages. The bounding subduction zones of the the Philippine Sea Plate\index[places]{Philippine Sea Plate} exhibit significant NS variation in slab morphology \cite{CizkovaBina2015}. As a results of the interplay of these factors, the trench is consisting of the Izu (north) and the Bonin (south) separated roughly by the Ogasawara Plateau\index[places]{Ogasawara Plateau}. Thus, seafloor ageing affects advancing trench through the subduction of progressively older and stiffer lithospheric material \cite{Faccennaetal2009}, which was further discussed by \cite{Ishizukaetal2018} who pointed on asymmetric spreading caused by ridge migration in the Izu-Bonin region in view of age difference between the oldest forearc basalt and the crust of the Amami Sankaku Basin\index[places]{Amami Sankaku Basin}. 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\subfloat[Geoid model]{\includegraphics[width=6.5cm]{Fig-IBT3.jpg}}
			\hspace{3mm}
		\subfloat[Free-air marine gravity model]{\includegraphics[width=7.5cm]{Fig-IBT4.jpg}}
			\hspace{3mm}
	\caption{Izu-Bonin Trench}\label{map:IBT4}
\end{map}

More detailed description of the Izu-Bonin tectonics, geophysical, petrological and geochemical settings, volcanism\index[concepts]{volcanism} and seismic characteristics, analysis and interpretation of geomorphological\index[concepts]{geomorphology}, geological, and thermochronological data, are reported in relevant publications \cite{Ichiyamaetal2017}; \cite{ArisaHeki}; \cite{Sendaetal2016}; \cite{Oshikaetal2014}; \cite{Hashimaetal2016}; \cite{Gongetal2017}. Hence, the region of the Izu-Bonin-Mariana Arc\index[places]{Izu-Bonin-Mariana Arc} is now one of the best and intensively studied oceanic arcs. However, lesser attention is given to the submarine geomorphology\index[concepts]{geomorphology} of the \ac{IBT}\index[places]{Izu-Bonin Trench} as a deep-sea geomorphic structure. Referring the lack of such research, current study aims at visualizing the trench by mapping and modelling its geomorphic form using cartographic approach of the \ac{GMT}.

Morphologic development of the \ac{IBT} took place in several phases shaped by the impact of various factors: geologic conditions, volcanic submarine areas, and inner ocean currents and sedimentation. Factors affecting formation, geomorphic development\index[concepts]{geomorphology} and bathymetric patterns of the trench are diverse. The most important ones include tectonic development, geological, hydro-chemical, biological, geothermal, climatic, tectonic, bathymetric and geomorphological determinants, deep ocean currents that bring sediments to the trench bottom and contributing towards accumulation of the sediment thickness. Impact of lithosphere\index[concepts]{Earth!lithosphere} is illustrated by a constant exchange of matter and energy between the submarine volcanoes\index[concepts]{submarine!volcanoes} located nearby \cite{HussongUyeda1982}. The hydrosphere\index[concepts]{hydrosphere} influence on the \ac{MT} is reflected by deep ocean currents bringing sediments to the trench bottom and contributing towards accumulation of the sediment thickness \cite{HorlestonHelffrich2012}. The trench is located in the collision of the tectonic plates. It has a distinctive morphological feature of the convergent Pacific Plate boundaries, along which lithospheric plates move towards each other. Not all seismic zones in subducted slabs of the \ac{IBT} are continuous and that some deep events seem to occur isolated from those at shallower depths. 

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\pagebreak
\section{Japan Trench}
The \ac{JT}\index[places]{Japan Trench} is located in western part of the Pacific Ocean, eastwards off Japan\index[places]{Japan} (Map \ref{map:JT1}). The geologic and tectonic settings in the region of the \ac{JT} are strongly influenced by the subduction of the Pacific Plate beneath northeastern Japan\index[places]{Japan Plate} and North American Plate\index[places]{North American Plate} along the \ac{JT} (Map \ref{map:JT3}), which is well described in the previous works \cite{HueneLallemand1990}. The region of the \ac{JT} is a convergent zone associated with many earthquakes \cite{Yoshii1979}. It belongs to the circum-Pacific seismic belt (a.k.a. 'Ring of Fire'\index[places]{Ring of Fire}) where large earthquakes originate within the areas of sedimentation on upper forearc slopes \cite{Araietal2014}. 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=14cm]{Fig-JT1.jpg}
	\caption{Japan Trench: topographic map}\label{map:JT1}
\end{map}

The structure of the trench presents a typical arc-trench system \cite{Ludwigetal1966}. In terms of tectonic geomorphology\index[concepts]{geomorphology}, the arc-trench system is a type of the mobile belts with bathymetric and topographic relief formed under the impact of tectonic plates subduction, active volcanism\index[concepts]{volcanism}, repetitive earthquakes and orogenesis. The geological complexity of the \ac{JT}\index[places]{Japan Trench} results in the high seismicity\index[concepts]{seismicity} of the area, which is well studied in various works \cite{Nakamuraetal2013}, \cite{HueneCulotta1989}; \cite{Linetal2016}; \cite{Bostonetal2017}. The geology of Japan trench-arc system\index[places]{Japan trench-arc system} consists of Jurassic to Neogene accretionary prisms and Neogene\index[concepts]{Geologic time period!Neogene} to Quaternary\index[concepts]{Geologic time period!Quaternary} sedimentary and volcanic rocks caused by volcanism\index[concepts]{volcanism} during the opening of the Sea of Japan\index[places]{Sea of Japan} in the middle Miocene \cite{Wakita2013}.

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=14cm]{Fig-JT3.jpg}
	\caption{Japan Trench: geological map}\label{map:JT3}
\end{map}

The Cretaceous-Paleogene granitic rocks are exposed in several regions, especially on the fore-arc side. High seismicity\index[concepts]{seismicity} of the region can be illustrated by several large interplate earthquakes that have affected the Tohoku\index[places]{Tohoku} region along the \ac{JT} during the last century \cite{Yagietal2012}. Because \ac{JT} is located on the tectonic plates subduction zone, the earthquakes here belong to the largest and most destructive earthquakes in the world \cite{Janssenetal2015}. Many open cracks\index[concepts]{slope!instability!tension cracks} on the diatomaceous clayey sediment surfaces of oceanward slopes of the northern \ac{JT} were detected by \cite{Ogawaetal1997}. The surface of the seabed outside the cracks (i.e. normal seabed) appears to be coated by ferroman-ganese oxides \cite{ItoOgawa1994}, and is overlain by fine pelagic mud, containing dominantly diatoms, silicoflagelates, radiolarians and sponge spicules, together with a considerable amount of detrital quartz and ash fragments.

Northern and southern parts of the \ac{JT} demonstrate variations in their geologic features with heterogeneously distributed seismicity\index[concepts]{seismicity} around the \ac{JT}. Most of the large earthquakes with magnitudes > 7 occurred in the northern forearc region of the trench. For example, the Tohoku-Oki\index[places]{Tohoku-Oki} earthquake of 2011, is the largest earthquake that took place in forearc region of the Northeast Japan subduction zone, west off the \ac{JT} \cite{Layetal2011}; \cite{Ikeharaetal2016}, through the shallow part of the subduction zone to the seafloor surface. It triggered a devastating tsunami with massive damages along the northeast coast of Honshu\index[places]{Honshu Island}, Japan \cite{Janssenetal2015}. 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=13cm]{Fig-JT4.jpg}
	\caption{Japan Trench: geoid model map}\label{map:JT4}
\end{map}

Southern part of the trench is seismically more 'quiet': no large earthquakes were recorded during recent centuries \cite{Miuraetal2005}, except for the 1938 Shioya-oki earthquake. The subduction zone characterizes northern part of the trench \cite{Tsuruetal2000}. In a 60 km buffer gap off the trench axis, a decollement structure with soft sediments is located. The zone of this structure between two tectonic plates decouples shear stresses, which in turn, forms an aseismic zone. Comparing two devastating earthquakes: the 1968 Tokachi-Oki\index[places]{Tokachi-Oki} and the 1994 Sanriku-Haruka-Oki\index[places]{Sanriku-Haruka-Oki}, two distinct heterogeneities along the plate boundary at $40^\circ 10^\prime$N, located exactly at the southern boundary of the two earthquakes were detected \cite{Hayakawaetal2002}. This well illustrates the direct affect of the plate tectonics triggering earthquake events that naturally lead to significant deformations in the geomorphic landforms\index[concepts]{geomorphology}. It further explains the coincidence of the focal regions and the structure with slower velocities in the lower crust. 

The subduction of the seamount chain penetrating to the Pacific Plate\index[places]{Pacific Plate} possibly take place. It can further create irregular geomorphic landforms\index[concepts]{geomorphology}. When these seamounts\index[concepts]{seamounts} subduct deep, they collapse due to the mechanical affects from the free water released by a collapsing seamount. In turn, it may rise into the island-arc mantle\index[concepts]{Earth!mantle!island-arc} and the lower crust\index[concepts]{Earth!crust!lower}. This brief illustration of the deep correlation of the tectonic and geological processes that result in actual land forms and geomorphological patterns\index[concepts]{geomorphology}. Seismic investigations of the \ac{JT} axis shown \cite{Boseetal2015} variations in the frictional resistance along a d\'{e}collement\index[concepts]{d\'{e}collement} on the structural development of the frontal wedges near the subduction zones. 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=13cm]{Fig-JT5.jpg}
	\caption{Japan Trench: gravity model map}\label{map:JT5}
\end{map}

The observed slope sediments between the inner and outer wedge may have accumulated during the growth of supercritical wedge. Seismic surveys indicate \cite{Kodairaetal2012} that shallow areas at the Tohoku earthquake\index[places]{Tohoku} experienced significant deformation. During this event multiple faults slipped above and below the pelagic clay layer \cite{Rabinowitzetal2015}. 

The geophysical features of the \ac{JT} area were discussed \cite{Linetal2016}: comparison of the \ac{JT} Nankai subduction zone\index[places]{Nankai subduction zone} for the distribution of the stress state. Based on the correlations between strength and temperature, the deep seismicity\index[concepts]{seismicity} is likely to result from plastic instabilities, with the distribution of earthquakes being related to strength distribution and therefore slab\index[concepts]{slab!mineralogy} mineralogy. A complex rheological structure for subducting slabs\index[concepts]{slab!subduction} has been proposed by \citet{Karatoetal2001}, due to the effects of grain size reduction during phase transformations. Based on their model, rapidly subducting and therefore relatively cold slabs\index[concepts]{slab!cold} should be weaker than slowly subducting warm slabs\index[concepts]{slab!warm}. Not all seismic zones\index[concepts]{seismicity} in subducted slabs are continuous and that some deep events seem to occur isolated from those at shallower depths \citep{IsacksMolnar1971}. 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=13cm]{Fig-JT6.jpg}
	\caption{Japan Trench: 3D mesh model map of geomorphology}\label{map:JT6}
\end{map}

Structure, magnetism, and dynamics of subduction zones\index[concepts]{subduction!zone}, the continuation of the low wave speed\index[concepts]{wave!velocity}, high attenuation (low Q), and seismically anisotropic wedge are normally near 400 km depth beneath some back arcs\index[concepts]{back arcs}. This may be explained by the persistence of hydrous phases to that depth and that magnetic systems are not limited to near-surface regions of the mantle. The correlation also exist between the dehydration of the slab\index[concepts]{slab!dehydration}, rupture nucleation and crustal earthquake triggering.


%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\pagebreak
\section{Puysegur Trench}
\ac{PsT}\index[places]{Puysegur Trench} has ca. 800 km in length, stretching from the most southern tip of the South Island\index[places]{South Island} to the Macquarie Triple Junction\index[places]{Macquarie Triple Junction}\index[concepts]{triple junction}. 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=13.0cm]{Fig-HPH-4.jpg}
	\caption{Hikurangi, Puysegur and Hjort trenches, New Zealand: GEBCO based topographic map}
	\label{map:HPH4}
\end{map}

The Macquarie Fault Zone\index[places]{Macquarie Fault Zone} is associated with the Alpine Fault\index[places]{Alpine Fault}, which is the right-lateral transform fault\index[concepts]{transform!fault, right-lateral} boundary separating the \ac{PsT}\index[places]{Puysegur Trench} and the northern \ac{KT}\index[places]{Kermadec Trench} \cite{Beavan2001}. The \ac{MRC}\index[places]{Macquarie Ridge Complex} is a dominantly translational structure accommodating right-lateral motion\index[concepts]{plate!motion, right-lateral} between the Australian\index[places]{Australian Plate} and Pacific Plates\index[places]{Pacific Plate} and \ac{PsT}\index[places]{Puysegur Trench}, where Australian lithosphere subducts beneath the southwest corner of New Zealand\index[places]{New Zealand} on the Pacific Plate\index[places]{Pacific Plate}. The plate boundary south of New Zealand is located along the \ac{MRC} which is crucial for understanding the tectonic structure of the region. Comparing to the Hikurangi\index[places]{Hikurangi Trench}, \ac{PsT}\index[places]{Puysegur Trench} and Hjort\index[places]{Hjort Trench} are younger (started at 16–8 Ma) and less developed \cite{Sutherlandetal2006}. The Indo-Australian Plate\index[places]{Indo-Australian Plate} subducts beneath the Puysegur Bank\index[places]{Puysegur Bank} and the Fiordland Massif\index[places]{Fiordland Massif\} forming \ac{PsT}. 

Hjort Trench\index[places]{Hjort Trench} continues \ac{PsT}\index[places]{Puysegur Trench} southwards from the Macquarie Island\index[places]{Macquarie Island} (Fig. \ref{map:HPH4}). It presents a linear topographic depression south of Macquarie Island in the southwest Pacific Ocean (Fig. \ref{map:HPH5}). Hjort Trench lies in an area of transpression\index[concepts]{plate!deformation!transpression} where plate boundary transitions from a transform boundary to a convergent one\index[concepts]{convergent}. Hence, this region shows how a transform boundary with a vertical (near-vertical) transform fault\index[concepts]{fault!transform!near-vertical} becomes an area of under-thrusting \cite{Meckel2003}. The deepest point of Hjort Trench is ca. 6.3 km b.s.l. Eastwards, the Hjort Ridge\index[places]{Hjort Ridge} follows the general curve of the Hjort Trench, separating it from the Hjort Plateau\index[places]{Hjort Plateau}. 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=13.0cm]{Fig-HPH-8.jpg}
	\caption{Hikurangi, Puysegur and Hjort trenches, New Zealand: geoid model}
	\label{map:HPH8}
\end{map}

Unique tectonic setting of the region consist in the connection of two subduction zones\index[concepts]{subduction!zone} in the north (Hikurangi margin\index[places]{Hikurangi Margin}) and in the south (Puysegur margin\index[places]{Puysegur Margin}), connected by an oblique continental collision along the Alpine Fault\index[places]{Alpine Fault} located in the South Island\index[places]{South Island} of New Zealand\index[places]{New Zealand}. This cause variations in the submarine geomorphic structure\index[concepts]{geomorphology} of the trenches which is also referred as 'subduction scissor'\index[concepts]{subduction!scissors} \cite{Pysklywec}. The Puysegur/Hjort subduction is highly oblique (dextral) and directed southwards. The Hikurangi subduction\index[concepts]{subduction!zone} is directed northwestwards. Hence, the South Island\index[places]{South Island} is caught in between by the 'subduction scissor'. In the absence of available high-resolution information and detailed mapping on the seabed geomorphology\index[concepts]{geomorphology} of the Hikurangi, Puysegur and Hjort trenches, a \ac{GMT} approach to map their seafloor on a local scale were undertaken. Since seafloor of the deep-sea trench represents an integrated response to a complex range of factors, such as oceanographic, geological and tectonic conditions which eventually define the present shape of the seabed landforms, an integrated approach was used to visualize, model and map three trenches on the New Zealand\index[places]{New Zealand} margin. 

Hjort Trench\index[places]{Hjort Trench} is characterized by an oblique subduction zone\index[concepts]{subduction!zone} geoid anomaly\index[concepts]{geoid}. Large 1924 earthquake occurred in the northern Hjort Trench which suggest a thrust focal mechanism\index[concepts]{earthquake!focal!mechanism} and active subduction of young oceanic lithosphere beneath older oceanic lithosphere \cite{Cazenave1985}. Frequent seismic events, most less than 20 km deep, characterize the transpression\index[concepts]{plate!deformation!transpression} along this plate boundary\index[concepts]{plate!boundary} \cite{LodoloCoren}. The southern segment of the Alpine Fault\index[places]{Alpine Fault} on the South Island\index[places]{South Island} continues into the Tasman Sea Basin\index[places]{Tasman Sea} and finishes at the \ac{PsT} (Fig. \ref{map:HPH5}). The oceanic Indo-Australian Plate\index[places]{Indo-Australian Plate} subducts eastwards through the \ac{PsT}\index[places]{Puysegur Trench} under the Pacific Plate\index[places]{Pacific Plate}. 

Geologic events during the tectonic historical records of this region, such as Eocene-Oligocene\index[concepts]{Geologic time period!Eocene}\index[concepts]{Geologic time period!Oligocene} rifting as well as Miocene-Quaternary\index[concepts]{Geologic time period!Miocene}\index[concepts]{Geologic time period!Quaternary} strike-slip, crustal thinning in the Solander Basin, development of the oblique subduction zone\index[concepts]{subduction!zone} in the \ac{PsT}\index[places]{Puysegur Trench}, are studied by processing seafloor relief data and interpretation of the seismic and bathymetric cross-section profiles acquired over the \ac{PsT} area \cite{Melhuish1999}; \cite{Lamarche1997}; \cite{Lamarche2000}. Effects of slab mineralogy and phase chemistry on the subduction dynamics\index[concepts]{subduction!dynamics} (buoyancy, stress field), kinematics (rate of subduction and plate motion), elasticity (deformation and seismic wave speed\index[concepts]{wave!seismic}), thermometry (effects of latent heat, isobaric superheating) and seismicity\index[concepts]{seismicity} (due to adiabatic shear instabilities) are well discussed \cite{Binaetal2001}. The morphology and development of the subducted slabs are more complex than modeled \cite{Christensen1996}, on either whole mantle flow or convective layering at 660 km depth. Apparently, slabs can deflect horizontally in the upper mantle transition zone beneath some convergent margins whereas penetration to lower mantle depths can occur beneath other island arc\index[concepts]{volcanic!island arc} segments. Different styles of the subduction across the upper mantle transition zone and trench formation.

Various physical and chemical processes interact during tectonic plates subduction\index[concepts]{tectonic!plate!subduction}, and the complexity of the system necessitates an interdisciplinary approach involving seismology\index[concepts]{seismology}, mineral physics\index[concepts]{mineral physics}, geochemistry\index[concepts]{geochemistry}, petrology\index[concepts]{petrology}, structural geology\index[concepts]{structural geology}, rock mechanics\index[concepts]{rock!mechanics}, and geodynamic modeling. For example, phase transformation kinetics determine buoyancy, rates of subduction and, therefore, thermal structure, with the latter feeding back to affect the kinetics. 

Rheology and large scale slab dynamics is affected by mineral transformations and their kinetics. The rheology of mantle minerals, though having considerable importance in controlling the subduction process\index[concepts]{subduction!process}, is exceedingly difficult to study experimentally, due to the experimental limitations, since rheology at pressures of the transition zone and lower mantle \cite{KaratoRubie1997}. An increase of intrinsic density or viscosity with depth as well as phase transitions with a negative Clapeyron slope can all inhibit or delay deep subduction\index[concepts]{subduction!deep}, tectonic conditions and relative motion of the tectonic plates at Earth’s surface play important role. The effects of subduction of crustal rocks during continental collision\index[concepts]{continental!collision} consists in following subduction to depths >90km. Slices of continental crust\index[concepts]{continental!crust} exhumed back to the surface very rapidly, thus ensuring the survival of the high-pressure minerals. 

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\pagebreak
\section{Hikurangi Trench}
The \ac{HT}\index[places]{Hikurangi Trench} (sometimes also referred as trough) is located on the Hikurangi margin\index[places]{Hikurangi Margin}, 500 km long 480 km wide \cite{NicolWallace2007}, northeast from the North Island\index[places]{North Island}. 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=12.0cm]{Fig-HPH-5.jpg}
	\caption{Hikurangi Trench, New Zealand: tectonic and geological settings. }
	\label{map:HPH5}
\end{map}

The Pacific Plate subducts beneath the Indo-Australian Plate\index[places]{Indo-Australian Plate} at the \ac{HT}; Indo-Australian Plate subducts beneath the Pacific Plate\index[places]{Pacific Plate} at the Puysegur\index[places]{Puysegur Trench} and Hjort trenches\index[places]{Hjort Trench}, Fig. \ref{map:HPH5}. Two subduction margins\index[concepts]{subduction!margins}  are joined by the dextral transform\index[concepts]{fault!transform!dextral}  Alpine Fault\index[places]{Alpine Fault} west off New Zealand. The area selected for the dataset lies in  southern Pacific Ocean and encompasses region off New Zealand\index[places]{New Zealand} coast (Fig. \ref{map:HPH4}). 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=12.0cm]{Fig-HPH-9.jpg}
	\caption{Hikurangi, Puysegur and Hjort trenches, New Zealand: Marine free-air gravity anomaly}
	\label{map:HPH9}
\end{map}

This area is characterized by a geologically complex history and current situation with oblique subduction zone\index[concepts]{subduction!oblique} and subduction scissors\index[concepts]{subduction!scissors}. Such complex bathymetry exhibits considerable geomorphological variability\index[concepts]{geomorphology} among the Hikurangi, Puysegur\index[places]{Puysegur Trench} and Hjort\index[places]{Hjort Trench} trenches: there are differences caused by seamounts\index[concepts]{seamounts} and seafloor obstructing natural curvatures of the trenches\index[concepts]{trench!curvature}. The toponymy of the \ac{HT}\index[places]{Hikurangi Trench} vary in the existing literature due to its specific geomorphology\index[concepts]{geomorphology}: in some works it is referred as 'trench'\index[concepts]{trench}, e.g. \cite{Clarketal2019}; \cite{Reyes2010}; \cite{Reyners2013}; \cite{Reynersetal2011}, in other works -- as 'trough\index[concepts]{trough}, e.g. \cite{Barnesetal2010}; \cite{Greinert}; \cite{Jiaoetal2017}; \cite{Jiaoetal2015}.

The tectonic location of the Hikurangi is at the southern end of the 1000 km long Tonga-Kermadec-Hikurangi\index[places]{Tonga-Kermadec-Hikurangi subduction system} subduction systems, where Pacific Plate subducts obliquely southwards beneath the Indo-Australian Plate \cite{Barnes1997} and Indo-Australian Plate has a strike-slip motion at 4 cm/yr, Fig. \ref{map:HPH4}. Comparing to the \ac{KT}\index[places]{Kermadec Trench} reaching up to 9000 m, \ac{HT}\index[places]{Hikurangi Trench} is shallow (ca. 3000 m). 

Pacific Plate dips at \ac{HT} at a gentle angle of about 3\degree\space for at least 100 km beneath the trench and then further steepens beneath the North Island\index[places]{North Island} \cite{Barkeretal2009}. The Alpine Fault stretches sub-parallel\index[concepts]{fault!stretching, sub-parallel} to the western edge of the \ac{HT} (Fig. \ref{map:HPH4}). The speed of subduction\index[concepts]{subduction!speed} on the Hikurangi Margin\index[places]{Hikurangi Margin} varies with a general trend of increase northwards: 34 mm/yr in the south (westward off Bounty Trough\index[places]{Bounty Trough}), 38 mm/yr in its central part (North Island\index[places]{North Island}) and 47 mm/yr in the north (North Island) according to \cite{Reyners2013}. The Hikurangi north-west dipping subduction\index[concepts]{subduction!dipping} zone is old (started ca. 20 Ma). The subducted slab has a well defined Wadati–Benioff zone\index[concepts]{Wadati-Benioff zone} and steepens from shallow angles near the surface to near-vertical at depths >100 km \cite{Ballance1976}. 

The geophysical and geological settings of the Hikurangi margin vary along the trench from south to north \cite{Wallaceetal2009}. Thus, interseismic coupling\index[concepts]{interseismic coupling} is strong and deep in the south, then gradually shallows northwards and becomes shallow and weak in the north. Southern region is characterized by accretionary wedge\index[concepts]{accretionary wedge} and tectonic contraction in the forearc\index[concepts]{forearc}. Northern region is characterized by frontal subduction erosion\index[concepts]{subduction!frontal erosion}, numerous seamounts\index[concepts]{seamounts}, and a slightly extensional upper plate\index[concepts]{plate!upper}. 

Kinematics\index[concepts]{trench!kinematics} of the Hikurangi margin\index[places]{Hikurangi Margin} studied by \ac{GPS}\index[concepts]{geodesy!GPS} measurements of the horizontal velocity fields\index[concepts]{velocity, horizontal fields} \cite{NicolWallace2007} shown changes in faulting along the margin and clockwise tectonic block rotations\index[concepts]{tectonic!block rotations} caused by collision\index[concepts]{tectonic!plate!collision} of the Hikurangi Plateau\index[places]{Hikurangi Plateau} and Chatham Rise\index[places]{Chatham Rise} with \ac{HT}\index[places]{Hikurangi Trench} and subduction of oceanic crust\index[concepts]{Earth!crust!oceanic} northeast of the North Island. 

The Hikurangi Plateau\index[places]{Hikurangi Plateau} initially formed a part and now separated from the largest oceanic plateau\index[concepts]{plateau!oceanic} on Earth, the Ontong Java Plateau\index[places]{Ontong Java Plateau}, located in the southwestern Pacific Ocean\index[places]{Pacific Ocean} \cite{Neal1997}. The Hikurangi Margin and continental shelf\index[concepts]{shelf!continental} of the North Island of the New Zealand\index[places]{New Zealand} is well studied through various geologic investigations, more specifically: bathymetric, hydro-acoustic investigations \cite{Barnesetal2010}, active tectonics. Palaeogeographic reconstruction of the region of Hikurangi introduced by \cite{Jiaoetal2015} with respect to the evolution of its current topography/bathymetry was done using advanced methods of the thermochronology\index[concepts]{thermochronology}. 

Slab dynamics is one of the most important drivers for the trench formation, dynamics and migration. Many factors affect speed and direction of the trench migration. These include plate-mantle coupling, slab interactions with the mantle transition, plate geometry, kinematics, strain rate, temperature, fluid pressure, deformation mechanisms, as well as mantle rheology \cite{Faccennaetal2018}. Understanding mechanisms of trench migration (retreat or advance) is crucial to characterizing the driving forces of Earth’s tectonics plates, the origins of subducting slab morphologies in the deep mantle\index[concepts]{Earth!mantle!deep}, and identifying the characteristics of subduction zones systems, which are among the fundamental issues of solid Earth science \cite{Yoshida2017}. 

Further findings in exploration geology in the \ac{HT}\index[places]{Hikurangi Trench} with a special focus on methane seepage and gas hydrates\index[concepts]{gas hydrates} in the underlying sediments are relatively well studied (e.g. \cite{Greinert}; \cite{Wangetal2017}; \cite{Klauckeetal2010}; \cite{Pecheretal2010}; \cite{Krabbenhoeft2013}; \cite{Krabbenhoeft2010}; \cite{Martinetal2010}); studies on past (Holocene) subduction earthquakes, coseismic coastal deformation\index[concepts]{deformation!coseismic} and tsunami\index[concepts]{tsunami} \cite{Clarketal2019}; \cite{Berrymanetal2011} which later on largely affected submarine geomorphology\index[concepts]{geomorphology} of the Hikurangi margin forming submerged terraces\index[concepts]{terrace!submerged} \cite{Berryman1993}.

% Methodology
%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\pagebreak
\chapter{Methodology}\label{chp:3}
	\counterwithin{figure}{section}
	\counterwithin{table}{section}

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Current dissertation contributed towards the development of the advanced approaches of data analysis demonstrating the combined use of \ac{GMT}, Python, AWK, R and Octave/Matlab. \ac{IT} approaches in Earth sciences are effective method. Statistical data analysis was implemented by \ac{ML}\index[concepts]{Artificial Intelligence!Machine Learning} algorithms. Seamless integration of the multi-dimensional data by R and Python statistical packages enabled to perform marine geodata analysis and proper selection of the optimal algorithms to process the data features. The fully provided codes written on the programming language make this research easily repeatable and accessible for other scientists. The adoption of the open source programming rapidly replacing the commercial technologies, Python and R will soon become popular among the marine geologists. This research contributes towards the development of the technical implementation of the \ac{ML}\index[concepts]{Artificial Intelligence!Machine Learning} algorithms for effective processing of the geodata. 	

\section{Data}
\lettrine[lines=3]{\color{DeepPink4}D}{ata} used in the dissertation for modelling and visualization include raster and vector types. Data are listed in details in Table \ref{tab:T2}. High-quality geospatial data sets are crucial for the geospatial data analysis in a big research project. The importance of the quality of the input data is explained by the fact that it affects the final results: high-precision data always results in the reliability of the output. Therefore, the selection, assessment and integration of relevant data was initial step of the cartographic project. Selecting correct data significantly improves the quality of the results, and the interpretation of the trench geomorphology\index[concepts]{geomorphology}. The more it is actual for the bathymetric and geomorphic mapping where precision is the key factor for the research output \cite{Smith}, \cite{WesselWatts}. 

Raster data include bathymetric and topographic grids. Based on an analysis of the \ac{ETOPO1}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!ETOPO1} maps for the 20 trenches, suitable segments were selected for plotting sample data on the cross-section profiles. The geoid\index[concepts]{geoid} and gravity grids were visualized based on the CryoSat-2, Jason-1 raster grids. Usage of such data as \ac{GEBCO}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!GEBCO} (supported by \ac{BODC}), \ac{ETOPO1}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!ETOPO1} (from \ac{NOAA}, \cite{AmanteEakins2009}), \ac{SRTM}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!SRTM} (supported by \ac{NASA}), CryoSat-2\index[concepts]{cartography!data!CryoSat-2} and Jason-1\index[concepts]{cartography!data!Jason-1} raster grids enables to perform advanced spatial analysis that were demonstrated further in relevant sub-sections. Data analysis and processing grids were performed using \ac{GMT}, 

Vector data include global layers of coastlines, continents and shorelines embedded in the \ac{GMT} and downloaded high-resolution \ac{ASCII}\index[concepts]{data!ASCII} xyz topographic and gravity data sets from open source repositories (\ac{USGS}). Coastal borders on all maps were based on the \ac{GSHHGD}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!GSHHGD} to visualize thematic geologic maps for the area of the trenches. High-resolution geo data collection was performed from the web-site \ac{USGS} and available embedded \ac{GIS}\index[concepts]{GIS} layers of \ac{GMT} \cite{WesselSmith1996}, \cite{WesselSmith1991}, \cite{SmithSandwell1997b}. A subset of regional geological settings was collected from the unified catalog of the \ac{SIO}. Geologic lineaments, ridges, volcanic spots, seafloor fabric, lines of tectonic plates boudaries, rate of movements and fracture zones were mapped through the batch use of several \ac{GMT} modules and converted to image files by \ac{GMT} 'psconvert' module. Topographic descriptions of the submarine features were referenced to the existing \ac{IHO} database of names. 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=13cm]{Fig-JT2.jpg}
	\caption{Comparison of the resolution in datasets: GEBCO, SRTM, ETOPO1 and ETOPO5}\label{map:JT2}
\end{map}

Other data used in current dissertation included high-resolution bathymetric dataset \href{https://www.gebco.net/}{GEBCO}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!GEBCO} \cite{GEBCO2016}; \cite{Monahan2004}). The \ac{GEBCO}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!GEBCO} project aimed at high precision mapping of the ocean seafloor originated as early as origins at the beginning of the \nth{20} century and since then up to now have been developed by several oceanographic organizations \cite{Mayeretal2018}; \cite{Weatheralletal2015}. 

Regular updates of the \ac{GEBCO}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!GEBCO} dataset grid, \ac{R/V} based bathymetric observation and hydrographic meetings \cite{Ferrini2018} maintain both the accuracy of \ac{GEBCO}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!GEBCO} grids \cite{Tani2017} and the actuality of names of the submarine objects \cite{Stagpooleetal2016}. There are other data sets commonly used in the geographic studies (\ac{SRTM}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!SRTM}, \ac{ETOPO1}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!ETOPO1}, \ac{ETOPO5})\index[concepts]{cartography!data!ETOPO5}. However, \ac{GEBCO}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!GEBCO} resolution of the 15 seconds grids gives a reliable accuracy comparing to the other raster data (Fig. \ref{map:JT2}). Using \ac{GEBCO}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!GEBCO} grid was done by \ac{GMT} scripting toolset using existing methodologies and technical documentation available both on \ac{GEBCO}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!GEBCO} \cite{Kumar2013} and \ac{GMT} \cite{Wesseletal2013}.

High precision data resources and grids were selected and chosen from the variety of the available datasets, as a ground basis of this research as summarized in the Tab. \ref{tab:T2}. General bathymetric maps were based on a cartographic visualizing of the \ac{ETOPO1}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!ETOPO1} raster grid \cite{AmanteEakins2009}, enlarged visualization by \ac{SRTM}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!SRTM} \cite{Farretal2007} and \ac{GEBCO}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!GEBCO}, both 15 seconds resolution. High-quality bathymetric \ac{ETOPO1} and and \ac{ETOPO5} data were used and compared to perform mapping and geomorphological\index[concepts]{geomorphology} modelling  (Fig. \ref{map:JT2}).

\pagebreak
\section{Approaches}
\lettrine[lines=3]{\color{HotPink4}G}{eneral} methodological scheme of the presented research is summarized in Tab. \ref{tab:T5}. Traditional methods of the marine geological modelling include using \ac{GIS}\index[concepts]{GIS} based processing of the \ac{RS} images, such as for instance aerial photos, \ac{SPOT}3\index[concepts]{cartography!data!SPOT3}, \ac{SPOT}4\index[concepts]{cartography!data!SPOT4} and \ac{ENVISAT}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!ENVISAT} data, or producing digital maps based on the data capture in the field \cite{Bogdanovetal2011}. The methodology of the research in submarine geomorphology\index[concepts]{geomorphology} is traditionally being supported by the GIS software, mainly in geological modeling. Geomorpholoigal studies of the hydrological effects often use \ac{GIS}\index[concepts]{GIS} modeling and digital terrain analysis for geospatial analysis \cite{Berger2011}.
