Riverine morphological analysis \cite{Bertoldietal2015} uses methodology base don experimental fieldwork, setup of network development combined with spatial data analysis by \ac{GIS}\index[concepts]{GIS}. Other examples include application of aerial imagery of the analysis of the river deltas discharge time series, and land cover and morphological planimetric changes by methods of combined \ac{GIS}\index[concepts]{GIS} based overlay of the topographic maps, air photographs, and satellite images showing river banks \cite{Arrospideetal2018}. 

Recent methodological example of the \ac{ESRI} ArcGIS application \cite{Bechteretal2018} shown riverine geomorphology\index[concepts]{geomorphology} covering large spatial extents by assessment of their structure, functioning and geomorphology using free datasets, GEarthView plugin for \ac{QGIS} and algorithms of the \ac{RS} data processing. ArcGIS based research is presented by \cite{Marcheseetal2017} focused an a regional scale analysis of changes in channel width and pattern. The assessment of the magnitude and direction of changes caused by flood management in the low land rivers is presented by \cite{Hudsonetal2008}. By cross-section profiles plotting large datasets of the multi-source data were analyzed: sedimentary cores, hydrographic surveys and historic map to visualize degrees of the involved engineering activities and infrastructure resulting in channel and floodplain modification that affect fluvial processes. 

Combined methods of field surveys, seismic profiles echo sounding and classification of the Landsat TM imagery is a core approach \cite{Iannirubertoeta2018}. The approach towards submarine canyon mapping is presented as a \ac{ROV}-based habitat mapping program using \ac{GEBCO}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!GEBCO} map cartography \cite{Huvenneetal2011}. The proposed methodology of modeling hydro-morphological degradation \cite{Klaueretal2014} is based on various environmental parameters of particular ecological importance (longitudinal and transverse banks, flow diversity, depth and width variance, substrate type and diversity). An application of the Matlab ProcessV3 for hydrological modeling was presented by \cite{Leetal2018}. Effective approach of the assessment of status and trends in marine fisheries and ecosystems considering marine geomorphology\index[concepts]{geomorphology} as proxy for marine habitats is proposed by \cite{HeymanWright2011}. 

On the contrary with given above examples, current dissertation makes an accent on using high-level programming languages and scripting approach for processing large data frames\index[concepts]{data!frame} imported from \ac{GIS}\index[concepts]{GIS}. 

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\pagebreak
\section{Generic Mapping Tools (GMT)}\label{sec:GMT}

\lettrine[lines=3]{\color{Magenta4}M}{ain} toolset used in this research is \ac{GMT}\index[concepts]{GMT} with modules functionality described in relevant documentation and software release announcement \cite{Wesseletal2013}. Comparing to the traditional \ac{GIS}\index[concepts]{GIS}\index[concepts]{GIS} software, the advantages of the \ac{GMT} consist in its high efficiency for the scripting based cartographic workflow rather than \ac{GUI} as well as open source availability. General scheme of the methodology in \ac{GMT} included following steps: 

\begin{itemize}
	\item Bathymetric mapping based on the extracted \ac{ETOPO1} grid by \ac{GMT} modules 'grdcut'\index[concepts]{GMT!module!grdcut} and 'grdimage'\index[concepts]{GMT!module!grdimage}; 
	\item mapping, visualizing and analysis of the thematic data sets: marine gravity and geoid,
	\item topographic and gravimetric surface modelling: based on the xyz \ac{ASCII} data\index[concepts]{data!ASCII}; 
	\item cartographic data processing by 'psbasemap'\index[concepts]{GMT!module!psbasemap}, 'psxy'\index[concepts]{GMT!module!psxy}, 'grdcontour'\index[concepts]{GMT!module!grdcontour} for contour plotting; 
	\item 3D-mesh modelling\index[concepts]{plot!3D}; 
	\item automatically digitized set of the orthogonal cross-stacked profiles by 'grdtrack'\index[concepts]{GMT!module!grdtrack} module along the selected segment of the trench with track 400 km long, spaced 20 km, sampled 2km along;
	\item visualizing mean, median values and plotting trends for the profiles by 'trend1d'\index[concepts]{GMT!module!trend1d} module; 
	\item descriptive statistical analysis through the visualized histograms. 
\end{itemize}

The use of the \ac{GMT} was presented as mapping in some works, e.g. \cite{Hsuetal2012}. Examples of other advanced tools for geological data analysis include, e.g., PHASER diffractometer, direct observations received from the \ac{R/V} cruises, observations from \ac{DEM} \cite{Giletycz}. The mapping in the presented dissertation is based on the available data sets and those embedded in the \ac{GMT} \cite{SmithSandwell1997a}. 

\ac{GMT} scripting toolset was selected as a geospatial tool for raster grids processing, mapping and automated digitizing of the geomorphological profiles\index[concepts]{geomorphology}. The \ac{GMT} was initially developed by P. Wessel and P. Smith \cite{Wesseletal2013}, maintained and improved to finally become a powerful open source scripting toolset for the geospatial analysis and cartographic visualization. 

In contrast with classic \ac{GIS}\index[concepts]{GIS}, \ac{GMT} is completely based on the UNIX-based shell scripting\index[concepts]{UNIX!shell script} approach with command-line usage without graphical \ac{GUI} interface. Thus, it is not a classical \ac{GIS}\index[concepts]{GIS}. Using the full powerful functionality of the \ac{GMT} requires writing a script with numerous arguments for the fine-tuning and setting up of every cartographic element: projections\index[concepts]{cartography!map projection}, labels\index[concepts]{cartography!map element!labels}, north direction\index[concepts]{cartography!map element!north direction}, major and minor grids\index[concepts]{cartography!grid}, titles, color palettes\index[concepts]{cartography!map element!grid}, legends\index[concepts]{cartography!map element!legend}, vector elements\index[concepts]{cartography!map element!vector lines}, annotations\index[concepts]{cartography!map element!annotation}, map elements\index[concepts]{cartography!map element} (coastlines\index[concepts]{cartography!map element!coastline}, directional roses\index[concepts]{cartography!map element!directional rose}, scale bars\index[concepts]{cartography!map element!scale bar}) etc. 

Hence, the functionality of the \ac{GMT} approach consists in its scripting when individual features for the cartographic objects can be defined in each line of the \ac{GMT} code. Each small code line presents a reference to a specific \ac{GMT} module. Combined together as a sequential script, the codes are used for mapping. As demonstrated further in the presented codes (see Appendix for the \ac{GMT} scripts), a great advantage of the \ac{GMT} is a high cartographic functionality of the toolset enabling to fully adjust and refine aesthetics of the cartographic elements: fonts, lines width and type, layout, a large variety of the visualization, plotting, modelling, advanced cartographic modules (mapping projections\index[concepts]{cartography!map projection}, modelling, grid raster data processing), color scales\index[concepts]{cartography!map element!color scale} etc. \cite{WesselSmith1991}.  

However, the high cost of the steep learning curve of the GMT as well as its non-visual command line based operation pays off by its powerful functionality and excellent visualization. The GMT is compatible with Unix commands (cut\index[concepts]{UNIX!utility!cut}, rm\index[concepts]{UNIX!utility!rm}, echo\index[concepts]{UNIX!utility!echo}) and \ac{GDAL}\index[concepts]{GDAL} raster processing, it operates both with raster\index[concepts]{cartography!data!raster} and vector\index[concepts]{cartography!data!vector} data, produces high-quality maps and figures, has embedded functions for descriptive statistical analysis (histograms). All this makes it one of the most preferred data visualization and mapping toolsets by the cartographers. 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
 		\includegraphics[width=9.5cm]{Fig-GMT-ortho-PO.jpg}
	\caption{North Pacific Ocean: contour map in the Orthographic azimuthal projection, GMT code \cite{Lemenkova201955}}
\label{map:NPO}       
\end{map}

As the utility of the seafloor\index[concepts]{seafloor}-mapping technology, a \ac{GMT} scripting was demonstrated with a case study for comparative analysis of the 20 trenches.  Because the \ac{GMT} is a scripting toolset consisting of modules, several main modules were used for preparing maps. The auxiliary \ac{GMT} modules were used for mapping and cartographic visualizations. Methods of data visualization through \ac{GMT} modules include cross-section profiles, topographic modelling, thematic geologic mapping, modelling geoid\index[concepts]{geoid} and gravity fields. A number of \ac{GMT} techniques and modules (e.g. grdhisteq\index[concepts]{GMT!module!grdhisteq}, pscoast\index[concepts]{GMT!module!pscoast}, pshistogram\index[concepts]{GMT!module!pshistogram}, pstext\index[concepts]{GMT!module!pstext}, gmtset\index[concepts]{GMT!module!gmtset}, gmtdefaults\index[concepts]{GMT!module!gmtdefaults}, grdcut\index[concepts]{GMT!module!grdcut}, makecpt\index[concepts]{GMT!module!makecpt}, grdimage\index[concepts]{GMT!module!grdimage}, psscale\index[concepts]{GMT!module!psscale}, grdcontour\index[concepts]{GMT!module!grdcontour}, psbasemap\index[concepts]{GMT!module!psbasemap}, nearneighbor\index[concepts]{GMT!module!nearneighbor} (codes: \cite{Lemenkova201976} for \ac{KKT}, Figure and \cite{Lemenkova201977} for \ac{MT}), gmtlogo\index[concepts]{GMT!module!gmtlogo}, psconvert\index[concepts]{GMT!module!psconvert}) were used to visualize, model and map seafloor\index[concepts]{seafloor} characteristics, as well as texture and shape of the cross-section profiles. A 'gmtinfo'\index[concepts]{GMT!module!gmtinfo}module was used to get information about data tables from the raster grids. Auxiliary \ac{GMT} modules were used for technical data processing, for example, 'gmtwhich'\index[concepts]{GMT!module!gmtwhich} to find the full paths to the files given on the command line, a 'gmt.conf'\index[concepts]{GMT!module!gmt.conf} module was used to define configuration of the \ac{GMT} parameters. Interpretation of the topographic profiles, geological sampling, gravity modelling and statistical analysis was also made by means of the \ac{GMT} scripting toolset. A combination of \ac{GMT} modules was applied for modelling, analysis, comparison and visualization of the variations in the geomorphology\index[concepts]{geomorphology} of the trenches.  

\ac{ML}\index[concepts]{Artificial Intelligence!Machine Learning} approach of \ac{GMT} was used for semi-automatic plotting, visualization, modelling, mapping and interpretation of the three hadal trenches located in southern Pacific Ocean. Sequential use of \ac{GMT} modules enabled to gain further insights into their geomorphic\index[concepts]{geomorphology} shape, and to compare accuracy of the data grid interpretation approaches based on the scripting concept aimed to visualize cross-section transects. Indeed, \ac{GMT} cross-section stacking methodology as performed in this research proved to be a successful means of visualizing and plotting geomorphological models applied for the submarine bathymetry by effectively minimizing the hand-made cartographic routine. Indeed, using \ac{GMT} enables to automatically digitize profiles, then to perform statistical analysis by plotting histograms and model trends for the general shape of the profiles using mathematical models of the lines approximation. 

Several \ac{GMT} modules were tested and applied for the cartographic visualization of the marine free-air gravity\index[concepts]{gravity!anomaly!marine free-air}, geoid\index[concepts]{geoid}, bathymetric mapping, geomorphic modelling\index[concepts]{geomorphology} and statistical data analysis. Existing semi-automated methods of cartographic digitizing (e.g. Autotrace based) \cite{SchenkeLemenkova2008}, \cite{Lemenkova201561} or sequential use of the specialized software for bathymetric mapping, Hydrosweep DS-2 \cite{Lemenkova201569}, CARIS HIPS \cite{Lemenkova201565}, GMT for mapping using CARIS HIPS data by grids and coastlines \cite{Lemenkova2006A072}, \cite{Lemenkova2006A073}, \cite{Lemenkova2006A074}, \cite{Lemenkova2006A014}, \cite{Lemenkova2006A013}, \cite{Lemenkova2006A012}, \cite{Lemenkova2006A011} was driven largely by the developed algorithms of the \ac{ML}\index[concepts]{Artificial Intelligence!Machine Learning}. Application of the \ac{AI}\index[concepts]{Artificial Intelligence} in the cartographic routine, significantly minimizes the handmade routine and subjectivity. On the contrary, it increases the overall precision of the data processing and accuracy of visualization. The \ac{GMT} proposes more advanced solutions in the automatization of the cross-sections through the \ac{ML}\index[concepts]{Artificial Intelligence!Machine Learning} based digitizing that results in a series of the profile transects across the 20 trenches of the Pacific Ocean. 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
 		\includegraphics[width=9.5cm]{Fig-KKT-land-mask.jpg}
	\caption{Landmask of the shorelines (land vs water areas), GMT code \cite{Lemenkova201971}}
\label{map:NPO}       
\end{map}

Applying \ac{GMT} techniques to\index[concepts]{seafloor} mapping trenches enabled to model profiles and visualize geologic settings. Each trench was distinguished by specific geomorphic shape\index[concepts]{geomorphology} of the submarine landform, geologic substrate, complexity of the tectonic settings and historical development of the lithospheric plates movements in this area. Bathymetry data were modeled by \ac{GMT} cross-section profiling for the trenches and compared. Maximal depths in the trench segments derived from digitized \ac{GEBCO}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!GEBCO} and \ac{ETOPO1}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!ETOPO1} grids were used for comparative analysis and visualized at histograms. 

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\pagebreak
\subsection{Bathymetric mapping}
\lettrine[lines=3]{\color{Magenta4}M}{apping} oceanic trench geomorphology data\index[concepts]{geomorphology} from the bathymetric maps should be based on the high resolution data to highlight isolines enabling to interpret steepness and gradient which requires certain cartographic interpretation. High-resolution bathymetric data is important  for mapping trench shape in a cross-section and to identify clearly its geomorphology. Pixel size at a course resolution bathymetric maps (e.g. \ac{ETOPO5}) may reduce accuracy, therefore, \ac{ETOPO1}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!ETOPO1} and \ac{GEBCO}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!GEBCO} dataset grids were chosen for bathymetric mapping.

The \ac{ETOPO1}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!ETOPO1} global relief model was modeled using ‘grdimage’ module of \ac{GMT}. Examples of trenches are presented in corresponding section (Map \ref{map:A1}), \ac{KKT} (Fig. \ref{map:KKT1}).
The ‘psbasemap’, ‘grdcontour’ \ac{GMT} modules were used to map base map elements (e.g. contours of the land, geoid\index[concepts]{geoid} and bathymetry, net grids and titles) on the bathymetric maps. Each module consists of the small code line. Combined together as a script, they were used to produce and visualize maps. For example, the \ac{GMT} modules ‘grdimage’ was main module used to map the bathymetric grid of the \ac{RT} (Map \ref{map:R1}) using \ac{GMT} code: 

gmt grdimage GEBCO\_2019.nc -Cmyocean.cpt -R120/134/20/33 - JM6i -P -I+a15+ne0.75 -Xc -K > \$ps 

For example, in case of Tonga-Kermadec region, the -R command was used to cut off  part of the image was by the given coordinates: -R140/195/-50/-5 from the whole grid in WESN way. As can be noticed, the southern hemisphere has negative coordinates. 

The color palette was made using 'makecpt' module from the available 'geo' palette adjusted according to the data range (for example, the Tonga-Kermadec area had topography from -11000 to 4500. The cartographic projection\index[concepts]{cartography!map projection}, for the bathymetric map was selected as traditional Mercator, which was set up using the following command: '-JM6i' means Mercator projection\index[concepts]{cartography!map projection!Mercator}, with 6 inches width of the map; '-P' means portrait orientation; '-K' means continue of the script (not finalized). The legend\index[concepts]{cartography!map element!legend} on the left part of the bathymetric maps was added using 'psscale' module by following code snippet (here example is given for the Kermadec region, Map \ref{map:TK1}): 

gmt psscale -Dg131/-50+w14.8c/0.4c+v+o0.3/0i+ml Rtkt\_relief.nc -J -Cmyocean.cpt \\
--FONT\_LABEL=8p,Helvetica,dimgray --FONT\_ANNOT\_PRIMARY=5p,Helvetica,dimgray \\
-Baf+l"Topographic color scale" -I0.2 -By+lm -O -K >> \$ps 

For the case of Middle America Trench, the general bathymetric map (Fig. \ref{map:MAT1}) was visualized using a sequence of \ac{GMT} modules: 'grdcut' module for cutting area from the global grid earth\_relief\_01m.grd by selecting coordinates -R263/278/7/17, following by 'grdimage' module for visualizing the grid itself. Several auxiliary modules were then used both for cartographic purposes (color scale\index[concepts]{cartography!map element!color scale}, scale bar\index[concepts]{cartography!map element!scale bar}, directional rose\index[concepts]{cartography!map element!directional rose}, grid ticks\index[concepts]{cartography!map element!grid ticks} and lines\index[concepts]{cartography!map element!vector lines}) and for the map embellishment\index[concepts]{cartography!map element!embellishment} (\ac{GMT} logo\index[concepts]{cartography!map element!logo}) using available techniques \cite{SmithSandwell1997a}. The shoreline vector layers were driven from the existing GMT data sets \cite{WesselSmith1996}: tectonics, bathymetry, geomorphology\index[concepts]{geomorphology} and geology. 

The \ac{GMT} modules ‘psconvert’, ‘psscale’ were used for visualizing scale bar on the maps, \ac{GMT} module ‘logo’ to add \ac{GMT} logo below the maps, ‘pstext’ \ac{GMT} module was used for adding annotations on the maps, ‘psxy’ module - for plotting geologic lineaments and sample points. The bathymetric data are presented as plan and oblique shaded-relief grids to highlight the major and distinct seafloor landforms of the \ac{MAT} (Fig. \ref{map:MAT1}) which was mapped using the script provided in full in Appendix \ref{GMT:01}. 

Cartographic functionality of the GMT is assessed by technical visualization of the produced maps, models and graphs as well as tested variety of map projections\index[concepts]{cartography!map projection}. In this study, following various cartographic projections were used for plotting maps: Mercator\index[concepts]{cartography!map projection!cylindrical} (Fig. \ref{map:HPH4}, \ref{map:HPH5}, \ref{map:HPH6}, \ref{map:HPH7}, \ref{map:HPH10}, \ref{map:HPH11}), Mollweide pseudo-cylindrical homolographic equal-area\index[concepts]{cartography!map projection!Mollweide pseudo-cylindrical homolographic equal-area}, Eckert IV equal-area pseudo-cylindrical\index[concepts]{cartography!map projection!Eckert IV equal-area pseudo-cylindrical}, Lambert Azimuthal Equal-Area\index[concepts]{cartography!map projection!Lambert Azimuthal Equal-Area}\index[concepts]{cartography!map projection!azimuthal} (Fig. \ref{map:HPH8}), Oblique Mercator\index[concepts]{cartography!map projection!Oblique Mercator} (Fig. \ref{map:HPH9} by code \cite{Lemenkova201965}) or \cite{Lemenkova201966} (Mariana Trench area), Orthographic azimuthal projection\index[concepts]{cartography!map projection!Orthographic azimuthal} \cite{Lemenkova201955} (Fig. \ref{map:NPO}), Polyconic projection\index[concepts]{cartography!map projection!Polyconic}, \cite{Lemenkova201968}, Cylindrical Equal-Area Gall-Peters\index[concepts]{cartography!map projection!Cylindrical Equal-Area Gall-Peters}, \cite{Lemenkova201969} (here: example for the Mariana Trench), Transverse Mercator cylindrical \index[concepts]{cartography!map projection!Transverse Mercator}, Fig. \ref{map:BathyMT}, code \cite{Lemenkova201978}. Conic equal-area Albers projection for map with digitized lines of the profiles (on Fig. 7. Location of the cross-sectioning 25 profiles in \cite{Lemenkova201998}) is plotted by code \cite{Lemenkova201970}.

\pagebreak
\subsection{Seismic mapping} 
\lettrine[lines=3]{\color{NavyBlue}F}{ocal} mechanisms\index[concepts]{earthquake!focal!mechanism} (Fig. \ref{map:PM3}) were drawn using 'psmeca'\index[concepts]{GMT!module!psmeca} \ac{GMT} module that reads dataset values from \ac{ASCII} file\index[concepts]{data!ASCII} and generates a PostScript code plotting focal mechanisms\index[concepts]{earthquake!focal!mechanism}. Centroid moment tensor\index[concepts]{earthquake!centroid moment tensor} solutions are shown for shallow depth earthquakes of $Mw <10$ along the \ac{PSB} margins from 1976 to 2010. Earthquakes\index[concepts]{earthquake} along the tectonic plate subduction zone are shown by \ac{GMT} using data from Global \ac{CMT}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!CMT} Project.

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
 		\includegraphics[width=0.9\textwidth]{Fig-PM3.jpg}
	\caption{Seismic map of the focal mechanisms: \ac{PSB} margins}
\label{map:PM3}       
\end{map}

Focal mechanisms depict a commonly accepted theory of the focal depths of the earthquake, showing a depth from the surface to the earthquake's origin (hypocenter)\index[concepts]{earthquake!hypocenter}. Given dataset contains significant amount of shallow earthquakes of the \ac{PSB}  with focal depths of several tens kilometers (mostly $<$50), intermediate earthquakes with focal depths from 70 to 200 kilometers, and also a few earthquakes of \ac{PSB} with deep focus reaching depths $>$ 500 kilometers, Table \ref{tab:11}. The foci of the most \ac{PSB} earthquakes\index[concepts]{earthquake} are concentrated in the crust and upper mantle, that is, originate in shallow parts of the Earth's interior.

The original dataset of the focal mechanism solutions are from the was taken from the Global \ac{CMT}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!CMT} Catalog, formerly known as the Harvard \ac{CMT}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!CMT} catalog \cite{Ekstrom2012}. Technical parameters are set up as following: 

\begin{itemize}
	\item Moment magnitude $(Mw) <10$; 
	\item Surface wave magnitude $(Ms): <10)$;
	\item Body wave magnitude $(Mb): <10$;  
	\item Data time span: 1976-2010.
\end{itemize}

While magnitude is a widely understood concept, describing the energy of the earthquake release on a logarithmic scale, some technical details are presented in Table \ref{tab:11} and Table \ref{tab:12} used for mapping velocity (Fig. \ref{map:PM4}), assigning and interpreting magnitudes. Introduced in the 1930's, the Richter Scale is the best known scale for measuring the magnitude of earthquakes  \cite{Muller2002}. L-waves (Surface waves) magnitude\index[concepts]{wave!surface (L-waves)!magnitude}, creating the strongest disturbance within the upper layers of the Earth, is computed by the following algebraic Formula \ref{eq:foc}:

\begin{equation}
	MS=\log_10(A/T)+1.66\log_10(D)+3.30
\end{equation}\label{eq:foc}\myequations{Surface wave magnitude (Ms)}

where T is the measured wave period and D is the distance in radians. 

On the contrary, earthquakes occurring deep in the Earth do not generate large surface waves\index[concepts]{wave!surface (L-waves)!magnitude}. Therefore, a body wave magnitude\index[concepts]{wave!body!magnitude} (Mb) is scaled based on the seismic waves\index[concepts]{wave!seismic} penetrating through the Earth's interior (body) \cite{Muller2002}. Body wave magnitude (mb) is measured based on the maximum amplitude \emph{A} by a Formula \ref{eq:mag}:

\begin{equation}
	Mb=\log_10(A/T)+Q(D,h)
\end{equation}\label{eq:mag}\myequations{Body wave magnitude (mb)}

where T is the measured wave period\index[concepts]{wave!period} and Q is an empirical function of focal depth $h$ and epicentral distance $D$ \cite{Muller2002}.

Moment magnitude (MW) relies on an underlying robust physical and mathematical development through converting seismic moment using a formula calibrated to agree with MS over much of its range. Others parameters are taken as default ones. 

Data are collected in both \ac{GMT} 'psvelomeca'\index[concepts]{GMT!module!psvelomeca} and \ac{GMT} 'psmeca'\index[concepts]{GMT!module!psmeca} input formats and tested. Final solution was for \ac{GMT} 'psmeca'\index[concepts]{GMT!module!psmeca} (the reason is for the \ac{GMT} version 5.4.5 compatibility). The focal mechanisms are derived from a solution of the earthquakes' moment tensor, which is estimated by an analysis of the observed seismic wave forms\index[concepts]{wave!seismic}.

	030277B MINDANAO, PHILIPPINE IS & 1977/3/2 &9:53:26.4 GMT & 6.62 & 123.53 & 33.4 & 6.0 & 3.2 \\
\noalign{\smallskip}\hline
\end{tabular}
		}% end scale box
\end{center}
*Half duration; **Centroid \ac{HcT}
\end{table}

Technically, the seismic moment tensor (Harvard \ac{CMT}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!CMT}, with zero trace) was plotted (Fig. \ref{map:PM3}) by 'psmeca' module using the following GMT code snippet: 

gmt psmeca -R CMT.txt -J -Sd0.5/8/u -Gred -L0.1p -Fa/5p/it -Fepurple -Fgmagenta -Ft -W0.1p -Fz -Ewhite -O -K $>>$ \$ps

The scale (0.5) was adjusted to the scaling of the 'beach ball' radius, which is proportional to the magnitude. Here the '-Sd0.5/8/u' implies the scale, label size and annotation placement below the 'beach ball'. Filling of the extensive quadrants was defined by -E parameter; -L parameter defines drawing the 'beach ball' outline of 0.1 pt. Shaded compressional quadrants of the focal mechanism 'beach ball' are colored by -Gred function. The --Fa/5p/it option was used to plot size, P\_axis\_symbol and T\_axis\_symbol, to compute and plot P and T axes with symbols (selected inverse triangle (i) and triangle (t)). 

Focal mechanisms (Fig. \ref{map:PM3}) visualized as commonly accepted in geophysical mapping 'beachballs' show a representation of the lower half of the focal sphere as viewed from above the focus at the earthquake epicenter and respective zones of compression and dilatation. Based on the recorded dataset of the P-waves\index[concepts]{wave!primary (P-waves)}, compressional longitudinal waves, a graphical model shows red areas of the 'beachballs' as upward moving, i.e. compressional motion, while white areas-- a downward, dilatational motion. Determining geological information, such as rupture of the Earth's surface, it depicts two nodal planes, fault and auxiliary ones. For instance, auxiliary plane intersects the line of fault plane between the circle and its center of the 'beachball' with the general rule that the closer the intersection is to the center, the more dominant is the strike slip. Increase of the the curvature of the crossing hemispheres denotes shallower dip. The strike of a nodal plane is measured in degrees around the circle from north to the line of the nodal plane. Based on existing classification \cite{Muller2002}, various focal plane solutions are shown: 

\begin{enumerate}
	\item normal fault striking north, dipping E/W at ca. 45\degree
	\item oblique fault, right or left lateral and reverse slip
	\item oblique fault, reverse slip dominant, with some right/left lateral 
	\item oblique fault, strike slip dominant, attitude ca. N\degree 40W / 70\degree W (right/left lateral), or N35\degree E / 70\degree E (left/right lateral).
\end{enumerate}

Earthquakes event map at \ac{PSB} area (Fig. \ref{map:PM5}) shows prime hypocentres and magnitude values by color and size of the circles, respectively. The map was plotted on the basis of the \href{http://www.isc.ac.uk/isc-ehb/search/catalogue/}{\ac{ISC}-EHB Bulletin}, overlaid on the Google Earth\index[concepts]{Google Earth} map as a file in \ac{KMZ}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!KMZ}, which is a file extension for a placemark file used by Google Earth Pro. The geographic coordinates include x,y,z components in decimal degrees defined by the \ac{WGS84}. The overlay is visualized on Fig. \ref{map:PM5}. The explanation of the map is as follows. Circle size shows magnitude values. Circle color shows hypocenter\index[concepts]{earthquake!hypocenter} depths, km (from red: 0 to dark blue/black: $>600$). 

A multi-source dataset applied for seismic mapping includes a compilation of \ac{GEBCO}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!GEBCO}, \ac{ETOPO1}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!ETOPO1}, \ac{ETOPO5}, \ac{CMT}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!CMT}, \ac{EGM96}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!EGM96}, \ac{GVP} and \ac{GSHHGD}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!GSHHGD} sources. Combined studies of seismic and tectonic settings (focal mechanism, velocity, geological lineaments), volcanic activity and general topography of the ocean seafloor are essential for understanding complicated bathymetry and geomorphic patterns\index[concepts]{geomorphology} of the trenches in the \ac{PSB}. A multi-source \ac{GIS}\index[concepts]{GIS}\index[concepts]{GIS} data analyze on the southern side of the \ac{PSB} and discussion of the current situation and historical evolution of the geology of this area was performed and presented.

\pagebreak
\subsection{Velocity mapping} 

\lettrine[lines=3]{\color{DarkOrchid4}V}{elocity} ellipses with 95\% confidence in rotated convention (red) and in (N,E) convention (green), on a \ac{PSB} region were plotted using 'psvelo'\index[concepts]{GMT!module!psvelo} module of \ac{GMT}, Fig. \ref{map:PM4}. Theoretical background of the best-fitting angular velocities and \ac{MORVEL} is presented by \cite{DeMetsetal2010} who described and modeled geologically current motions of 25 tectonic plates using geologically determined and geodetically\index[concepts]{geodesy} constrained subsets of the global circuit. The datasets include \ac{PVEL} 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
 		\includegraphics[width=0.9\textwidth]{Fig-PM4.jpg}
	\caption{Velocity ellipses at \ac{PSB} margins}
\label{map:PM4}       
\end{map}

Velocity ellipses at \ac{PSB} margins from 1976 to 2010 in (N, E) convention (green) and rotated convention (red).  Rotational wedges (purple 'gear wheels'): by 'psvelo'\index[concepts]{GMT!module!psvelo} module of \ac{GMT}. Data: Global \ac{CMT}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!CMT} Project. Contour: \ac{GEBCO}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!GEBCO} grid. Technical visualization of the velocity map (Fig. \ref{map:PM4}) was performed using following \ac{GMT} code snippet: \\
\begin{itemize}
	\item Velocity ellipses in (N,E) convention:  'gmt psvelo CMT.txt -R -J -W0.3p,green -L -Ggreen -Se0.1/0.95/5 -A0.2p -O -K $>>$ \$ps'. Here, the Vscale gives the scaling of the velocity in inches; the CMT.txt is the dataset; parameters -R and -J indicate the region (in this case coordinates: -R120/152/4/35)
	\item Velocity ellipses in rotated convention: 'gmt psvelo CMT.txt -R -J -W0.3p,red -Sr0.1/0.95/5 -Ggreen -A0.2p -O -K $>>$ \$ps'. Here the 'Sr0.1/0.95/5' is the main \ac{kwargs} showing the Vscale (0.1 inch), Confidence (0.95) and Fontsize (5pt).
	\item Rotational wedges: 'gmt psvelo CMT.txt -R -J -W0.05p -L -Sw0.5/1.e7 -D2 -Gslateblue2 -Elightgray -A0.2p -O -K $>>$ \$ps'. Here, the '-Sw0.5/1.e7' indicates Rotational wedges with \ac{kwargs} as Wedge\_scale and Wedge\_mag. The '-D2' parameter enables to rescale the uncertainties of velocities by sigma\_scale (in this case 2 scale was set up). 
\end{itemize}

The respective red and green circles with confidence ellipses are \ac{PVEL} dataset estimates, which use a plate circuit to estimate subduction across the plate subduction zones in the west Pacific. According to \cite{Demetsetal1990}, relative to the \ac{PSP}, the Pacific Plate rotates conter-clockwise around a pole near the southern end of the plate boundary. The best-fitting angular velocity indicates rapid decrease of the convergence rates southward, from $49\pm 0.7 mm yr^{-1} (1\sigma)$  at the northern end of the \ac{IBT} to $9\pm 0.8 mm yr^{-1}$ of orthogonal subduction along the southern \ac{YT}, south off \ac{MT} \cite{DeMetsetal2010}.

The \ac{PSP}-Pacific plate velocities are estimated to fit the interval $1 mm yr^{1}$ and 2\degree\space along the lithospheric plate boundary \cite{Sellaetal2002}, which reflects the overlap in the \ac{GPS}\index[concepts]{geodesy!GPS} stations used to estimate the motions of the two plates. The existence of the Caroline plate was first proposed by \cite{WeisselAnderson1978} who estimated its motions from a synthesis of marine seismic, bathymetric, and seismologic observations from its boundaries with the Pacific and Philippine plates. Located in the western equatorial Pacific immediately south of the \ac{PSP} (Fig. \ref{map:PM2}), it remains the poorly understood and enigmatic with uncertainties about the style and rate of its present deformation caused by scarcity of reliable kinematic data.

\pagebreak
\subsection{Histogram equalization}
\lettrine[lines=3]{\color{DarkRed}T}{he} smoothness of the grids intensities for \ac{GEBCO}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!GEBCO} and ETOPO1\index[concepts]{cartography!data!ETOPO1} was improved by passing the output of 'grdgradient' to 'grdhisteq' with a case example of the Hikurangi, Puysegur and Hjort trenches. 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=13.0cm]{Fig-HPH-6.jpg}
	\caption{Histogram equalization on the topography grids, ETOPO1}
	\label{map:HPH6}
\end{map}

The histogram equalization was performed by key \ac{GMT} module 'grdhisteq'\index[concepts]{GMT!module!grdhisteq} to enhance visualization of the various ranges of the comparison of two grids (\ac{ETOPO1}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!ETOPO1} and \ac{GEBCO}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!GEBCO}) depending on their frequency distribution (Fig. \ref{map:HPH6} and Fig. \ref{map:HPH7}). Four subplots were generated to find values dividing grid files into equal area patches. The ’grdhisteq’ module performs a histogram equalization of raster images. Using 'grdhisteq', the \ac{ASCII}\index[concepts]{data!ASCII} data values dividing the range of the initial raster data into cells segments was written to an output file. A -C16 argument defines 16 cells. The resulting raster grid has an equal area in the images. Using 'makecpt'\index[concepts]{GMT!module!makecpt} \ac{GMT} module this output was then colored according to the actual bathymetric values by following code: gmt makecpt -Crainbow -T- 9000/3000 > t.cpt. The explanations for the subplot with four maps (Fig. \ref{map:HPH6} for \ac{ETOPO1}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!ETOPO1} and Fig. \ref{map:HPH7} for \ac{GEBCO}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!GEBCO}) is as follows:

\begin{enumerate}
	\item Top left: The original map (top left) visualizes initial raster (artificial cpt ’rainbow’) to highlight changes in elevation;
	\item Top right: equalized raster grid;
	\item Low left: normalized raster grid derived using -N argument that stands for Gaussian output, used to to receive data with smooth Gaussian distribution. The default standard normal scores was used for grid normalization. 
	\item Low right: quadratic equalization 
\end{enumerate}

The subplot on the low right of the presented Figures \ref{map:HPH6} and \ref{map:HPH7} shows quadratic equalization plotted using following snippet: gmt grdhisteq hpt\_relief.nc -Gout.nc -Q. Here, the ’-Q’ argument is output selecting quadratic histogram equalization, unlike the default linear one. The ’-T-9000/3000’ argument means range of the topographic elevations. The image was then visualized using 'grdimage' module by code snippet: \\
	\$gmt grdimage hpt\_relief.nc \\
	-I+a45+nt1 -Ct.cpt -JM3i -Y6i -K -P -Bpxg5f5a10 -Bpyg4f2a8 -Bsxg5 -Bsyg4 -BWSne > \$ps

In this code, the ’-Ct.cpt’ argument passes cpt created in the previous step for visualization; the '-JM3i' arguments explains the Mercator projection\index[concepts]{cartography!map projection!Mercator} with 3 inches width; '- Y6i' argument plots the map with 6 inches distance from the previous one by Y axis; '-K' argument refers to the continuation of the code. The annotation was added using Unix 'echo'\index[concepts]{UNIX!utility!echo} prog: echo $172 -33 Original" | gmt pstext -Rhpt\_relief.nc -J -F+jBL+f12p -T -Gwhite@10 -Dj0.1i -O -K >> \$ps$. 

The 'grdhisteq'\index[concepts]{GMT!module!grdhisteq} \ac{GMT} module enables to write a raster grid with statistics based on cumulative distribution function. That means, after applying the 'grdhisteq'\index[concepts]{GMT!module!grdhisteq} module, the output raster file has relative elevations in the same locations as the input file (that is, x, y). However, the values are modified to reflect their place in cumulative distribution with reference to the initial input file. This illustrates the principle of the equalization of the topographic grids by \ac{GMT} module 'grdhisteq'\index[concepts]{GMT!module!grdhisteq}, as visualized on Fig. \ref{map:HPH6} for \ac{ETOPO1}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!ETOPO1} and Fig. \ref{map:HPH7} for \ac{GEBCO}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!GEBCO}, respectively. The difference in grids intensities for \ac{GEBCO}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!GEBCO} and \ac{ETOPO1}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!ETOPO1} raster grids can be clearly seen on the subplot 4 on quadratic equalization. The grid was smoothed prior to use by applying 'blockmean'\index[concepts]{GMT!module!blockmean} module of the \ac{GMT}: it interpolated the original values in the table by smoothing algorithm. Two other modules were tested as well: 'blockmedian'\index[concepts]{GMT!module!blockmedian} and 'blockmode'\index[concepts]{GMT!module!blockmode} as pre-processors before running surface to avoid aliasing in a grid.

The effect of using different histogram equalization algorithms was examined using subplots for data on \ac{GEBCO}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!GEBCO} and \ac{ETOPO1}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!ETOPO1} covering study area for three trenches (Fig. \ref{map:HPH6} and Fig. \ref{map:HPH7}). Equalization compression for bathymetry grids were tested as comparison for four grids: original, equalized, normalized, quadratic algorithms. The results for this step are demonstrated in Fig. \ref{map:HPH6} and Fig. \ref{map:HPH7} showing a visual summary of a grid equalization by different \ac{GMT} algorithms. Consistent cpt across all raster dataset grids where applied for better comparison. Differences can be seen between the values calculated at quadratic equalization at \ac{ETOPO1} and \ac{GEBCO}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!GEBCO}. 
\index[concepts]{cartography!data!ETOPO1}
From the analysis of histogram equalization algorithms it is notable that there are variations in the grids values obtained depending on the method: equalized, normalized, quadratic algorithms. This in itself means that the topic is worthy of further elaboration. Through the demonstrated examples (Fig. \ref{map:HPH6} and Fig. \ref{map:HPH7}) the effects of using different algorithms varying with raster dataset grids equalization can be notable. 
	
\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=13.0cm]{Fig-HPH-7.jpg}
	\caption{Histogram equalization on the topography grids, GEBCO}
	\label{map:HPH7}
\end{map}

\pagebreak
\subsection{Geological mapping}
\lettrine[lines=3]{\color{DarkSlateGrey}G}{eological} mapping (example of the \ac{KKT} shown on Map \ref{map:KKT2}) is based on the use of the sequence of the \ac{GMT} modules, the most important of which are the following. Example of the geological mapping of the hadal trench using \ac{QGIS}\index[concepts]{GIS}\index[concepts]{GIS!QGIS} with a case study of the \ac{MT}, the deepest trench on the Earth includes application of R programming and various statistical approaches towards data modelling. The scope of the research is more focused on the geological assessment than statistical analysis. Therefore, the special attention was paid to mapping geological lineaments, faults, trench extent, location of the \ac{LIPs}, volcanic spots and earthquakes in the \ac{KKT} (Map \ref{map:KKT2}). Various depth of the earthquakes are presented in different colors, as well as fracture zones, ophiolites, magnetic anomalies and extension of the tectonic slabs (Map \ref{map:KKT2}).

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=10cm]{Fig-KKT2.jpg}
	\caption{Kuril-Kamchatka Trench: map of geologic settings, \cite{Lemenkova201957}, \cite{Lemenkova202001}}\label{map:KKT2}
\end{map}

The geological layers (lines and points) were added on the thematic geological maps. Plotting of the vector elements on the map is done by '-W' command, e.g. '-Wthinnest,red'. Each code is saved to the initially created file using >>ps command. The \nth{1} mention of this command goes with single bracket, e.g. '> \$ps'. Then all the elements added to the map overlay the same. In this sense, there is certain similarity with \ac{GIS}\index[concepts]{GIS} layers and a sequence of codes in the whole GMT script, \cite{Lemenkova201958} (here: example for the \ac{MT}). The initial files with extension gmt (e.g. ridge.gmt) are tables with attribute data (coordinates and values) in native GMT format. Example of the geological mappings for the Kermadec-Tonga region is on Map \ref{map:TK2}, right, \ac{KKT} (Map \ref{map:KKT2}). GMT module ‘psxy’ was used for adding annotations on the maps, plotting geologic lineaments and sample points. The ‘psbasemap’ and ‘grdcontour’ GMT modules were used to map base map elements: bathymetry contours, net grids, titles and subtitles. The full GMT codes for geological mapping are presented in Appendix, for contour mapping \ref{GMT:03}, regional mapping \ref{GMT:06} and global geologic map \ref{GMT:05}.

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\pagebreak
\subsection{Geoid modelling}
\lettrine[lines=3]{\color{DarkCyan}T}{he} geoid model\index[concepts]{geoid} is a complex function that depends on various geophysical phenomena, such as permanent Earth tide, postglacial land uplift, sea level changes, polar drift and other parameters \cite{Ekman1995}, \cite{Bursa}, \cite{Heiskanen}. The geoid\index[concepts]{geoid} model was analyzed to determine the oceanographic properties of the study area: a mathematical shape that of the ocean surface on the Earth with the sole impact of the gravity and rotation, with absent other influence (e.g. winds and tides). A modeled geoid\index[concepts]{geoid} presents a smooth yet irregular surface with visualized shape resulted from the uneven distribution of the mass on the Earth's surface. 

The sequence of \ac{GMT} modules (gmtset\index[concepts]{GMT!module!gmtset}, grd2cpt\index[concepts]{GMT!module!grd2cpt}, grdimage\index[concepts]{GMT!module!grdimage}, pscoast\index[concepts]{GMT!module!pscoast}, grdcontour\index[concepts]{GMT!module!grdcontour}, psbasemap\index[concepts]{GMT!module!psbasemap}, psscale\index[concepts]{GMT!module!psscale}, psimage\index[concepts]{GMT!module!psimage}, logo\index[concepts]{GMT!module!logo}, pstext\index[concepts]{GMT!module!pstext}, psconvert\index[concepts]{GMT!module!psconvert}) was used to map the geoid model\index[concepts]{geoid} for the trench by code \cite{Lemenkova201953} (here: example for the Mariana Trench) and \cite{Lemenkova201954} (here: example for the World global coverage) areas for plotting contours of the terrestrial and water areas, geoid\index[concepts]{geoid} and bathymetry, net grids and basic cartographic elements such as titles, scales, annotations. Each \ac{GMT} module consists of the set of code lines, which, combined together as a script produce and visualize maps. 

The geoid\index[concepts]{geoid} modelling of the \ac{RT} (Map \ref{map:R3}) was done using following code\index[concepts]{cartography!data!EGM96}: gmt grdimage geoid.egm96.grd -I+a45+nt1 -R120/134/20/33 - JD127/24/27/30/6i -Cgeoid.cpt -P -K > \$ps Example of modelling geoid of \ac{MAT} area is on Map \ref{map:MAT4}. The additional cartographic elements were added using GMT basemap module: grid, title, coastline and GMT pscoast module. The full script is presented in Appendix \ref{GMT:04}.

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=10cm]{Fig-MAT4.jpg}
	\caption{Middle America Trench: geoid model}\label{map:MAT4}
\end{map}

There are various existing gravity and geoid\index[concepts]{geoid} concepts that originate from differing numerical applications and approaches in processing of the permanent Earth’s tide \cite{Ekman1989}. Modelling geoid\index[concepts]{geoid} and gravity was plotted using sequence of several \ac{GMT} modules (to be more specific: img2grd, grd2cpt, grdimage, pscoast, makecpt, grdcontour and psbasemap) based on the existing methodology \cite{Lemenkova201994}, \cite{Lemenkova201995}. The data are global marine gravity model from CryoSat-2, Jason-1, free-air gravity\index[concepts]{gravity!anomaly!marine free-air} grids, Scripps Institution of Oceanography \cite{Sandwelletal2014}: grav/grav\_27.1.img and grav/curv\_27.1.img via topex.ucsd.edu.

The geoid\index[concepts]{geoid} data grids used for current study and retrieved from the website \href{https://earth-info.nga.mil/GandG/wgs84/ gravitymod/egm96/egm96.html}{geoid data grids} are based on the \ac{EGM96}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!EGM96} with n=m=360 and WGS 84 reference system \cite{Malys1996}: geoid.egm96.grd. The \ac{EGM96} has reference ellipsoid the same as WGS 84, but higher spatial resolution and more accurate geoid\index[concepts]{geoid}. The model is based on the fully-normalized, unitless spherical harmonic coefficients. The data are made public by \ac{NIMA}, the \ac{NASA} \ac{GSFC}, and the Ohio State University. 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=10cm]{Fig-Clip-MT.jpg}
	\caption{Mariana Trench: color geoid image with grey-shaded topography of the clipped areas, code \cite{Lemenkova201980}}\label{map:Clip-MT}
\end{map}

The \ac{EGM96} geopotential model\index[concepts]{cartography!data!EGM96} is a spherical harmonic model of the Earth’s gravitational potential complete to degree and order 360 (Lemoine et al., 1998). The \ac{EGM96} contains fully-normalized, unitless spherical harmonic coefficients and their standard deviations for a gravitational model from 2\degree, to 360\degree. These coefficients are consistent with scaling values of gravitational models \cite{Rapp1994}. The data are based on the surface gravity data covering various regions of the globe including southern hemisphere. 

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
%\pagebreak
\subsection{3D mesh grid modelling}
\lettrine[lines=3]{\color{MidnightBlue}T}{he} 3D\index[concepts]{plot!3D} mesh gridding aimed to visualize and highlight the topographic and bathymetric landforms and patterns of the region of the trenches, through dividing the input grid model into smaller shapes formed after discretization of the geometric model of the area. 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=10cm]{Fig-MAT3.jpg}
	\caption{Middle America Trench: 3D topographic mesh model map}\label{map:MAT3}
\end{map}

Hence, the purpose of the 3D-mesh\index[concepts]{plot!3D} gridding (example for the \ac{MT}: Map \ref{map:MAT3}) was to create an overlay that highlights the geomorphic surface\index[concepts]{geomorphology} of each of the trenches and adjusting continental steep slope. Therefore, unlike in case of bathymetric mapping (Fig. \ref{map:MAT1}) where the data were taken as (\ac{ETOPO1}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!ETOPO1}, lower resolution grid was used (\ac{ETOPO5}). This was done to avoid small-scale irregularities possible in case of the high-resolution data which tend to visually overload the derived topographic attributes, making 3D\index[concepts]{plot!3D} mesh overlay visually complex. This allowed a smoother visualization of the 3D-mesh polygons by removing unnecessary details in 3D\index[concepts]{plot!3D} plotting using code \cite{Lemenkova201948} (example for Kuril-Kamchatka Trench). 

The 3D modelling consists in the following steps. the data were initially extracted from the ETOPO5 grid using the following (snippet for the Vanuatu): gmt grdcut earth\_relief\_05m.grd -R160/170/-25/-10 -Gvv3d\_relief.nc. The ETOPO5 was selected to plot the 3D with aim to reduce the grid size and hence to save computer memory space. 
The next step of the 3D\index[concepts]{plot!3D} modelling is a calculation of the regular 3D grid mesh using \ac{GMT} parameters of the 'grdview' module. The dimension of the grid was chosen in a way which ensures that every point of the trench axis is located within the grid boundaries. For example, the coordinates for Vityaz were selected as 163\degree 173\degree -15\degree 0\degree and for Vanuatu: 160\degree 170\degree -25\degree -10\degree\space. Visualization parameters of the 3D models can be summarized as follows. Grid extent was in 160/45 azimuthal direction rotation with the grid ranges from 160\degree to 173\degree for both grids, in latitudinal direction from -25\degree to 0\degree, and in vertical direction from -9,000 km depths up to 1000 km. On 'Z' argument the grid was extended by 4cm as a visual distance between the 3D and basemap 2D\index[concepts]{plot!2D}, to enable interpreted them better. The 2D\index[concepts]{plot!2D} mesh was divided into small simple polygons with curved contour lines and color palette highlighting the topography. The 3D\index[concepts]{plot!3D} map was then overlaid with 2D geoid\index[concepts]{geoid}. 

The background geoid\index[concepts]{geoid} contour (a plane grayscale map below the map, Map \ref{map:MAT3}) was plotted using \ac{GMT} module 'grdcontour'\index[concepts]{GMT!module!grdcontour}. The geoid field data of the 2D\index[concepts]{plot!2D} map were taken from the \ac{ETOPO1}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!ETOPO1} dataset are given by following code (here: Vanuatu): $gmt grdcontour earth\_relief\_01m.grd -JM6.0c -R160/170/-25/-10 -p160/45 -C500 -Gd6c -Wthinnest,dimgray -Y5.5c -P -K > \$ps$. In order to ensure that grids are visually depict geomorphic shapes\index[concepts]{geomorphology} of the surfaces, the '-Qsm' argument was selected to specify 's' for the surface plot, and 'm' to have mesh lines drawn on top of surface. For the vertical component, an option '-N-9000+glightgray' was selected to draw a plane at the -9,000 m level of bathymetry. Final 3D model of the two subplots is shown on Fig. \ref{map:VV7}. The contour lines are given by every one with annotations at every five (-C1 -A5). The rotation of the 3D\index[concepts]{plot!3D} mesh grid was chosen at the angle most corresponding to the visualization of the trenches, after several trials at different angles, depending on the oblique rotation and depth by code \cite{Lemenkova201949} (here: example for Kuril-Kamchatka Trench, azimuth 165\degree). A plane was drawn at -7500 m s z-level with dimgray color provided via the +g modifier to highlight the frontal facade between the 3D plane and the data perimeter and -Wf0.1p,red setting used for the pen outline, \cite{Lemenkova201950}. 

After the data were compared and optimal resolution selected, the rotation azimuth was set as -p165/30$^{\circ}$ with an overlay distance from the  geoid\index[concepts]{geoid} contour at 4,5 cm. As a result of the combination of several \ac{GMT} modules, 3D mesh model of the trenches (example of the Aleutian: Fig. \ref{map:A3}) was computed and visualized. They show the 3D\index[concepts]{plot!3D} model rotated in 165/45\degree overlapping the contour map of the geoid\index[concepts]{geoid}. Several \ac{GMT} modules were used, e.g. 'pscoast'\index[concepts]{GMT!module!pscoast} module used for mapping coastal lines and contour, 'psscale'\index[concepts]{GMT!module!psscale} module was used for mapping color legend\index[concepts]{cartography!map element!legend} strip showing visual colors, 'pstext'\index[concepts]{GMT!module!pstext} module was used to add textual explanations on the map. The \ac{NetCDF} grid format \cite{RewDavis1990} was processed as a grid file. The cartographic routine include adding elements on the map: plotting ticks, selecting lines for grids, adding directional rose\index[concepts]{cartography!map element!directional rose} and legend\index[concepts]{cartography!map element!legend}, selecting proper projection\index[concepts]{cartography!map projection}. Topographic data were collected rom the \ac{ETOPO5} covering seafloor bathymetry and coastal topography of the area of each trench. The modelling was performed using \ac{GMT} module 'grdview' followed by a sequence of the \ac{GMT} modules: grdcut\index[concepts]{GMT!module!grdcut}, grd2cpt\index[concepts]{GMT!module!grd2cpt}, grdcontour\index[concepts]{GMT!module!grdcontour}, pscoast\index[concepts]{GMT!module!pscoast}, grdview\index[concepts]{GMT!module!grdview}, logo\index[concepts]{GMT!module!logo}, psconvert\index[concepts]{GMT!module!psconvert}. Additionally, some UNIX progs (rm\index[concepts]{UNIX!utility!rm}, echo\index[concepts]{UNIX!utility!echo}) were used. The output 3D model is presented at (Map \ref{map:MAT3}). The full GMT code is available in Appendix, \ref{GMT:02}.

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
%\pagebreak
\subsection{Gravity modelling}
\lettrine[lines=3]{\color{Blue4}G}{ravity} model is used to support geophysical investigations, geological prognosis as well as geological resources management. Here the \ac{GMT} script is presented for modelling gravity from m the 'grdimage' \ac{GMT} module, and 'img2grd'\index[concepts]{GMT!module!img2grd} module was used for the data conversion. The combination between the \ac{GMT} modules and cartographic features enables to properly visualize thematic map by the \ac{GMT} tools. The essential \ac{GMT} elements include selecting map projections\index[concepts]{cartography!map projection}, fonts, map layout, grid, adding elements on the map. Gravity data showing free-air gravity anomalies\index[concepts]{gravity!anomaly!marine free-air} covering study area including terrestrial and marine regions, were taken from the data sets based on the 1 arc-minute resolution CryoSat-2 and Jason-1 satellite-derived raster grids described in detailed in relevant studies \cite{Sandwelletal1995}, \cite{Sandwelletal2013}, \cite{SmithSandwell1997b}, \cite{Sandwelletal2014}, for the trenches and surrounding regions. Gravity data revealed ground information for the geologic and geophysical settings of the study area, as free-air gravity anomalies\index[concepts]{gravity!anomaly!marine free-air} reflect subsurface density variations \cite{Dooetal2018}. The gravity map was plotted using \ac{GMT} following code (here example for the \ac{RT}): 

gmt grdimage gravRT.grd -I+a45+nt1 -R105/123/8/24 -JY114/12/6.5i - CgravRT.cpt -P -K > \$ps. 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=10cm]{Fig-MAT6.jpg}
	\caption{Middle America Trench: gravity model map}\label{map:MAT6}
\end{map}

Here the -R function shows the selected region, the JY114/12/6.5i stands for the cylindrical equal-area Gall-Peters projection\index[concepts]{cartography!map projection!Gall-Peters} and 6,5 inches the overall dimensions of the map; -I+a45+nt1 shows the illumination parameters and -CgravRT.cpt selects the color palette created on the previous step. 

Chosen color palette (gravRT.cpt)  shows the transition from lower (red) values (loser than 20 mGal) to higher values (bright blue) gravity values (over 90 mGal). The gravity modelling (example: Fig. \ref{map:A5}) was performed using the following script. The example of the \ac{RT} is on Map \ref{map:R4}, \ac{MAT}: Map \ref{map:MAT6} and Map \ref{map:MAT7}. The full GMT code of modelling gravity is available in Appendix, \ref{GMT:07}.

Gravity and bathymetric data were examined to obtain a visualization and better understanding of the seafloor fabric and structures within the \ac{PSB}. The \ac{EGM96}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!EGM96} gravity grid was visualized based on the with the satellite altimetry data \cite{SmithSandwell1997a} producing gravity map of the \ac{PSB} (Fig. \ref{map:PM7}). The subset of the img file was extracted through the following \ac{GMT} shell script (example for the Kuril-Kamchatka Trench): Appendix, \ref{GMT:07}, \cite{Lemenkova201959} (Vertical gravity for Kuril-Kamchatka Trench), \cite{Lemenkova201960} (Vertical gravity for Mariana Trench), \cite{Lemenkova201961} (gravity for Kuril-Kamchatka Trench), \cite{Lemenkova201962} (gravity for Mariana Trench). 

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\pagebreak
\subsection{Automatic digitizing of the cross-section transecting profiles}
\lettrine[lines=3]{\color{OrangeRed4}C}{ross-section} profiling is a common technique used in geosciences to receive a sample of the selected segment in the study area and analyze variability of the studied elements along the track. Examples of the cross-section area presented: \ac{DEM} cross-profile \cite{Yuliantoetal2019}. Semi-automated methods have been explored in cartography, driven largely by the advantages of using machine-learning algorithms in cartographic routine, thus minimising handmade routine, subjectivity in processes of the digitizing and vectorizing of the bathymetric data. 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=12cm]{Fig-IBT6.jpg}
	\caption{Izu-Bonin Trench: Digitizing cross-section profiles}\label{map:IBT6}
\end{map}

Example of such works is presented by \cite{SchenkeLemenkova2008}. The \ac{GMT} proposes more advanced solution in the automatization of the cross-sections through \ac{ML}\index[concepts]{Artificial Intelligence!Machine Learning} based digitizing that results in a series of the profile transects across the trench \cite{WesselSmith2018}. Two methods were tested in visualizing the profile by \ac{GMT} arguments: median '-Sm'  and the mean '-Sa'. Of these two, median was selected as the best one representing the empirical nature of the trench's shape. Although mean method shows fine interpolation accuracy, median is more favorable, since visual representation of the trench lies within a reasonable reflection of the submarine geomorphology\index[concepts]{geomorphology}. 

The geomorphological modelling consisted in plotting sets of the cross-section orthogonal profiles in perpendicular direction to the main trench axis, and then comparing their parameters for each of the 20 trenches. Plotting cross-sectional profiles, or transects along the selected segment of the study area is a common technique in geological investigations. Examples of the geological cross-section profiles plotting and data analysis can be seen in \cite{RossShor1965}, \cite{Mooreetal1982}. In this study, the orthogonal cross-section bathymetric profiles were drawn automatically using presented \ac{GMT} script across the trenches. Because the scope of the current work is rather large (20 trenches of the Pacific Ocean), the automatization of the process was crucial point in the methodology and a key decision for selecting \ac{GMT}. 

To analyze the geomorphology\index[concepts]{geomorphology} of the trenches, the method developed by \cite{WesselSmith1991} was adapted in this study where the original focus was set on the modelling terrains, since the oceanic trenches have a similar geomorphic setting with reverse general shape form. In this method, the observed elevations, Z, along the cross-section transects is read into a table, using \ac{GMT} 'grdtrack' module along the selected segment of the trench with track 400 km long, spaced 20 km and samples taken 2km along the corresponding line \cite{Lemenkova201994}; \cite{Lemenkova201995}. These recorded bathymetric measurements are then read into the input table from which a graph was plotted denoting the profile shapes (example for the Philippine and \ac{MT}: Fig. \ref{map:PM12} and Fig. \ref{map:PM14}). 

The profiles have length of 400 km, that is from -200 to +200 km from the trench axis, spaced 20 km, sampled every 2 km to achieve evenness of the sampling for the trenches. The depths values were calculated by each profile, and their mean and median values were modeled thereafter. Mean values are notable for smoothing the splines rather as a mathematical functions which lesser reflects the geographic phenomena of the objects. The uncertainties of the actual geomorphology\index[concepts]{geomorphology} are expected to exceed the theoretical interpolation of the actual geomorphology\index[concepts]{geomorphology} represented on the graphs. Hence the shape of the trenches is highlighted as median using ’-Sm’ argument, example is visualized by red line on Fig. \ref{map:HPH10}. 

The combination of several GMT modules was used for mapping geomorphological profiles, as shown in GMT code \cite{Lemenkova201963} (example for the KKT, north), \cite{Lemenkova201964} (example for the KKT, south). Plotting and stacking of the profiles was done using using 'grdtrack' module of \ac{GMT} by statistical mean, through the following main lines of the code based on the 'tkt\_relief.nc' raster in \ac{NetCDF} format. Minor \ac{GMT} modules used for plotting cartographic elements included 'psxy'\index[concepts]{GMT!module!psxy}, 'gmtlogo'\index[concepts]{GMT!module!gmtlogo}, 'psconvert'\index[concepts]{GMT!module!psconvert}, as well as Unix 'echo'\index[concepts]{UNIX!utility!echo} utility. 
For example, with a case of the Philippine Trench, the target area for the  was selected off Mindanao Island, the \nth{2} largest island in the Philippines, situated in the southern part of the archipelago. The \ac{AOI} square is 126.62\degree E 10.7\degree N 127.27\degree E 8.0\degree N. The \ac{AOI} for the \ac{MT} is located in the southwestern part of the crescent (Fig. \ref{map:PM1}) and is bounded on the east by the Guam Island and on the south by the \ac{YT} (Fig. \ref{map:PM2}) encircled by the target square of 141.2\degree E 11.0\degree E 143.8\degree E 11.65\degree N, capturing the Challenger Basin. 

A sequence of the GMT codes was used for plotting transects as follows (example for Puysegur). A 'cat' Unix 'cat' utility was used to plot target coordinates. The line of transect with start and end points was plotted using 'psxy'\index[concepts]{GMT!module!psxy} module. Generating cross-track profiles 400 km long, spaced 20 km, sampled every 2 km and saving them in a table (here: tableP.txt) was done using GMT code (Appendix \ref{GMT:08}). Other examples of modelling and digitizing a series of the cross-section profiles along the two trenches: Hikurangi and Puysegur is presented on Fig. \ref{map:HPH8}, performed using \ac{GMT} and to plot the graphs using retrieved tables (Fig. \ref{map:HPH9}). Example is presented on Fig. \ref{map:TK2}, left. The trench segment and end points were plotted and profiles written into table with modelling done at the next step. 

The annotations were added using Unix utility 'echo'\index[concepts]{UNIX!utility!echo}. The advantage of the \ac{GMT} for the automated profiles digitizing lies in the increased precision and speed of the plotting and highlighted submarine geomorphic features\index[concepts]{geomorphology} of the trench. For the case of \ac{MAT} (Map \ref{map:MAT10}), the cross-section  profiles were taken through the key sea floor features at coordinates 268.4$^{\circ}$W 13.0$^{\circ}$N 271.9$^{\circ}$W 11.6$^{\circ}$N to show the variety of the steepness angles. Full \ac{GMT} \ac{bash}\index[concepts]{UNIX!bash} code and characterization of the module options and elements in comments including UNIX progs (echo\index[concepts]{UNIX!utility!echo}, rm\index[concepts]{UNIX!utility!rm}, cat\index[concepts]{UNIX!utility!cat}) are provided in Appendix, \ref{GMT:08}.

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
%\pagebreak
\subsection{Modelling 2D statistical histograms}
\lettrine[lines=3]{\color{Orchid4}S}{tatistical} data modelling in geology is an important tool for the data analysis \cite{Davis2002}, \cite{Cressie1991}. The statistical techniques of the data analysis are widely used in geospatial studies, applied and described in previous works: \cite{MyersWell2003}; \cite{Ciaccioetal2012}; \cite{MarquesdeSa2007}; \cite{Robertsetal2018}. Proper data interpretation always include testing several algorithms, selecting appropriate approaches and statistical packages \cite{Jonesetal2014}. This thesis applied the histogram techniques showing data distribution (depths), along the profiles. Unlike traditional statistical data analysis that utilizes standard tools for this purpose, such as R and Python \cite{BilinaLawford2009}; \cite{Gill2001}, MATLAB/Octave, \ac{SPSS}\index[concepts]{statistics!SPSS} IBM Statistics \cite{MarquesdeSa2007}, \cite{Lemenkova201990}, Gretl \cite{Lemenkova201988}, this research demonstrated the possibility to perform statistics directly from the cartographic \ac{GMT} toolset using embedded statistical modules. 

\begin{figure}[H]
	\centering
	\includegraphics[width=12cm]{Fig-KKT8.jpg}
	\caption{Kuril-Kamchatka Trench: 2D histograms and rose diagrams of depths \cite{Lemenkova202001}}\label{fig:KKT8}
\end{figure}

Plotting statistical 2D\index[concepts]{plot!2D} histograms and rose diagrams  was done using GMT code by 'pshistogram'\index[concepts]{GMT!module!pshistogram} and 'psrose'\index[concepts]{GMT!module!psrose} modules, respectively (\ac{KKT}: Fig. \ref{fig:KKT8}). It shows statistical 2D histograms and rose diagrams for comparative analysis of depths distribution in the southern and northern part of the \ac{KKT}. Rose diagram shows the aspect of the slopes. Modelling digitized cross-section profiles was followed by a comparison of the two sets either in southern and northern segments of one trench, or comparison of the two neighboring trenches. The statistical modelling and digitizing cross-section profiles were done using \ac{GMT} modules ‘pshistogram\index[concepts]{GMT!module!pshistogram}; and 'grdtrack'\index[concepts]{GMT!module!grdtrack}. The statistics by histograms was performed using a sequence of the GMT modules: gmtset\index[concepts]{GMT!module!gmtset}, psrose\index[concepts]{GMT!module!psrose}, pshistogram\index[concepts]{GMT!module!pshistogram}, pslegend\index[concepts]{GMT!module!pslegend}, logo\index[concepts]{GMT!module!logo} and psconvert\index[concepts]{GMT!module!psconvert}. The histogram plotting demonstrated the frequency of the data distribution against bathymetric variables. The full GMT code of modelling 2D\index[concepts]{plot!2D} statistical histograms and rose diagrams is presented in \cite{Lemenkova201985} and \cite{Lemenkova201986} and in Appendix, \ref{GMT:12}.

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
%\pagebreak
\subsection{Modelling 3D statistical histograms}
\lettrine[lines=3]{\color{Plum4}E}{xample} of the 3D modelling of the histograms is presented on Fig. \ref{fig:KKT3} (here: the \ac{KKT}). The full GMT code of modelling 3D statistical histograms is presented in \cite{Lemenkova201987} and available in Appendix, \ref{GMT:13}.

\begin{figure}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=10cm]{Fig-KKT3.jpg}
	\caption{Kuril-Kamchatka Trench and Sea of Okhotsk: 3D histograms: depths distribution \cite{Lemenkova202001}}\label{fig:KKT3}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Modelling statistical trends of the profiles gradient}
\lettrine[lines=3]{\color{SlateGray4}V}{arious} approaches in modelling trends are presented to show the variety of the mathematical algorithms embedded in the \ac{GMT} tool set. The trend curves were modeled using ‘trend1d’ module by the following code: gmt trend1d -Fxm stackRTn.txt -Np1 > model.txt
Using generated table the statistical data processing were performed: modeling trend regression for the cross-section profiles (Fig. \ref{fig:MAT11}), computing trends for the profiles and plotting two types of the statistical histograms, that is rose diagram and standard box histogram (Fig. \ref{fig:MAT12}). A sequence of the codes was applied to model of the mathematical approximation of the slope gradient trends by profiles (example is visualized on Fig. \ref{fig:TK4}). For example, the basic LS line shown on the Fig. \ref{fig:TK4}, E (lower left) was plotted using following methodology combining \ac{GMT} and Unix utilities. Four types fo the mathematical models were used to analyze the curvature of the profiles, GMT regression analysis Formula \ref{eq:1}:  

\begin{equation}
	 m_{2}t = a + b*t
\end{equation}\label{eq:1}\myequations{GMT regression analysis formula (1)}

GMT regression analysis Formula \ref{eq:2}:
 
\begin{equation}
	m_{3}t = a + b*t + c*t^{2}
\end{equation}\label{eq:2}\myequations{GMT regression analysis formula (2)}

\begin{figure}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=10cm]{Fig-MAT11.jpg}
	\caption{Middle America Trench: trend regression models for the cross-section profiles}\label{fig:MAT11}
\end{figure}

GMT regression analysis Formula \ref{eq:3}: 

\begin{equation}
	m_{5}t = a + b*t + c*t^2 + d* \cos2 \pi *t + e*\sin2* \pi *t
\end{equation}\label{eq:3}\myequations{GMT regression analysis formula (3)}

GMT regression analysis Formula \ref{eq:4}: 

\begin{equation}
	\epsilon(t) = y(t) - m_{5}t 
\end{equation}\label{eq:4}\myequations{GMT plotting residuals formula (4)}

Example of the resulting graph of the trend regression models for the \ac{MAT} was plotted on Fig. \ref{fig:MAT11}. The GMT scripts used for the cartographic visualization and modelling of the statistical trends are based on codes \cite{Lemenkova201983} and \cite{Lemenkova201984} and provided in Appendix, \ref{GMT:11}.

%\pagebreak
\subsection{Surface modelling}
\lettrine[lines=3]{\color{Sepia}T}{he} dataset of this research was augmented by free \ac{ASCII}\index[concepts]{data!ASCII} data on gravity and topography as xyz table, collected from the available source in a table format: \href{http://topex.ucsd.edu/cgi-bin/get_data.cgi}{USGS}. The data range was set to the following coordinates square (here with a systems of 0-360; 0-90 rotation): Lon 263$^{\circ}$/278$^{\circ}$; Lat 7$^{\circ}$/17$^{\circ}$. According to the gravity data inspection (gmt info grav\_MAT.xyz), the range for the data is as follows: total number of points = 554115; minimal value is -157.1 mGal at point with coordinates 263.0083$^{\circ}$ 7.0076$^{\circ}$; maximal value is 452.4 mGal at coordinate 278.0083$^{\circ}$ W/ 17.0032$^{\circ}$ N. Based on this, the color palette was adjusted accordingly to the data range: gmt makecpt -Ccyclic.cpt -V -T-160/455 > surface.cpt where -T-160/455 shows the min/max data. Thereafter, before modelling surface, the data were interpolated for the correction for the source data by generated 1 by 1 minute block for the mean values from the raw \ac{ASCII}\index[concepts]{data!ASCII} xyg table. Afterwards the data were processed by 'gmt surface grav\_MAT\_BM.xyg -R263/278/7/17 -T0.25 -I30s -GSurface\_MAT.nc -Vv' command, which modeled the surface from the xyz data. Both topographic surface modelling (Fig. \ref{map:MAT8}) and gravity surface mapping (Fig. \ref{map:MAT9}) are based on the modelling numerical \ac{ASCII}\index[concepts]{data!ASCII} data in xyz format to the \ac{NetCDF} format  \cite{RewDavis1990}. 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=10cm]{Fig-MAT9.jpg}
	\caption{Middle America Trench: gravity surface map modeled by ASCII data}\label{map:MAT9}
\end{map}

Gradient illumination was added to intensify the surface with azimuth 45$^{\circ}$ using gmt 'grdgradient'\index[concepts]{GMT!module!grdgradient} module with shading azimuth gradient 45$^{\circ}$. It highlighted topographic pits, furrows and submarine landforms on the seafloor of the \ac{MAT}. The data were coded by the cyclic color palette (cyclic.cpt) with the resulting image: Fig. \ref{map:MAT9}. Raster image output was made by GMT code 'gmt grdimage Surface\_MAT.nc -Csurface.cpt -R263/278/7/17 -JM6i -P -ISurface\_MAT.int -Xc -K > ps'. Additional cartographic elements (ticks, legend\index[concepts]{cartography!map element!legend}, scale bar\index[concepts]{cartography!map element!scale bar}, grid ticks\index[concepts]{cartography!map element!grid ticks}, directional rose\index[concepts]{cartography!map element!directional rose}, etc) were added by GMT modules. The 1 m bathymetry and xyz grids were interpolated to 30-sec m resolution and grids derived from bathymetry and gravity were visualized. Gravity contours (mGal) were derived with tension factor of the curvature splines at 0,25. Final data were exported as a 30-sec resolution grid spacing in \ac{NetCDF} grid. Full GMT code for surface modelling (codes for gravity surface: \cite{Lemenkova201972}, \cite{Lemenkova201973}; codes for topographic surface: \cite{Lemenkova201974}, \cite{Lemenkova201975}) of the xyz \ac{ASCII}\index[concepts]{data!ASCII} data is available in \ref{GMT:09} and \ref{GMT:10}.

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=11cm]{Fig-A10.jpg}
	\caption{Aleutian Trench: surface modelling from the ASCII xyz data}\label{map:A10}
\end{map}

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=11cm]{Fig-A5.jpg}
	\caption{Aleutian Trench and Aleutian Islands: gravimetry map}\label{map:A5}
\end{map}

\section{AWK}\label{ssec:AWK}

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Advantages of AWK}
AWK, a domain-specific programming language designed for the text processing, was used in this dissertation for table reshaping. The AWK programming language was created at Bell Labs in the 1970s \cite{Aho} and its name is derived from the surnames of its authors: Alfred Aho, Peter Weinberger, and Brian Kernighan. The GNU implementation of AWK is called GAWK. The available documentation and information on AWK was used extensively in this research \cite{Stutz}, \cite{Ahoetal1988}, \cite{Lieberman}. AWK language is highly suitable for sorting multi-field records in a table due to its specially designed syntax and embedded algorithms. Other possibilities include managing small databases, generate reports based on tables, validating data, producing indexes, performing document-preparation tasks, extracting pieces of data for further processing, sorting data \cite{Robbins2019}. 

AWK is a data-driven scripting language and a powerful tool for data extraction integrated with commands of Unix operating system and can be run from the console. It consists of a set of codes-actions that can be taken against to operate with streams of table data \cite{Robbins2000b}. AWK enables to extract or transform text, produce formatted records \cite{Robbins2001}. It uses string datatype, associative arrays indexed by key strings, and regular expressions \cite{Robbins2001b}. Based on this, AWK was selected as a powerful advanced tool for the processing of tables.
