\subsection{Python libraries: Matplotlib, NumPy, SciPy, Seaborn, StatsModels, Bokeh}\label{ssec:PyMpl01}
General functioning of Python is well described in the existing references and manuals \cite{Oliphant2007}; \cite{Pedregosaetal2011}; \cite{PerezGranger2007}. Using Python libraries enables to process large marine geological datasets effectively, to apply advanced computation algorithms,  to create namespaces while working with modules. Python’s modules contain packed classes, objects, functions and constants. 

Processing geodata received from the attribute tables as vector layers was performed by Python libraries. Necessary installed libraries include Matplotlib\index[concepts]{programming language!Python!Matplotlib}, \ac{NumPy}\index[concepts]{programming language!Python!NumPy} and \ac{SciPy}\index[concepts]{programming language!Python!SciPy}. The \ac{SciPy} library has many modules used for numerical computations: statistical data transformation, simulation, modeling, visualization by \ac{ML}\index[concepts]{Artificial Intelligence!Machine Learning} algorithms. The Matplotlib\index[concepts]{programming language!Python!Matplotlib} library is the main library used for statistical data processing \cite{Hunter2007}, visualization and plotting. Its installation was done using command via \ac{bash} terminal (example for Matplotlib): \$ python3 -m pip install matplotlib. 

The next step included installing certificates to enable connections to the websites containing data frames in Python 3.7.1 on the Mac OS Mojave 10.14.3. This step was necessary, because the Python 3.x has no certificates. This action required a post-install step installing the 'certifi' package, version certifi-2018.11.29. To validate \ac{SSL} connections the following command has been implemented in the \ac{bash} terminal: 

\$ sudo /Applications/Python\ 3.7/Install\ Certificates.command \\
Jupyter Notebook, an open-source web application for Python, was installed by command: 

\$ sudo python3 -m pip install jupyter \\
It is used to store and share Python codes, equations and plot visualizations with legends. Running Jupyter Notebook was done by call: 

\$ jupyter notebook. All Python scripts were processed, saved and exported via Jupyter Notebook\index[concepts]{programming language!Python!Jupyter Notebook}. \\
Libraries and their dependancies were installed by pip: \ac{NumPy}\index[concepts]{programming language!Python!NumPy} and \ac{SciPy}\index[concepts]{programming language!Python!SciPy}: 

\$ python3 -m pip install numpy

\$ python3 -m pip install scipy
 
\ac{NumPy} is effective tool for processing arrays, sorting and selecting of read-in data from the tables. \ac{NumPy} creates a multidimensional array object from a given ‘table.csv’. Using Python’s syntax and semantics, it operates with matrices using logical, bit-wise, functional operations with elements, and performs a series of the routines for fast operations on arrays \cite{NumPyCommunity2019}. \ac{NumPy} is based on the object-oriented approach, mathematical and logical manipulations with table using 'ndarray'. 

Scripts, modules and codes were written using reference semantics and built-in functions of \ac{NumPy} and Python. Saved scripts included written codes that parse command lines and perform graphs plotting by executing functions and modules. The namespaces of NumPy were imported from numpy.core and numpy.lib by calling:

$>>>$ import numpy as np

The depending libraries have been installed as well. These include Jinja2, \ac{NumPy}, Pillow, PyYAML, Six, Tornado. Manipulating with tabular data stored in csv files was implemented in Pandas optimized for the high-level processing of tabular data. The Matplotlib\index[concepts]{programming language!Python!Matplotlib} library\index[concepts]{programming language!Python} was installed and imported to customize plots. 

Matplotlib library was selected as a main tool for graphical plotting by Python, as it enables operating statistical analysis and plot figures using ‘pyplot’ functions: plotting and adjusting the areas, visualizing statistical outputs, selecting colors for the plots, adding labels etc. Seaborn\index[concepts]{programming language!Python!Seaborn} library enables to perform refine plotting, modelling and analysis of a large set of marine geological data aimed at understanding poorly explained submarine geomorphic structure\index[concepts]{geomorphology} of the oceanic trenches. 

StatsModels library was used for linear regression applied for data interpretation. The aim is to investigate correlation between various factors: bathymetry, geomorphology, tectonic plates and their correlation. The methodological approach include data analysis, modelling, plotting and visualizing. Statistical modelling by StatsModels\index[concepts]{programming language!Python!StatsModels} include several tested algorithms: 

\begin{enumerate}
	\item weighted least square linear regression between geological variables;
	\item autocorrelation; 
	\item design matrix;
	\item \ac{OLS} regression;
	\item quantile regression\index[concepts]{algorithm!regression!quantile}
\end{enumerate}

Spatial and statistical analysis of the correlation between factors aimed at detecting of geological and geodetic factors\index[concepts]{geodesy} affecting steepness and shape of the trench. Following factors were analyzed: geology (sediment thickness), geographic location of the trench, tectonic plates and depths records along the profiles: max, mean, min values, and statistical calculations of the \nth{1} and \nth{3} quantiles. The study revealed correlations between sediment thickness, distinct variations in submarine geomorphology\index[concepts]{geomorphology} and sampling across the trench segments. 

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Data Analysis}
The profiles were analyzed by modelling in Python. A statistical modelling aimed at the analysis of the relationships between the attribute factors use  main existing statistical methods. During statistical testing and experiments, numerous approaches \cite{BoxTiao1992}; \cite{Timm2007}; \cite{Oliphant2007}; \cite{Oliphant2015} provided by Python \ac{StatsModels} and Matplotlib\index[concepts]{programming language!Python!Matplotlib} libraries were used as the core algorithms. The statistical analysis was done using table containing attribute values of the marine geospatial parameters. Topographic values of the profiles were derived from the table with depths of samples. Min/max and quartile values were read into table. Despite variations in density of the sample data caused by geomorphic shapes (ranging from extremely steep slope to gentle slope), it was possible to merge and integrate the results of the vector overlay.

Statistical analysis was based on Python libraries that include mathematical algorithms and common approaches for data processing and analysis \cite{DavidNagaraja2003}; \cite{Borradaile2003}; \cite{Cielenetal2016}. The \ac{KDE}\index[concepts]{algorithm!KDE} analysis was carried out in Seaborn library using mathematical algorithms described by \cite{Ciaccioetal2012}. Visualized results of combining five \ac{KDE}\index[concepts]{algorithm!KDE} plots for the bathymetric cross-section profiles are shown on (Fig. \ref{fig:AR-3}). It can be seen that combining five profiles enables to compare the geomorphology of the trench and significantly increases recognition of the variations, depth and heights from plotting single profiles separately. Thus, the range of the density distribution in the elevations across the archipelago and trench can be seen (Fig. \ref{fig:AR-3}). 	

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsubsection{Letter-value plots}\label{ssec:PySea02}
The features shown on the letter-value\index[concepts]{plot!letter-value} plot (Fig. 5) are distribution of the bathymetric data samples across 25 profiles. The statistical approach is derived from the Tukey's original boxplot\index[concepts]{plot!boxplot} principle \cite{Hofmannetal2011}. The letter-values\index[concepts]{plot!letter-value} plot shows a large number of quantiles in the bathymetric sample set, defined as 'letter values'. The layout of this plot is similar to a boxplot\index[concepts]{plot!boxplot}, modified and improved. Besides plotting a nonparametric representation of a depths distribution across the profiles, where data distribution corresponds to the observations, it plots more quantiles. 
	
\begin{figure}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=13cm,clip]{Fig-MedFAR-5.jpg}
	\caption{Mariana Trench: letter-value plot for data distribution by profiles, Python}\label{fig:MedFAR-5}
\end{figure}

Letter-values plot\index[concepts]{plot!letter-value} gives more deep inside into the shape of the bathymetric data distribution. Hence, it can be seen clearly that the maximal depths of the Mariana Trench are located in the south-western part of its crescent. Comparing to the boxplot\index[concepts]{plot!boxplot}, the letter-value plot\index[concepts]{plot!letter-value} provides more detailed information in the ‘tails’ using letter values on the bivariate big real data sets demonstrate to be more useful due to the more detailed approach on data analysis. The letter-value plots were done using Python code \ref{P:08}.

Technical implementation of the letter values plots was based on the existing methods \cite{Hofmannetal2011}; \cite{Hoaglin1983} embedded in Seaborn library. More sophisticated theoretical explanation on the boxplots\index[concepts]{plot!boxplot} is given \cite{McGilletal1978}). Comparing box plots code \cite{Lemenkova201908} with letter value plots \cite{Lemenkova201909}, the letter value plots are better suited to the marine geological datasets, as they represent detailed information in the tails by letter values (Fig. \ref{fig:MedFAR-5}) showing data distribution. The samples shown on the letter-value plot\index[concepts]{plot!letter-value} illustrate actual range of the samples (Y axe) by 25 profiles (X axe). The median, mean and quantiles are shown as horizontal lines on each ‘box’ representing the set of the samples across each of the profile. The plots for the 25 profiles include reliable estimates of their corresponding quantiles (as Tukey’s boxplot\index[concepts]{plot!boxplot}) showing outliers (profiles 2 and 3) labeled as observations beyond the most extreme letter values. 

Calculation of the pairwise Pearson correlations and plotting correlation matrix between the geological, geomorphic\index[concepts]{geomorphology} and bathymetric factors was done in R. Methods to plot correlation matrix (Fig. \ref{fig:GML9}) include R based data analysis that embed computing correlograms for the data arrays \cite{GolubVanLoan1996}. The methods and approaches of the Matplotlib\index[concepts]{programming language!Python!Matplotlib} and Seaborn\index[concepts]{programming language!Python!Seaborn} libraries used for plotting figures by Python language were derived from the existing literature describing statistical algorithms \cite{Hunteretal2018}. Finally, the geomorphic steepness of the slopes was tested (Fig. \ref{fig:GML10}) using ranking plotting algorithm available in R libraries \{ggsignif\}\index[concepts]{programming language!R!ggsignif}, \{graphics\} \index[concepts]{programming language!R!graphics}and \{tidyverse\}\index[concepts]{programming language!R!tidyverse}. 

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsubsection{Violin plots}\label{ssec:Py02}
Analysis of data distribution for depths across the 1000km hydrographic profiles using violin plot\index[concepts]{plot!violinplot} approach by script \cite{Lemenkova201910}. This was done using Python’s sb.catplot() function for categorical data analysis by code \ref{P:01}. The violin plot (Fig. \ref{fig:COMU-3}) combines a box plot with \ac{KDE}\index[concepts]{algorithm!KDE} aimed to analyze the distribution of the bathymetric data. 

\begin{figure}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=13cm,clip]{Fig-COMU-3.jpg}
	\caption{Mariana Trench: violin plots on depth ranges, Python, by code \ref{P:01} \cite{Lemenkova2019105}}\label{fig:COMU-3}
\end{figure}

Inside the violin quartile and whiskers are shown from the boxplot\index[concepts]{plot!boxplot}. As violin plot uses the \ac{KDE}\index[concepts]{algorithm!KDE}, the wider portion of the violin plot shows, the higher density of the bathymetric observations. Vice versa, and narrow regions indicate very deep or very shallow values with lower density, rare across the bathymetric profile. The presented plot shows distribution of the bathymetric values: depths along the profiles. 

The inter quartile ranges in the boxplot\index[concepts]{plot!boxplot} and higher density portion in \ac{KDE}\index[concepts]{algorithm!KDE} are placed in the same region of their categories within the violin plot. There is a notable decrease of depth in profiles 23, 24, 25 (Fig. 3), crossing Caroline Plate, which may be caused by changes in local geologic settings. Profiles 21 to 24 show the deepest depth in data range for profiles 21 and 22. Since tectonic attribute values differ significantly, a comparative analysis of the data variation across tectonic plates was undertaken. 

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsubsection{Hierarchically-clustered heatmaps}\label{ssec:Py03}
Cluster map\index[concepts]{plot!clustermap} was plotted by Seaborn function seaborn.clustermap() by Python code \ref{P:02} to group bathymetric values by ranges in 25 profiles. Cluster map is a method applied in this data analysis showing a matrix plot dataset represented as a hierarchically-clustered ‘heat’ map. Core technique of the clustermap implies graphical representation of the bathymetric data ranging from 0 to -10.000 m, where samples are located in cells of a matrix, represented as colors (Fig. \ref{fig:MedFAR-6}). 

\begin{figure}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=11cm,clip]{Fig-MedFAR-6.jpg}
	\caption{Mariana Trench: heatmap for the bathymetric data distribution by profiles. Python}\label{fig:MedFAR-6}
\end{figure}

Structured representation arranges depth values by their semantic similarity using clustering method ignoring outliers in colormap limits. To plot clustermap, Python assigns each of the 518 samples to a specific cluster of depths, divided into 6 groups with max cluster of depths >7.500 m. Cluster centers serve as local target points with largest node resting at the center and attracting other values of depths that belong to this cluster. Clustermap (Fig. \ref{fig:MedFAR-6}) highlights groups of samples with similar depths by colors assigned as increasing darkness to the deepest values.

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsubsection{Histograms plotted by Python}\label{ssec:Py05}
In a general mathematical sense, a histogram is a function $m_{i}$ that counts the Nr. of samples that fall into each of the groups (bins), while graph of a histogram is a graphical way to visualize a histogram. Thus, if \emph{n} is the total number of samples and \emph{k} is the total number of bins, the histogram $m_{i}$ meets the following conditions, Formula \ref{eq:12}:

\begin{equation}
	n=\sum_{i=1}^{k}m_{i}
\end{equation}\label{eq:12}\myequations{Mathematical definition for statistical histogram}

The data set contained environmental variables separately for tectonic plates to be analyzed. This required merging columns by category ‘tectonic plates’. Therefore, columns with the plate names were melted into one class to enable data analysis by plates. The data were processed by code \ref{P:04}, \cite{Lemenkova201906}. The histograms show underlying frequency distribution of a submarine topography of the trench (Fig. \ref{fig:COMU-7}). It allows data distribution (normal), as well as outliers, skewness. The data are distributed and colored by tectonic plates.

\begin{figure}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=13cm,clip]{Fig-COMU-7.jpg}
	\caption{Mariana Trench: histograms of data distribution by tectonic plates. Python. Source: \cite{Lemenkova201993}}\label{fig:COMU-7}
\end{figure}

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsubsection{Strip plots by Python}\label{sssec:Py06}
Visualizing strip plots\index[concepts]{plot!strip plot} showing the relationship between the sediment thickness and slope angles as compared by tectonic plates was done by Python code \ref{P:05}, \cite{Lemenkova201911}. The particularity of the scatter strip plots (stripplot()) lies in its nature: this is a categorical type of plot showing variable dependance (max depths by profile) upon another variable (tectonic plates). It represents depth values by 25 profiles sorted along tectonic plates. One can see the difference on data distribution. Pacific and Mariana plates are comparable, while Philippine Plate covers the majority of the data. As can be seen on Fig. \ref{fig:COMU-7}, the majority of the observation points are located in the Pacific and Philippine plates, following by Mariana, while Caroline only covers a few points \cite{Lemenkova201993}. Sedimentation processes differ significantly by four tectonic plates, due to the overall complexity of the sedimentation, trigger factors and mechanisms.

\subsubsection{Euler-Venn diagrams by Python}
The Euler-Venn diagram\index[concepts]{plot!Euler-Venn diagram} were used for visualizing logical relations between geological variable. It shows modelling intersection and correlation of various factors for four cases where overlapping in values or concepts are visualized. Fig. \ref{fig:GP-9} (A) shows cumulative total sediment thickness by tectonic plates. Fig. \ref{fig:GP-9} (B) shows detected igneous volcanic spots. Fig. \ref{fig:GP-9} (C) shows slope degree and Fig. \ref{fig:GP-9} (D) shows methodological overlay showing the approaches of this work: GIS data mapping and digitizing, geological analysis and Python and R programming languages. Fig. \ref{fig:GP-9} shows modelling intersection and correlation of various factors in the Mariana Trench project. In particular, it shows A): Bathymetric observations by plates, cumulative computing; B): Sediment thickness, slope degree and closeness of the volcanic spots; C): Subdivision of the slope steepness by classes; D): Methodological approaches. The Euler-Venn diagram\index[concepts]{plot!Euler-Venn diagram} was done by Python code \ref{P:26}, \cite{Lemenkova201945}.
	
\begin{figure}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=13cm,clip]{Fig-GP-9.jpg}
	\caption{Mariana Trench: Euler-Venn diagrams, Python \cite{Lemenkova2019105}, \cite{Lemenkova201945}}\label{fig:GP-9}
\end{figure}

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsubsection{Regression Analysis}\label{ssec:PySea01}

The regression models\index[concepts]{algorithm!regression analysis} propose that $Y_{i}$ is a function of $X_{i}$ and $\beta$, with $e_{i}$ representing an additive statistical error term (residual) that stands in for umodeled determinants of $Y_{i}$ or random statistical noise\index[concepts]{statistics!noise}, Formula \ref{eq:11}:

\begin{equation}
	Y_{i}=f(X_{i},\beta) + e_{i}
\end{equation}\label{eq:11}\myequations{Regression model}

Initially, 25 profiles were visualized by area chart\index[concepts]{plot!area chart} and facet grid\index[concepts]{plot!facet grid} (Fig. \ref{fig:MedFAR-3}) by code \cite{Lemenkova201920}. This gives an overview of the geometry (shape and steepness) and bathymetry (depths) of the profiles.

\subsubsection{Cluster heatmap and hexagonal data distribution and Kernel Density estimation}\label{ssec:PySea03}
Hexagonal mapping\index[concepts]{plot!hexagonal!mapping} is another useful approach for analysis of the big data sets when there are many observation points that may overlap. In this case, the data set comprised 12.950 points. Hexagonal binning\index[concepts]{plot!hexagonal!mapping} that plots density rather than points showing concentration between data values in specific profiles (Fig. \ref{fig:MedFAR-7}, right). Depth points are binned into gridded hexagons and distribution (the number of depths ranges per hexagon). As a result, it is displayed (Fig. \ref{fig:MedFAR-7}, right) using the density of color within the hexagons. The \ac{KDE}\index[concepts]{algorithm!KDE} is a non-parametric approach that visualized the probability of the density of a depth variables by profiles (Fig. \ref{fig:MedFAR-7}, left). Joint Hexagonal and \ac{KDE}\index[concepts]{algorithm!KDE} plots are drawn by Python code \ref{P:09}, \cite{Lemenkova201912}.

\end{figure}

The color palette was selected as ‘reversed’, i.e. the most deep values are visualized as light purple values, and vice versa. The x axis shows the bathymetric profiles, the y axis shows the observation points in each profile. The color shows the depth recorded values. The left y axis shows the sub-division of the observations into hierarchical dendrograms starting from the two groups and soon, grouped based on the similarity of the depths. The records of geologic data (sediment thickness), submarine topography (slope angle degree and steepness by profiles) and bathymetry (absolute depth values: maximal and mean) on the map was also analyzed with respect to relationship in the dependence of the parameters.

\subsubsection{Correlograms by Python}\label{ssec:PySea04}
Correlograms, or covariance matrices\index[concepts]{correlation!covariance matrices}, give the background for all standard multivariate techniques. Correlograms\index[concepts]{correlation!correlogram} allow to check randomness of data, and analyze data structure \cite{Friendly2002}. Three correlation methods research were tested (Pearson\index[concepts]{correlation!Pearson} \cite{Lemenkova201917}, Kendall\index[concepts]{correlation!Kendall}, \cite{Lemenkova201915} and Spearman\index[concepts]{correlation!Spearman}) \cite{Lemenkova201916} to visualize the relationship between the trench variables and representing a matrix of correlations schematically in forms numbers placed within the shaded squares (two of the three methods are shown on Fig. \ref{fig:COMU-9}).  

\begin{figure}[H]
	\centering
		\subfloat[Pearson's correlation (parametric approach)]{\includegraphics[width=7.5cm]{Fig-MedFAR-8.jpg}}
			\hspace{3mm}
		\subfloat[Kendal rank correlation (non-parametric approach)]{\includegraphics[width=7.5cm]{Fig-COMU-9.jpg}}
			\hspace{3mm}
	\caption{Mariana Trench: correlation matrices for bivariate analysis, Python}\label{fig:COMU-9}
\end{figure}

The \ac{UPGMA} algorithm constructs a rooted tree (dendrogram) that reflects the structure present in a pairwise similarity matrix (or a dissimilarity matrix). The mean distance between elements of each cluster is computed by Formula \ref{eq:upgma}: 

\begin{equation}
	\frac{1}{\big| A \big| * \big| B \big|}\sum_{x\in A}\sum_{y\in B}d(x,y)
\end{equation}\label{eq:upgma}\myequations{Hierarchical clustering method (simple agglomerative approach)}

The numbers give the degree of the correlation, ranging from -1.0 to 1.0. In the shaded rows, each cell is given the assigned color, explained in the legend, subdivided into four classes ranging from two to four depending on the sign of the correlation. The intensity of color is scaled in proportion to the magnitude of the correlation. Several variations have interpreted the criterion of similarity of the properties and inter-relationships. A represented clustering of the matrix (a. k. a. heat map), made by Python code \ref{P:10}, shows the variables with the displayed correlations. Plotting correlation matrix induces a partial-ordering on the geological variables as contiguous clusters. The covariance matrix $K_{xx}$ is the matrix whose (i, j) entry is the covariance estimated by Formula \ref{eq:cov}:

\begin{equation}
	K_{x_{i}x_{j}}=cov\left[ X_{i},X_{j}\right]=E\left[(X_{i}-E\left[X_{i}\right])(X_{j}-E\left[X_{j}\right])\right]
\end{equation}\label{eq:cov}\myequations{Covariance matrix formula}

where the operator E denotes the expected value (mean) of its argument\index[concepts]{statistics!argument}. The associated names of the environmental variables\index[concepts]{statistics!variable} (slope angles, sediment thickness, max/min and mean depth values) are shown on the correlogram (Fig. \ref{fig:COMU-9}) annotated on the axes. The matrices were done by \ac{NumPy} and Pandas objects read in as tables and imported by Seaborn library. The data were processed by Python code \ref{P:06}. The correlograms show dependancies between the factors affecting trench based on the relationships of the values. 

The effectiveness of the correlograms lies in its properties: the diagonal shows distribution of each of the geological variables by a representative square plot which helps to analyze the relationship between the pairs of numerical variables within a matrix. Correlation between each pair of variables is visualized through a numerical symbols and highlighted by colors. The use of both Pearson and Kendall correlation techniques (Fig. \ref{fig:COMU-9}) is used for exploratory analysis to observe a matrix of geological variables.

\subsubsection{Probability of the depths distribution by \ac{KDE} plots}
An implementation of the fundamental frequency estimation is presented by \ac{KDE}\index[concepts]{algorithm!KDE}. The algorithm of \ac{KDE} is based on a frequency-domain approach. It was applied to visualize probability of the depth ranges and bathymetric patters in various segments of the trench, Fig. \ref{fig:AR-3} and \ref{fig:KDEph}. 

Mathematically, \ac{KDE}\index[concepts]{algorithm!KDE} is a non-parametric way to estimate the probability density function of a random variable. The $(x_{1}, x_{2}, …, x_{n})$ is a univariate independent, identically distributed sample drawn from distribution with a density \emph{f}. Estimating shape of this function \emph{f} is based on its kernel density estimator by Formula \ref{eq:kde}:

\begin{equation}
	\hat{f}_{h}(x)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}K_{h}(x-x_{i})=\frac{1}{nh}\sum_{i=1}^{n}K(\frac{x-x_{i}}{h})
\end{equation}\label{eq:kde}\myequations{Kernel density estimator of the the probability density function}

where K is the kernel, i.e. a non-negative function and $h>0$ is a smoothing parameter, the bandwidth. This method was implemented using Python code \ref{P:16}. Python libraries Pandas\index[concepts]{programming language!Python!Pandas}, Matplotlib\index[concepts]{programming language!Python!Matplotlib}, Seaborn\index[concepts]{programming language!Python!Seaborn} and OS\index[concepts]{programming language!Python!OS} were used to process data by an embedded algorithms to obtain probability frequency. 

\begin{figure}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=15cm,clip]{Fig-GML3.jpg}
	\caption{Philippine Trench: \ac{KDE} of the bathymetric profiles, Python (Matplotlib and Seaborn)}\label{fig:KDEph}
\end{figure}

\subsubsection{Hierarchical dendrogram and clustermap}\label{ssec:PyMpl04}
The \ac{HCA} as shown on Fig. \ref{fig:EgeJFAS-5}, was done using \ac{SciPy}\index[concepts]{programming language!Python!SciPy} embedded algorithm of clustering based on the weighted average linkage clustering algorithm\index[concepts]{algorithm!KDE}, \ac{WPGMA} by code \cite{Lemenkova201918}. Mathematical background to the data partition is as follows. The \ac{WPGMA} algorithm constructs a rooted tree dendrogram that reflects the structure presented in a pairwise distance similarity matrix. At each step, the nearest two clusters, i and j, are combined into a higher-level cluster $i \cup j$. Then, its distance to another cluster $\kappa$ is simply the arithmetic mean of the distances between $\kappa$ and members of $i \cup j$: Formula \ref{eq:den}:

\begin{equation}
	d_{(i\cup j,\kappa)}=\frac{d_{(i,\kappa)}+d_{(j,\kappa)}}{2}
\end{equation}\label{eq:den}\myequations{Hierarchical dendrogram and clustermap algorithm WPGMA}

It demonstrates an approach of the method of cluster analysis used to build a hierarchy of bathymetric clusters based on the segmentation\index[concepts]{cluster analysis!segmentation} of the topographic features among 25 cross-section profiles. The dendrogram\index[concepts]{cluster analysis!dendrogram} (Fig. \ref{fig:EgeJFAS-5}) made by code \cite{Lemenkova201926} shows geomorphic similarity among profiles crossing the trench. Specifically, it illustrates the subdivision of the bathymetric values within each cluster composed by the U-shaped link between a main cluster and its branches representing bathymetric grouping. The dendrogram showing the hierarchical dendrograms clusters was plotted by Python code \ref{P:13} in five steps. 
	
\begin{figure}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=10cm,clip]{Fig-EgeJFAS-5.jpg}
	\caption{Mariana Trench: hierarchical cluster dendrogram of the profiles. Python}\label{fig:EgeJFAS-5}
\end{figure}

The choice of an appropriate metric influences the shape of the clusters, since elements may be close to each other according to one distance and farther away to the other. Two first forks of the of the U-link ('parents' of clusters) indicate which clusters were merged, while the length of each two corresponding branches visualize the distance between the ‘children’. Marginal dendrograms are represented on the Clustermap that shows another approach of data grouping in statistical analysis where main area of the plot show ranging data and marginal dendrograms – clusters of the similarity groups. 

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------	
\subsubsection{Principal Component Analysis (PCA)}\label{ssec:PyMpl06}
The \ac{PCA}\index[concepts]{algorithm!Principal Component Analysis} was made by \ac{PCA} function from Scikit Learn\index[concepts]{programming language!Python!Scikit Learn} library of Python (from sklearn.decomposition import \ac{PCA}) by code \cite{Lemenkova201924}. \ac{PCA}) is a statistical procedure that applies orthogonal transformation to convert a dataset of probably correlated variables to a new dataset of linearly uncorrelated variables, principal components. \ac{PCA} is mathematically defined as an orthogonal linear transformation that modifies the data to a new coordinate system. In this way, the greatest variance by scalar projection\index[concepts]{plot!projection!scalar} of the data lies on the first coordinate (\nth{1} principal component), the \nth{2} greatest variance on the \nth{2}  coordinate, etc.

Using orthogonal transformation procedure embedded in \ac{PCA} to convert a set of samples on sediment thickness and possibly correlated variables into a set of values of the linearly uncorrelated variables \cite{AbdiWilliams2010}. In order to maximize variance, the first weight vector w(1) in \ac{PCA} satisfies Formula \ref{eq:pca1}:

\begin{equation}
	w_{1}=arg\space max_{\|w\|=1}=\Bigr\{\sum_{i}(t_{1})^{2}_{i}\Bigr\}=arg\space max_{\|w\|=1}=\Bigr\{\sum_{i}(x_{i}*w)^{2}\Bigr\}
\end{equation}\label{eq:pca1}\myequations{PCA formula for the \nth{1} weight vector}

The $n^{th}$ component is found by subtracting the \nth{1} \emph{k-1} components from \emph{X} by Formula \ref{eq:pca2}: 

\begin{equation}
	\hat{X}_{k}=X-\sum_{s=1}^{k-1}Xw_{s}w_{s}^{T}
\end{equation}\label{eq:pca2}\myequations{PCA formula for the $n^{th}$ weight vector}

Using \ac{PCA} is especially useful technique in case of marine geological large datasets and other implications of the big geodataset with many variables for each sample for exploratory data analysis \cite{Journel2000}. The \ac{PCA} enables to better visualize variations: sediment thickness, slope steepness and depth ranges. 

\begin{figure}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=11cm,clip]{Fig-EgeJFAS-8.jpg}
	\caption{Mariana Trench: \ac{PCA} of the sediment thickness by 25 bathymetric profiles, 3D view.}\label{fig:EgeJFAS-8}
\end{figure}

The \ac{PCA} on the bathymetric spatial series was performed to assess the overall spatial geomorphic\index[concepts]{geomorphology} variability of the cross-section profiles and their similarities (Fig. \ref{fig:EgeJFAS-8}). For this calculation the sediment thickness as a categorical value of the studied area was used.

A point affine projection\index[concepts]{plot!projection!affine} of the dataset into a 3D\index[concepts]{plot!3D} space represented in classic (X, Y, Z) format is a technique approach of the 3D scatterplot matrices by code \cite{Lemenkova201927}. The 3D\index[concepts]{plot!3D} scatterplot matrices (Fig. \ref{fig:EgeJFAS-7}) show variability of the geological, geometric and bathymetric factors (that is, sediment thickness, steepness of slope degrees and depth values) that is similar to the model by four tectonic plates. The sediment thickness is broadly correlated with the slope steepness degree, showing consistent geographic patterns by two pairs of plates: firstly, Mariana and Pacific, and secondly,  Caroline and Philippine (Fig. \ref{fig:EgeJFAS-7}). 

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------	
\subsubsection{\ac{ANOVA}}\label{ssec:PyMpl07}
The purpose of the \ac{ANOVA}\index[concepts]{algorithm!ANOVA} method in Python part of this dissertation was to test significant differences between the means of the marine geological variables, that is sedimentation, tectonic and geospatial factors. The definitional equation of sample variance is as follows in Formula \ref{eq:an}:

\begin{equation}
	s^{2}=\frac{1}{n-1}\sum(y_{i}-\bar{y})^{2}
\end{equation}\label{eq:an}\myequations{ANOVA definitional equation of sample variance}

Where the divisor is called the degrees of freedom, the sum presents the sum of squares, the result is the mean square and the squared terms are deviations from the sample mean. The fundamental technique is a partitioning of the total sum of squares into components related to the effects used in the model. Test differences in means for groups of these variables for the statistical significance\index[concepts]{statistics!significance} was done by existing algorithms \cite{Gelman2005}; \cite{Montgomery2001}; \cite{Bailey2008}. The \ac{ANOVA} is accomplished by analyzing variance: partitioning total variance\index[concepts]{statistics!variance} into the components, due to true random error (i.e., within-group) and the components that are due to differences between the means. 

\begin{figure}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=13cm,clip]{Fig-EgeJFAS-10.jpg}
	\caption{Mariana Trench: one-way \ac{ANOVA} box plots, \cite{Lemenkova201923}}\label{fig:EgeJFAS-10}
\end{figure}

Computed F values (0.270259 and 0.459078) and statistical significance obtained from testing with \ac{ANOVA} shows the effect of depth and model scenario on the sediment thickness estimated through the \ac{StatsModels} library of Python (statsmodels.stats.anova anova\_lm) by embedded function \ac{ANOVA} by Python code \ref{P:15},\cite{Lemenkova201922}, and Pandas for table data processing. The correlation cluster dendrogram between a spatial series of the transects shows grouping between the profiles based on their geomorphic similarities. One-way \ac{ANOVA} box plots were computed and plotted to show relationship between the selected environmental, bathymetric and geomorphological\index[concepts]{geomorphology} variables based on the analysis of similarities.

Deep-sea sediments have consistent correlation with the slope class of the steepness, Fig. \ref{fig:EgeJFAS-10} (c). Lower effect has the location of the igneous volcanic areas near the trench on the geometry of the profiles, as referred by the angle\degree \space(Fig \ref{fig:EgeJFAS-10}, d). While shape of the trench is less steep and absolute depths are lower in the NE part of the trench (Pacific and Mariana plates), the bathymetry is substantially deeper in its SE part (Caroline and Philippine Sea plates). These findings also support variations in the deformation of the subducting plate along the Mariana Trench \cite{ZhouLin2018} detected by analysis of flexural bending and normal fault features. Normal faults initiated outwards and grew toward the trench axis, and the trench relief and maximum fault throws are greater in the S (5 km and 320 m) comparing to the N and C (2 km and 200 m).
	
\subsubsection{Flowchart network}
Data analysis is aimed to find potential influence from various geological and tectonic factors affecting geomorphology\index[concepts]{geomorphology} of the trenches. Statistical analysis of the data set in marine geology requires adequate tools for big data processing. In this context, a Python-based methodology is combined with \ac{GIS}\index[concepts]{GIS} geospatial data analysis. This thesis uses libraries Pandas, \ac{NumPy}\index[concepts]{programming language!Python!NumPy} and \ac{SciPy}\index[concepts]{programming language!Python!SciPy}. Data processing involves subsampling of the auxiliary masked data frames from the initial large dataset that only consists of target variables: sediment thickness, slope angle degrees and bathymetric samples. Flowchart\index[concepts]{plot!flowchart network} includes 3 stages (Fig. \ref{fig:AR-2}) visualized as logical parts: \ac{QGIS} data processing; statistical analysis; spatial analysis.
	
\begin{figure}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=10cm,clip]{Fig-AR-2.jpg}
	\caption{Methodological network, \cite{Lemenkova201905}, codes: \cite{Lemenkova201930}, \cite{Lemenkova201931}}\label{fig:AR-2}
\end{figure}

\nth{1} block consisted in processing marine geological data by \ac{QGIS}: data import, digitizing, data export into .csv tables, processing in Python. The cross-section transects were digitized. Their attribute tables contained numerical data on geology, tectonics, volcanism\index[concepts]{volcanism} and bathymetry by sample points along each profile. \nth{2}  block contained in data interpretation and approaches of the statistical data analysis. \nth{3} block presents geospatial analysis by visualizing interplay between geological and tectonic variables. Mathematical algorithms were applied by libraries \cite{Beazley2009} \ac{SciPy}\index[concepts]{programming language!Python!SciPy}, \ac{NumPy}\index[concepts]{programming language!Python!NumPy}.

\subsubsection{Design matrix and model fit summary by Ordinary Least Squares}
Variables of the geologic interest were stored in a table consisting of 18 rows where numeric information describes geology, bathymetry, geodesy\index[concepts]{geodesy} and tectonics of the trench. To fit most of the models covered by \ac{StatsModels} Python library, the design or regressor matrix was created using existing approaches \cite{Everitt2002}; \cite{BoxTiao1992}; \cite{MillmanAivazis2011} and code \cite{Lemenkova201934}. The first is a matrix of endogenous variables of sediment thickness, which show the response or geological regress and varying environmental conditions: geographic location, depth or tectonic plate. The design matrix ‘dmatrices’ shows the results of the first six lines representing values of explanatory variables in a set of geological attributes, Mariana Trench. The computing of the \ac{OLS} by StatsModels of Python using codes \cite{Lemenkova201935}, \cite{Lemenkova201936} based on Formula \ref{eq:10}:

\begin{equation}
	b_{1}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i}-\bar{x})(y_{i}-\bar{y})}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i}-\bar{x})}
\end{equation}\label{eq:10}\myequations{Computing Ordinary Least Square (OLS)}

Where, n is the sample size; x is a constant and a scalar regressor;  y is a random regressor, sampled together with x; h is the number of lags being tested. Each row of the calculated \ac{OLS} coefficient estimates is an individual bathymetric profile with the successive columns corresponding to the geologic and oceanographic variables and their specific values across the profiles. 

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------		
\subsubsection{Quantile statistics (QQ)}
Used algorithm is very straightforward with a selected function of qqplot() by StatsModels\index[concepts]{programming language!Python!StatsModels}. The \ac{QQ} regression\index[concepts]{algorithm!QQ} is a common abbreviation for ‘quantile by quantile’ statistical plot. The plot shows (Fig. \ref{fig:SM-2}, \cite{Lemenkova201989}) one quantile against another across various geological parameters: Sediment thickness; Slope angle degrees; four tectonic plates; distribution of the samples of igneous volcanic areas. Technically, the plotting was performed using Python code \ref{P:22}, \cite{Lemenkova201937} for each corresponding plot. The \ac{QQ}\index[concepts]{algorithm!QQ} statistics calculation has been based on the following Formula \ref{eq:qq} after \cite{LjungBox1978}:

\begin{equation}
	Q=n(n+2)\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{l_{k}^{2}}{n-k}				
\end{equation}\label{eq:qq}\myequations{QQ statistics calculation}

Where, n is the sample size; $rho$ is the sample autocorrelation\index[concepts]{algorithm!autocorrelation} at lag k, and  h is the number of lags being tested. The comparison of the six subplots\index[concepts]{plot!subplot} enables to analyze the form of their shape against a straight line. The quantiles are bathymetric sample observations with geologic attribute values placed in the ascending order. The \ac{QQ}\index[concepts]{algorithm!QQ} statistics is used over the pool of the sampling data to study their distribution. A \ac{QQ} statistic is a visual representation of the quantiles of a standard normal distribution of the geological data set across the trench, showing their variation in space. 

Quantile regression\index[concepts]{algorithm!regression!quantile} estimates conditional median\index[concepts]{statistics!median} and other quantiles\index[concepts]{statistics!quantiles} of the response geological variables. Upper two rows of the plot show (Fig. 4 in \cite{Lemenkova201989}) data distribution for sediment thickness\index[concepts]{sediments!thickness} (m) versus geologic parameters across tectonic plates, data distribution for the cumulative sediment thickness and slope angle degree by profiles. The quantile regressions were plotted by code \cite{Lemenkova201939} for the following variables: A) Sediment thickness; B) Slope angle degrees; C) Pacific Plate; D); Philippine Plate E) Mariana Plate; F) Volcanic spots, Fig. \ref{fig:ASE-4} by Python code \ref{P:24} by \ac{StatsModels}. 

\begin{figure}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=12cm,clip]{Fig-QQ.jpg}
	\caption{Plotted QQ statistics for the data distribution}\label{fig:QQ}
\end{figure}

Essentially, quantile regression\index[concepts]{algorithm!regression!quantile} is another approach of the linear regression used for the conditional median of the response geologic variable given changing bathymetric values southwards across the trench. The quantitative results of the quantile regression (Tab. 3 in \cite{Lemenkova201989}) show computations for quantile regression for the sediment thickness against geologic parameters and tectonic plates: A) Pacific Plate B) Philippine Plate C) Mariana Plate D) Caroline Plate E) Cumulative sediment thickness F) Slope\degree by profiles.

\begin{figure}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=14cm,clip]{Fig-SM2.jpg}
	\caption{Quantile regression plotted for sediment thickness (m) versus geologic parameters}\label{fig:SM-2}
\end{figure}

Each \ac{QQ}\index[concepts]{algorithm!QQ} profile has a given value for data distribution by quantiles across the trench profiles. The distribution of the geologic residuals show data correlation for several cases: frequency of data distribution by plates, sediment thickness, range of depths for max values and geomorphic\index[concepts]{geomorphology} shape as slope\degree. The results represent the computed numerical values of the quantile regression.

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------	
\subsubsection{Weighted Least Squares}
A \ac{WLS}\index[concepts]{algorithm!least squares!weighted} for the geological variables analysis data distribution: by tectonic plates, sediment thickness, bathymetry and slope\degree. Following variables are visualized: A) Pacific Plate, B) Philippine Plate, C) Mariana Plate, D) Sediment thickness, E) Depths (max); F) Slope angle degree.

A \ac{WLS} is a statistical approach representing a special case of the generalized least squares. \ac{WLS} is a standard approach in regression analysis to approximate the solution of the overdetermined systems for complex marine geological systems. The least squares algorithms\index[concepts]{algorithm!least squares} has two sub-types: linear or ordinary least squares\index[concepts]{algorithm!least squares!ordinary} and nonlinear least squares\index[concepts]{algorithm!least squares!nonlinear}.

\begin{figure}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=13cm,clip]{Fig-SM1.jpg}
	\caption{Weighted Least Squares plotted for data distribution.}\label{fig:SM-2}
\end{figure}

In scope of this dissertation, only linear least squares were tested: \ac{OLS} and its generalization, a \ac{WLS}. The off-diagonal entries of the correlation matrix of geological residuals are null variances of the observations, yet unequal along the covariance matrix. The calculation is based on the principle of the Gauss-Newton algorithm\index[concepts]{algorithm!Gauss-Newton} that solves a non-linear least squares problem by modifying a Newton's method for finding a min of a function. The computation is based on general approach of the existing equation \cite{Bjoerck1996}, Formula \ref{eq:gna}:

\begin{equation}
	S(\beta)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}r_{i}^{2}(\beta)
\end{equation}\label{eq:gna}\myequations{Gauss-Newton algorithm for non-linear least squares problem}

Where m functions $r=(r_{1}, …, r_{m})$ (residuals) of n variables $\beta=(\beta1, …, \beta n)$, with $m\geq n$

The calculations of the \ac{WLS} for the data se shows computations of the \ac{WLS} modelling for the data distribution by plates, \cite{Lemenkova201989}, done according to the reported procedures \cite{SeaboldPerktold2010}; \cite{Strutz2016} by the Python code snippet \ref{P:23}.

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsubsection{Isotonic regression}

The statistical numerical method of the isotonic regression\index[concepts]{algorithm!regression!isotonic} was applied as a non-metric multidimensional scaling aimed at fitting a line to a sequence of the observations provided the line has variation in forms, that is non-decreasing and or non-increasing everywhere across the observations. Furthermore, the line lies most close to the observations pool. The concept of the isotonic regression has been introduced by \cite{Kruskal1964} and further developed by \cite{BestChakravarti1990}. The principle of the computation algorithm of the isotonic regression lies in the following Formula \ref{eq:isr} of the problem of \ac{QP}: 

\begin{equation}
	min\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}(x_{i}-a_{i})^{2}
\end{equation}\label{eq:isr}\myequations{Isotonic regression function of quadratic program}

subject to $x_{i}\leq x_{j}$ for all (i,j) $\in E$, where $x_{i}$ and $x_{j}$ are constraints; E are the edges or the is the set of pairs (i, j) for each constraint; w is a weights vector; n is a number of observations. The isotonic estimator, g minimizes the weighted least squares-like condition by Formula \ref{eq:soc}:

\begin{equation}
	min_{g\in A}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}(g(x_{i})-f(x_{i}))^{2}
\end{equation}\label{eq:soc}\myequations{Simply ordered case for isotonic regression}

Where, A is the set of all piecewise linear, non-decreasing continuous functions; f is a known function. Further references on isotonic regression and its applications are given by \ref{Wuetal2017}. The presented plot of the isotonic regression (Fig.\ref{fig:GP-7}) base don code \cite{Lemenkova201942} shows a non-decreasing approximation of a function while minimizing the mean squared error on the training bathymetric data set of the 25 profiles. 

\begin{figure}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=14cm,clip]{Fig-GP-7.jpg}
	\caption{Mariana Trench: isotonic regression for variables, Python \cite{Lemenkova2019105}}\label{fig:GP-7}
\end{figure}

The benefit of this kind of a model is consists in the flexibility of the model, that is, the function does not assume any form for the target function such as linearity. Thus, the real data o the observed values by 25 profiles can be compared with the isotonic curves, as can be seen on the isotonic regression graph for the selected variables: geological data on sediment thickness layer (A) and maximal depths recorded by 25 profiles (B) (Fig. \ref{fig:GP-7}).
Further references are given by \cite{Leeuwetal2009}.

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------	
\subsubsection{Dynamic regression model: SARIMA}
The methodology of the dynamic regression model is based on the StatsModels\index[concepts]{programming language!Python!StatsModels} embedded algorithm \cite{SeaboldPerktold2010}. The abbreviation \ac{SARIMA}\index[concepts]{algorithm!SARIMA} stands for the model, initially developed by \cite{AnsleyKohn1985}. The concept of the application of the \ac{SARIMA} time series estimation and post estimation lies in the following. Changes in variables taken as time lags in classic dynamic regression models are applied towards profiles (1 to 25). Unlike in time series case when ARIMA fits the univariate models with time-dependent disturbances, this case applies space-dependent disturbances, crossing sampling (X axe), \cite{Lemenkova201989}. Given a time series data $X_{t}$ where t is an integer index and the $X_{t}$ are real numbers, an ARMA (p', q) model is given by Formula \ref{eq:sar}:

\begin{equation}
	\Bigg(1-\sum_{i=1}^{p'}\alpha_{i}L^{i}\Bigg)X_{t}=\Bigg(1+\sum_{i=1}^{q}\theta_{i}L^{i}\Bigg)\varepsilon_{t}
\end{equation}\label{eq:sar}\myequations{Dynamic regression model (SARIMA) definition}

The model includes both dependent and independent variables, the selected type of \ac{SARIMA} was \ac{SARIMA}X (Python code \ref{P:25}), \cite{Lemenkova201940}. The first group consists in changing variables: geologic settings and bathymetry (depths). The \nth{2} group (independent variables) is presented by the profiles lags that cross the trench with the distance between each of 100 km and the length of 1000 km. This cross-section profiles are taken as independent variables. Therefore, the dependent variables differ spatially in the segments of the trench. Python code for used \ac{SARIMA}X algorithm and Statespace Model Results, \cite{Lemenkova201989}. Tested case is a Mariana Trench tectonics: sediment thickness across the Philippine Sea Plate. The model fits univariate model of the geomorphic structure\index[concepts]{geomorphology} of the trench by independent values of the distribution of the bathymetric samples by profiles with dependent disturbances of depths. Fitting the model was done using Python code \ref{P:25}.
	
The conducted statistical data modelling and several types of the regression analysis were aimed to compare variance in the geological datasets of the trench explained by the complex interplay of the geomorphic, geological and oceanological attributes of the data and bathymetric factors of their location at several segments. Several linear models of correlation between factors were computed, analyzed and explained by the groups of variables. Tested environmental variables of the Mariana Trench include four main geological factors (location on the tectonic plates, slope steepness degree, sediment thickness of the layer and bathymetric depth and submarine volcanism\index[concepts]{volcanism}) and attributes of the 25 cross-section bathymetric profiles (mean values, maximal depths, median values and two quartile sub-division of the data sets) and the shared variances between environment and attributes. 

Shared variance arises due to correlations between the factor of sediment thickness and slope angle degree, e.g. because the attributes of the sediment accumulation are influenced by the trench shape apart from the directly or indirectly depending on the oceanological conditions of the submarine currents. The dynamic regression model is possible using Python function embedded in \ac{StatsModels} library: State Autoregressive Moving Average. The model shows autocorrelation of the data by bathymetric profiles. The results demonstrated a correlation between the geological variables and geospatial location of the samplings across the trench, which proves the interplay between multiple factors affecting submarine geomorphology.

\section{Classification of the submarine geomorphology}

Oceanic trenches present important part of the oceanic lithosphere. They are developed on the oceanward side of both island arcs and orogens that form above subduction zones. Deep-sea trenches are the largest linear depressed features of the Earth’s surface, notable for their depth and continuity. For example, the \ac{PCT} is 4500 km long and reaches depths of 7--8km \ac{BSL}. The trenches in the western Pacific Ocean are typically deeper than those of the eastern Pacific Ocean, the greatest trench depths, of 10--11km, detected in the Mariana and Tonga–Kermadec trenches. 

Following geomorphological\index[concepts]{geomorphology} profile shape types have been identifies and trenches are classified into seven types: U-formed (in plan), V-formed (in plan), asymmetric, crescent-formed, sinuous-formed, elongated, elongated to upside-down-ridge-like, cascade-formed, circular-like, arcuate, symmetric of irregular: left- or right-sided. 

For each geomorphological type (U, V, asymmetric and so on) characteristic steepness sub-types are identified: strong, very strong, extreme, steep, very steep. Furthermore, valley slopes are classified as follows: very high, high, moderate, low based on the degree of curvature. Hence, size classes and valley slope classes are analyzed in the context of physical environment as well as tectonic and geological development of the area of trench formation. Flexural bending shape of a subducting lithospheric plate has effects on the tectonic loading at the trench axis, strength as well as faulting effects of the subducting tectonic plate. Necessarily, it is reflected in the variety of the trench shape forms. 

Main factor controlling the max depth of a trench is tectonics: the age of the subducted oceanic lithosphere, since it determines the depth to the oceanic crust entering the trench. The remarkable contrast between trench depths in the east and west Pacific Ocean is largely explained by the systematic difference in the age of the seafloor in these regions. Generally, plate age (relatively young or old plates) is a prevailing factor in controlling trench cross-sectiongin shape, independent from the axial sediment loadings. Trench geomorphology\index[concepts]{geomorphology} and width of the cross-section profiles are mainly controlled by the elastic thickness reduced by tectonic faulting near the trench axis. In turn, they are affected slightly by the initial thickness of the subjecting tectonic plate. The reduction of the tectonic plate causes significant deepening and narrowing of trench valleys.

As seen intros dissertation base don the cross-sections, trenches are generally 50--100km in width. In cross-section they form an asymmetric V-shape with the steepest slope, of 8--20\degree, on the side opposite the subducting ocean floor. The sediment fill of the trenches vary greatly as demonstrate din this work, from virtually nothing, as in the \ac{TT} and \ac{KT}, to almost complete, as in the \ac{AT}. This is explained by the supply of sediment coming from the adjacent continental areas. Trench depth is also reduced by the subduction of aseismic ridges.

Bending of the lithosphere plate gives rise to the topographic bulge presented in the subducting tectonic plate on the oceanward side of the island arc. Regional rise of the seafloor bathymetry is located between 100--200km from the trench axis. It has an amplitude of several hundred m. The presence of this bulge is a consequence of the downward deflection of the subducting plate.

In this dissertation, a systematic mapping and analysis of their geometric shape forms has been conducted abseils don the detailed analyses of 20 trenches of the Pacific Ocean with a diverse range of characteristics (geology, tectonics and geophysical settings). 

\ac{PT}: irregular\index[concepts]{trench!profile!irregular} sharp V-shaped\index[concepts]{trench!profile!irregular V-shaped} trench (in plan) trench. The cross-sections of the selected segment have a strongly asymmetric view which is caused by the Philippine Islands (Mindanao) located on the west. Therefore, western part of the cross-sections has a smooth gradual decrease in depths in a uniform way from -75 to 40 km in the left flank with a slope of 31\degree. The \nth{2} segment stretches from 40 until 10 km of the left cross-section with a gradient slope of 40,60\degree. The last and short segment on the left (10-0 km) has a steepness of 28\degree. The right flank of the profile has following variation in steepness. A segment of 0-20 km has a slope of 44\degree, following by the fragment of 20-35 km (41,70\degree) and 35-50 km (25,0\degree). A long segment on the right (50-200 km) has a very uniform geomorphology\index[concepts]{geomorphology} with almost no significant variations and dominating depths at around -6,000 \ac{BSL}. The deepest segment of the \ac{PT}  (10-0 km on the profile from the center line) has more gentle slope with a steepness of 28\degree versus 44\degree on the right flank for the same segment. The cross-sections of the \ac{PT} have asymmetric profile caused by the adjacent Mindanao Island. \ac{PT} has a V-shape form in its deepest parts. Fig. \ref{map:PM12} and \ref{map:PM14} show vertical cross section transects perpendicular to the segment for \ac{PT}. A lower placed graph shows a median stacked cross-section profile (red line) and an interval of the error bars for the measurements. Variation in the trench gradient angles, steepness and elevation variation from the western and eastern flanks are caused by tectonic settings. 

\ac{KKT}: cascade-shaped\index[concepts]{trench!profile!cascade-shaped} trench. Variations in the geomorphology\index[concepts]{geomorphology}  fo the \ac{KKT} are as follows. Southern part reaches -8,200 m maximal depths while northern has -7,800 m. Trench morphology in the northern termination of the southern segment of the \ac{KKT}, just approaching the Bussol Strait is at the U-trending seafloor rise. Wedge-shaped form of the trench is being formed in its S part on the W trench slope. Area around the northern part of the trench identified features of the trench geomorphological system (submarine canyons, trench wedge, submarine ridges, and basins) in the N part of the trench. Wedge-like or U-shaped morphology of the trench in two parts S- and N are distinct from the inner-ward slope and in longitudinal direction along the trench axis. S profiles along the trench, comparing to the N segment, located near the intense volcanic area demonstrate erosional moats at -3000 m depths. Orthogonal bathymetric profiles across the S and N segments of the \ac{KKT} identify two distinct U- and V-shaped morphologic features with affecting dominant processes: (1) in its N segment, the dominant processes along the Kamchatka Peninsula are sediment deposition; (2) S part is distinct by the processes of erosion and intense sediment transportation. N part of the trench show deeper values and less steep slopes comparing to the S segment, which refer to the erosional moats and drift of the submarine sediments. S part has deeper bathymetric ranges and steeper gradient slope. This is influenced by the intensive seismicity and volcanic activities. \ac{KKT} forms more V- shaped valley in its S segment with about 3 km wide in profiles and more U-shaped form in the N part. 

\ac{KT}: elongated to upside-down-ridge-like\index[concepts]{trench!profile!elongated} trench. Profiles in Fig. \ref{fig:TK3} are roughly perpendicular to the trench axis. Latitude beside each profile indicates position of the axial bottom. \ac{KT} has gentle shape form of the western slope off Tonga Ridge\index[places]{Tonga Ridge} (Fig. \ref{fig:TK3}, B), abrupt shape with 2,641 depth records from -6,600 to -6,800 m (Fig. \ref{fig:TK5}). Comparing the deepest values >9,000 meters for the \ac{KT} there are  251 samples proving that \ac{TT} is deeper than \ac{KT}. For seafloor depths at range from -6,000 to -5,000 \ac{KT} has more values in this range: 6167 samples \cite{Lemenkova201994}, Fig. \ref{fig:TK5}. \ac{KT} has gentle slope and shallow depths which can also be seen on Fig. \ref{fig:TK3}. This illustrates tectonic and geological local variations, as well as different sedimentation of the \ac{KT} causing variations in their geomorphic shape despite their close location.

\ac{PaT}: irregular V-shaped\index[concepts]{trench!profile!irregular V-shaped left-sided} left-sided trench. Bathymetric profiles of the axis of the \ac{PaT} have very sharp yet irregular V-shape except for its northern portion. Such a form indicates bare bottom or very thin sediment cover of the axis. Northern portion about 25 km long north of 7\degree38'N has U-shaped bottom that becomes gradually wider in northward direction. Fig. \ref{fig:YPT7} shows bathymetric profiles of the \ac{PT}. The average dip of the landward slope is ca. 12\degree, whereas much steeper 10 to 50 m high scarps are occasionally take place. Barrow-like bathymetric pattern is recognized on the lower landward slope close to the trench axis. It may be a slumped block with no deep-sea terraces which have trapped sediments. If surface sediments or rocks are collapsed into the axis, the V-shaped bottom will be buried to form flat basin, unless subduction or tectonic erosion proceeds. Such avalanche appears to occur easily and frequently, as dipping angles of the lower landward slope exceeds slope failure limit in \ac{PT}.

\ac{MnT}: sinuous-shaped\index[concepts]{trench!profile!sinuous-shaped} trench. It is a relatively young, shallow and narrow subducting plate trench (ca. 15--36 Ma), small trench relief and width. Along the trench axis, the bathymetry is deeper and narrower at the S and C sections than that of the N area. Depth and geomorphology\index[concepts]{geomorphology} of the slope of the \ac{MnT} vary significantly for the depths range -3,500 to -4,500: minimal values are notable for the northern part of the trench where 526 observation points (18,2\%) are recorded for the depths -4,000 to -4,200 m, as shown on Fig. \ref{fig:M7} A. S part of the trench has steeper slope gradient from the oceanward part. N part is steeper in the continental slope part (Fig. \ref{map:M6b}). Submarine terraces can be noted on the N segment of the trench (Fig. \ref{map:M6b}) at depths - 2,000 m. Generally, model of the N part shows more steep degree of the oceanward slope ( Fig. \ref{map:M6b}). Gradually slanting terrace on the S segment can be seen on the fragment 50-100 of the cross-section with depths from -1,100 to -1,300 m. Two segments of the trench located on the N and SE part of the trench differ: N segment has shallower values of depths comparing to the S, but more steep slope on the Luzon Island side.

\ac{AT}: elongated. NW part of the trench on the side of the islands chain has steeper and shorter slope with located series of marine terraces. The average slopes of its northern side range between 3\degree\space and 4\degree, while those of its even shallower southern side are 1\degree\space or 2\degree 

\ac{MT}: V-shaped (in plan).  Large trench relief and width. Dominating are steep (7--9\degree) slopes and flat bottom. Gradient slopes markedly symmetric accurate 'V'-shape form in its deepest parts. Perpendicular transects cross-sections: steeper gradient slope on the N (Fig. \ref{map:PM14}, below) flank on the archipelago's side (Fig. \ref{map:PM14}).
