\pagebreak
\section{New Britain (Bougainville) Trench}
\ac{NBT} has asymmetrical U-shaped cross-section dipping westwards with 35\degree\space eastward slope, and 41\degree\space westward slope (Fig. \ref{fig:NBT6}, A). The \ac{SCT} has 33\degree\space slope oceanwards while 33,69\degree\space landwards increasing in relief more direct on the oceanward side. 

\begin{figure}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=13cm]{Fig-NBT6.jpg}
	\caption{New Britain and San Cristobal trenches: graph model of the cross-section profiles}\label{fig:NBT6}
\end{figure}

The increase in depth by \ac{NBT} is more gentle: in 50 km segment it reaches 2000 m \ac{BSL} (-7,000 to -5,000) on the Solomon Sea side. In the cross-section graph, the \ac{NBT} is markedly asymmetric in the landward slope and has an arc form in the east (Fig. \ref{fig:NBT6}, B). \ac{NBT} is shallower comparing to the \ac{SCT}: it has median <-7,000m. The highest sample values are recorded in range -5,000 to -5,200 m (272) gradually followed by ranges -5,200 to -5,000, -5,000 to -4,800 and -4,800 to -4,600 m (number of samples: 204, 189, 157). The depth to the bottom of the \ac{NBT} becomes gradually deeper in a segment of -50 to 0 m in cross-section, decreasing from -3,000 to -7,000 in WE direction, with maximum depths reaching -7,000 m and median value -8,000 m. The histogram has a slightly more skewed truncated view (right-sided) with visually dominating values in shallower depths, while diminishing values below -5,000 m \ac{BSL} comparing to the \ac{SCT} with bell-shaped normal data distribution.

Mathematic approximation of the gradient slopes for the \ac{NBT} and \ac{SCT} applied 3 types of the Fourier functions modelling the general trend of the profile curves (median) and residuals plotted for both trenches (example for the \ac{SCT} is shown on Fig. \ref{fig:NBT9}). Free-air gravity anomalies\index[concepts]{gravity!anomaly!marine free-air} Fig. \ref{map:NBT4} derived from the satellite altimeter measured \ac{SSH} have an anomaly range from -60.0 mGal and lower over the hadal trenches (deep purple color). The \ac{NBT} and \ac{SCT}es are distinguishable by the isolines. Lower geoid\index[concepts]{geoid} values (42-65 mGal, lime color) are notable for the regions north-east off the Solomon Islands. The tectonic and bathymetric complexity of the area (linear features, isolated constructs on the seafloor, seamounts) explains variations in the bathymetry of the \ac{NBT}.

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\pagebreak
\section{San Cristobal Trench}
The \ac{SCT} is more symmetric having accurate 'V' form for the segment of - 50 to -50 km on the graph (Fig. \ref{fig:NBT6}, A). The increase for \ac{SCT} is abrupt in profile: for the distance 0-50 km the depths rise from -8,000 to -5,200 m.

\begin{figure}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=12cm]{Fig-NBT9.jpg}
	\caption{San Cristobal Trench: modeled trends of the slopes gradient by profiles}\label{fig:NBT9}
\end{figure}

For the transect segment of -50 to 0 m in a cross-section the depth to the bottom becomes deeper decreasing from -3,000 to -8,000 in WE direction, that is, the gradient slope is steeper. Geomorphology is explained by the context of the historic and actual geomorphic and sedimentary processes that finally affected trench formation and structure. Associated to the oceanward side of the \ac{SCT}, several separated minor canyons and furrows has been defined (Fig. \ref{fig:NBT6}, A).

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=13cm]{Fig-NBT5.jpg}
	\caption{New Britain and San Cristobal trenches: segments of the digitized profiles}\label{map:NBT5}
\end{map}

The statistical comparison of the two trenches (Fig. \ref{fig:NBT6}) shows that the San Cristobal is generally deeper with bathymetric data reaching -9,000 m while \ac{NBT} has only 1 sample point <-8,000 m. The most frequent values for the \ac{SCT} lie in range -4,600 to -4,800 m (217 samples), -4,600 to -4,400 (202 samples) and -4,400 to -4,200 (167 samples). The majority of the observation samples for both trenches lie in range of - 4000 to -5,000 m. The geoid\index[concepts]{geoid} shows density of the topographic structures and the upper mantle lithosphere, the geoid\index[concepts]{geoid} anomaly or height can be high irrespective of the real topography. Therefore, as can be seen (Fig. \ref{map:NBT3}), the color of geoid for the Papua New Guinea Island has similar range (65-85 mGal) as Bismark Sea (orange color on the map).

Many factors affect deep-sea trench structure, dynamics and present geomorphology. The complexity of the processes happening at subduction zones in illustrated \cite{Woelkietal2018}: oceanic crust and sediment is recycled into the mantle, causing mantle melting and heterogeneity, arc magmatism, degassing and the creation of new continental crust. However, the trench is being formed at such areas of the tectonic plates subduction. The subduction and subduction-related volcanism\index[concepts]{volcanism} are the major processes through which geochemical components are being recycled between the Earth’s crust, lithosphere and mantle \cite{Yoshiokaetal2015}. A large proportion of the earthquakes and volcanoes are related to the subduction \cite{Kirbyetal1996}. 

\begin{figure}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=12cm]{Fig-NBT7.jpg}
	\caption{New Britain and San Cristobal trenches: histograms for bathymetric samples}\label{fig:NBT6}
\end{figure}

Deep-sea volcanism\index[concepts]{volcanism} results from a range of processes including dehydration\index[concepts]{dehydration}, melting\index[concepts]{melting} and melt migration. The deepest known earthquakes occur in the subducted lithosphere at depths of 660--700 km but their cause is still not explained. In addition, motion and velocities of the lithospheric plates at the Earth’s surface are largely controlled by the buoyancy\index[concepts]{buoyancy} forces that drive subduction. Because of the wide variety of processes involved, subduction zones can be regarded as natural laboratories through which dynamic behaviour in the Earth’s mantle can be studied. These factors explain variations in the submarine geomorphology of the trenches. 

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\pagebreak
\section{Philippine Trench}
\ac{PT} (Fig. \ref{map:PM12}) has a classic symmetric bell-shape form of histograms showing depth distribution with a notable sharp increase in data frequency at the interval of -5,000 to -6,000 m \ac{BSL} taking together 1,117 samples (22\% and 15\% of the data pool, i.e. 37\%). 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
 		\includegraphics[width=13cm]{Fig-PM12.jpg}
	\caption{Cross-section transect profiles: Philippine Trench}
\label{map:PM12}       
\end{map}

It indicates an abrupt gradual decrease of depths which can also be seen on Fig. \ref{map:PM12}. The cross-sections of the selected segment have a strongly asymmetric view which is caused by the Philippine Islands (Mindanao) located on the west. Therefore, western part of the cross-sections has a smooth gradual decrease in depths in a uniform way from -75 to 40 km in the left flank with a slope of 31\degree. The \nth{2} segment stretches from 40 until 10 km of the left cross-section with a gradient slope of 40,60\degree. The last and short segment on the left (10-0 km) has a steepness of 28\degree. The right flank of the profile has following variation in steepness. A segment of 0-20 km has a slope of 44\degree, following by the fragment of 20-35 km (41,70\degree) and 35-50 km (25,0\degree). A long segment on the right (50-200 km) has a very uniform geomorphology with almost no significant variations and dominating depths at around -6,000 \ac{BSL}. The deepest segment of the \ac{PT}  (10-0 km on the profile from the center line) has more gentle slope with a steepness of 28\degree versus 44\degree on the right flank for the same segment. 

\begin{figure}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=13cm]{Fig-GML2.jpg}
	\caption{Philippine Trench: faceted plot of the profiles. Plotting: QGIS \cite{Lemenkova2019104}}\label{fig:GML2}
\end{figure}


Unlike \ac{MT}, the cross-sections of the \ac{PT} have asymmetric profile caused by the adjacent Mindanao Island. Both trenches have a V-shape form in their deepest parts. Fig. \ref{map:PM12} and \ref{map:PM14} show vertical cross section transects perpendicular to the segment for \ac{MT} and \ac{PT}. A lower placed graph shows a median stacked cross-section profile (red line) and an interval of the error bars for the measurements. Comparison of the two graphs clearly shows the variation in the trenches gradient angles, steepness and elevation variation from the western and eastern flanks of the trenches. 3D\index[concepts]{plot!3D} bathymetry model of the selected segment of the \ac{PT} shows to the S (left) of the trench the margin is characterized by steep slope adjacent close to the Mindanao Island, to the N by sediment accretion, and to the NW by tectonic erosion. 3D relief map of the \ac{MT} showing selected segment of the Challenger Deep is characterized to the NW (left on the figure) off the trench a Mariana Ridge, to the NE a chain of seamounts\index[concepts]{seamounts}, and to the south by sediment accretion.

Comparing the \ac{MT} and \ac{PT}, seafloor bathymetry varies more notable westward of the trench axis by the \ac{MT}. West Mariana Ridge (Fig. \ref{map:PM14}) is located N off the trench axis at distance of 0-50 km, while N part of Caroline Ridge is intersected by the S transects of the trench profiles at a distance of ca.180-200 km. A prominent trench-parallel belt of seamounts\index[concepts]{seamounts} visualized on the 3D\index[concepts]{plot!3D} graphindex[concepts]{plot!3D} (Fig. \ref{map:PM15}), appears at 148\degree-149\degree E. A group of seamounts\index[concepts]{seamounts} eastwards off the \ac{PT} (Fig. \ref{map:PM13}), stretching in a south-east direction with a narrow seamount bases and shallow apexes appears E off the archipelago at the trench axis at 130\degree E-135\degree E. However, at an area of the plate subduction 127\degree E-129\degree E, seamounts\index[concepts]{seamounts} are absent and the seafloor has more unified pattern. Starting at 16\degree N-126\degree E, a ridge can be noted near the trench axis (Fig. \ref{map:PM12}). Marine free-air gravity anomaly\index[concepts]{gravity!anomaly!marine free-air} in the \ac{PSB} is presented on Fig. \ref{map:PM7}. Higher values ($>80$ mGal) of the gravity fields structure are apparent at volcanic areas and Philippine archipelago, as seen in orange to red colored areas on Fig. \ref{map:PM7}. Anomalies $>60$ mGal are evident in the trench areas Fig. \ref{map:PM7}, which are consistent with previous results (\cite{Yamazakietal2010}; \cite{Sandwelletal2014}; \cite{SmithSandwell1997a}). 

\begin{figure}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=13cm]{Fig-GML7.jpg}
	\caption{Philippine Trench: strip plots by tectonic plates and geology, Python code \ref{P:05} \cite{Lemenkova2019104}}\label{fig:GML7}
\end{figure}

The results were compared with existing 2D\index[concepts]{plot!2D} and 3D\index[concepts]{plot!3D} geophysical and geological maps, visualization of the slab gaps or tears in the \ac{PSP} (e.g. \cite{Ribeiroetal2017}; \cite{KatoJordan1999}; \cite{Otsuki1990}; \cite{Okamura}; \cite{HussongUyeda1982}; \cite{Ishizukaetal2010}; \cite{DeBarietal1999}) to highlight the geology, geophysical settings and geomorphic features of the submarine trenches in \ac{PSB} area. Comparative analysis of the \ac{MT} and \ac{PT} located on the margins of the \ac{PSB} is focused on modelling and mapping of the 2 oceanic trenches located on the margins of the basin of the \ac{PSP}: \ac{MT} and \ac{PT}. The aim was to analyze and compare variability of their geomorphological shape forms. 

Two segments in the southern parts of the trenches were transected by a series of the cross-section profiles. A segment of the \ac{PT} is located south off Mindanao, a segment of \ac{MT} -- in the Challenger Deep area. Samples received through transecting were analyzed and compared. A statistical analysis shown variability in depths by samples in both trenches. \ac{PT} has a classic symmetric bell-shape form of histograms showing depth distribution with a notable sharp increase in data frequency at the interval of -5,000 to -6,000 m (37\% of the data pool). The cross-sections of the segment have an asymmetric view. Western part of the cross-sections has a smooth gradual decrease in depths at -75-40 km with a slope of 31\degree. 

The analysis of the \ac{PT} geomorphology by Python and R shown correlation between changes in the slope steepness and geological variables. Variations in the slope steepness correlate with sediment thickness across the trench. Variations in the landform are explained by a combination of factors: geology, tectonics, increasing depths in bathymetry, caused by the tectonic slab dynamics. The results obtained by different statistical approaches performed using R and Python libraries are analyzed and integrated in order to provide a more detailed understanding of the character of the geomorphic structure of the \ac{PT}. The cross-sectional profiles give the insight to the variations in the geomorphological shape of the \ac{PT} and archipelago (Fig. \ref{fig:GML2}). The extreme depths values in the data set are notable for the following profiles: profile Nr. 12 (max depth: -9.700 meters), profile Nr. 13 (max depth: -9.600 m), profile Nr. 14 (max depth: -9.400 m), profile Nr. 18 (max depth: -9.450 m). All these profiles are cross sectioning the \ac{PT} in its central part where its geomorphic pattern can be characterized from ‘steep slope’ (profiles Nr. 12 and 13, see Fig. \ref{fig:GML10}) to ‘very steep slopes’ (profiles 14 and 18). Other profiles have depths not exceeding 9.000 m.
	
\begin{figure}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=13cm]{Fig-GML8.jpg}
	\caption{Philippine Trench: samples distribution by tectonic plates. Plotting: Python \cite{Lemenkova201914}, \cite{Lemenkova201943} and R \cite{Lemenkova2019104}}\label{fig:GML8}
\end{figure}

The strip plots (Fig. \ref{fig:GML7}) comprise two visualized relationship types of the data distribution, namely by seven types of the underlie geology with code given and explained in Table 1 (Fig. \ref{fig:GML7}, A) and two tectonic plates: Sunda Plate \index[places]{Sunda Plate} and Philippine Plate\index[places]{Philippine Sea Plate} (Fig. \ref{fig:GML7}, B). The distribution of the samples across the two tectonic plates is uneven (Fig. \ref{fig:GML7}, A). The Philippine Plate comprise more samples located in the Philippine Sea \index[places]{Philippine Sea} eastwards from the \ac{PT}. The maximal sampling can bee seen on the profiles 21, 22 and 23 in the southern part of the cross-sectioning (as shown on Map \ref{map:GML1}) with 765, 651 and 652 taken samples, respectively. This is explained by the complex submarine landforms in the western part of the Philippine Sea (Fig. \ref{fig:GML2}, the cross section profiles). Profiles 1-4 demonstrate (Fig. \ref{fig:GML7}, A) significantly lower density of the observation samples not exceeding 200. This part of the section is located in the northern part of the \ac{PT} and is characterized gentle landforms of the submarine geomorphology. 

As can be seen from the Fig. \ref{fig:GML7} (B), the Precambrian\index[concepts]{Geologic time period!Precambrian} (shield) rocks (P) are only present in profiles 5, 7, 24 and 19 and do not exist in the sample sets of other profiles. South tended data distribution is characterized for lithology types basic-ultrabasic plutonic rocks (Pb)\index[concepts]{rock!igneous!ultrabasic} where data are recorded for the profiles Nr. 12, 18-19, 21, 23-25, that is on the southern part of the Philippine archipelago. The metamorphic rocks (M) are noted across the profiles 23-25, 4, 6, 15-16, that is their distribution is more evenly spread across the study area. In this dissertation, data with complex lithology of Mesozoic\index[concepts]{Geologic time period!Mesozoic}, Jurassic\index[concepts]{Geologic time period!Jurassic} and Cretaceous\index[concepts]{Geologic time period!Cretaceous} origin (C) and Metamorphic rocks (M)\index[concepts]{rock!metamorphic} have comparable spatial heterogeneity with observation records not overcoming 80 sample observation points. On the contrary, basic volcanic rocks of the Cenozoic\index[concepts]{Geologic time period!Cenozoic} volcanic formations (Vb) have significantly higher values reaching maximal values at profiles 18, 17 and 15 having values of 120, 131 and 141 registered samples, respectively. 

Mixed sedimentary consolidated rocks of Cenozoic formation (Smi) had the highest detected values at profile 14 with 106 recorded points following by profile 8 (95 recorded samples). Finally, alluvial deposits of the Quaternary\index[concepts]{Geologic time period!Quaternary} have the highest data density recorded at profiles 16 (58 samples), following below by profile 13 and 15 (27 and 21 samples, respectively), that is the central part of the Philippines crossing the archipelago in the shelf area (Fig. \ref{fig:GML7}, B). The distribution of the sample points across the \ac{PT} has four distinct groups with the increased values caused by the steep pattern in the geomorphology (Fig. \ref{fig:GML8}, B): first group include profiles Nr. 5, 6, and 7 (northern part of the \ac{PT} crossing further Manila Trench). Second group comprises a group of profiles Nr. 13, 14 and 15: central transect of the \ac{PT} moving westwards towards the Negros Trench\index[places]{Negros Trench}. Third part includes two profiles Nr.  17 and 18. Finally, fourth part includes profiles Nr. 21, 22, 23, 24, that is a southern part of the \ac{PT} where the transects move further crossing Sulu Trench\index[places]{Sulu Trench} and Cotabato Trench\index[places]{Cotabato Trench} on the block of Sunda tectonic plate. 

\begin{figure}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=10cm]{Fig-GML9.jpg}
	\caption{Philippine Trench: correlation matrix of variables (Spearman method), Plotting: R \cite{Lemenkova2019104}}\label{fig:GML9}
\end{figure}

The opposite distribution is for the Sunda Plate (Fig. \ref{fig:GML8}, A): profiles 1-4 (northern Sunda) have increased values, followed by the \nth{2} group of profiles (8-12), then \nth{3} group (profiles 16-17 and 19-20) and profile Nr. 25 with max value for the Sunda Plate. The sediment thickness has a relatively even distribution for all the profiles, which is described by \cite{Hessleretal1978} who pointed that the sedimentation in the \ac{PT} is not significantly high to fill in the axis along the trench. However, the exception have profiles Nr. 2 and 3 with a distinct rise in the sedimentation volume caused by geologic conditions of the sedimentation in \ac{PT} \cite{Larsen1968} (Fig. \ref{fig:GML8}, D). SE part of the trench (profiles 17-21) has a distinct steeper geomorphic shape (Fig. \ref{fig:GML8}, C) which is caused by complex relationship between tectonic slabs and geological settings.  

Fitted model of the matrix shows (Fig. \ref{fig:GML9}) variables representing correlation between the elements of the trench system: elevation as topographic and bathymetric samplings (max/min, mean depth values and heights), sediment thickness, number of samples across tectonic plates (Sunda Plate and Philippine Plate), geomorphic elements of slope across the trench (tangent\degree, slope aspect), types of the geology (rock lithology). Absolute coordinates were excluded as a factor to remove the effect of the location. In total 15 variables were tested by attribute classes: geology: 7, geomorphology: 2, sedimentation: 1, tectonics: 2 and bathymetry: 3. Computed matrix visualizing impact factors is shown on Fig. \ref{fig:GML9}. The effects of the variations in the slope\degree\space were tested by computed tangent\degree\space for the profiles (Fig. \ref{fig:GML10} plot 1) and repeated the analysis by ranking profiles according to the slope steepness (Fig. \ref{fig:GML10} plot 2). Grouping profiles by slopes was performed by dividing the range into 5 classes: ‘strong slope’, ‘very strong slope’, ‘steep slope’, ‘very steep slope’, ‘extreme slope’. 
		
\begin{figure}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=11cm]{Fig-GML10.jpg}
	\caption{Philippine Trench: ranking geomorphology of the cross-section profiles. Plotting: R \cite{Lemenkova2019104}}\label{fig:GML10}
\end{figure}

The results highlight geomorphic variation and complex characteristics of the impact factors of geological origin affecting the trench. Location on various tectonic plates along with geological factors (sediment thickness) and lithology contributes in geomorphic shaping of the actual form of the \ac{PT}. Cross-sectional transects were taken in the southern regions, through key features such as deep valley, slopes, moraines, \ac{PSB} basin, and adjacent shelf area. The datasets were combined with multi-source grids explained in detailed 'Data and Materials'. 

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\pagebreak
\section{Manila Trench}

The results of the geomorphological modelling and mapping of the \ac{MnT} (Map \ref{fig:M6a}) shown spatial variation in the different parts of the trench. The characterization of the seafloor geology was performed by analyzing the trench and tectonic slabs location and areas of the submarine volcanoes. The profile transects were compared and differences in the geomorphic shape in southern and northern parts of the trench were revealed: southern part has steeper slope gradient on the oceanward part; northern part is steeper on the continental slope part. The submarine terraces are located on the northern segment of the trench at depths -2,000 m. 

\begin{figure}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=13cm]{Fig-M6a.jpg}
	\caption{Manila Trench: transect of the digitized cross-section profilesh}\label{fig:M6a}
\end{figure}

Depth and geomorphology of the slope vary for the range -3,500 to -4,500 m: minimals for the northern part of the trench with 526 samples (18,2\%) for the depths -4,000 to -4,200 m. The histogram for the northern part has bimodal distribution with two peaks. Southern part shows 142 values for the minimals - 3,500 to -3400 m. The statistical comparison of the data proved northern part of the trench to be deeper. The \ac{GMT} functionality shown in this paper enabled integration and interpretation of the multi-source data and approaches: automatically digitized profiles, geological mapping, 2D\index[concepts]{plot!2D} and 3D\index[concepts]{plot!3D} bathymetric modelling, statistical analysis, mathematical approximation for the trend modelling. 

Two different trench segments were analyzed aimed at detecting their spatial changes for an area covering \ac{MnT} and adjusting Luzon Island\index[places]{Luzon Island} (northern Philippines\index[places]{Philippines}). The south-east directed second segment (plotted as green line on the Map \ref{map:M6b}) shown steeper slopes on the oceanward side of the trench and deeper bathymetric records. On the contrary, northern segment (plotted as almost vertical yellow-colored line on Map \ref{map:M6b}) demonstrated softer geomorphic shapes yet steeper gradient from the Luzon Island side. The fine-resolution data such as \ac{SRTM}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!SRTM} grid was used to map bathymetry and topography of the study area and 1- min resolution from the gravity data (Jason-1, CryoSat).

The statistical results (Fig. \ref{fig:M7}) show differences in the depth ranges of the \ac{MnT} along the Luzon island. The depth and geomorphology of the slope vary significantly for the depths range -3,500 to -4,500: minimal values are notable for the northern part of the trench where 526 observation points (18,2\%) are recorded for the depths -4,000 to -4,200 m, as shown on Fig. \ref{fig:M7} A. 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=13cm]{Fig-M6b.jpg}
	\caption{Manila Trench: two segments of the digitized cross-section profiles}\label{map:M6b}
\end{map}

The histogram for the northern part has a clear bimodal distribution with two peaks. The second peak shows areas crossing the Luzon island. On the contrary, S part demonstrates 142 values for the minimal bathymetry with values -3,500 to - 3400 m; the second peak also shows areas of the Luzon Island. The comparison of the statistical data analysis clearly shows that the N part of the trench is deeper.

The southern part of the trench has steeper slope gradient from the oceanward part and on the contrary, the northern part is steeper in the continental slope part (Fig. \ref{map:M6b}). Submarine terraces can be noted on the N segment of the trench (Fig. \ref{map:M6b}) at depths - 2,000 m. Generally, model of the N part shows more steep degree of the oceanward slope (pairwise comparing on Fig. \ref{map:M6b}). However, a gradually slanting terrace on the S part of the trench can be seen on the fragment 50-100 of the cross-section with depths from -1,100 to -1,300 m. Hence, the comparison between two segments of the trench located on the N and SE part of the trench show that N segment has shallower values of depths comparing to the S, but more steep slope on the Luzon Island side.

As for the geophysical modelling, based on the patterns of the free-air gravity\index[concepts]{gravity!anomaly!marine free-air} map of the \ac{MnT} (Fig. \ref{map:M4}), high density materials are distributed continuously from the N the S Taiwan vertically southwards to the Luzon Island\index[places]{Luzon Island}, the Philippines\index[places]{Philippines} from -50 mGal to -200 mGal. The majority of the values in the South China Sea areas lie between the 10 to 30 mGal, while values >60 mGal can be noted in the island areas and southern Philippines. The results of the geomorphological modelling and mapping of the \ac{MnT} highlighted underlying tectonics conditions where the collision of two tectonic plates causes instability: repetitive earthquakes, submarine volcanism\index[concepts]{volcanism} and seismicity.

\begin{figure}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=13cm]{Fig-M7.jpg}
	\caption{Manila Trench: histograms of the bathymetry}\label{fig:M7}
\end{figure}

Strong topographical isolation and complex mix of water masses distinct trenches from other oceanic regions. Difficulties to study deep-sea life and habitats zone (6000-11,000 m) that presents the last great frontier in marine science are caused by remote location. Studies on marine biology are currently limited to the bathyal (200-3000 m) and abyssal depths (3000-6000 m), \cite{Ichinoetal2015}. Specifics of the benthic biology consists in the geomorphic shape of the trench: despite representing 1-2\% of the global benthic area, hadal zone constitute the deepest 45\% area of the vertical depth gradient \cite{Jamiesonetal2009}. 

Consequently, the communities of the \ac{MnT} represent spatially isolated environments, highly distinct from the shallower areas. It present a cluster of deep habitats that include features of the continental slopes, abyssal plains and unique properties of the hadal geomorphology affecting biological aspects. Hadal fauna survive on the fringes of two extremes caused by the hydrostatic pressure and remoteness from the surface-derived food supply \cite{JamiesonFujii2011}. The interconnectivity of the factors affecting hadal ecosystems is described by \cite{Xuetal2018}, who reviewed various aspects of the hadal biogeochemistry: the effect of food supply on hadal ecosystems, hadal carbon cycle in the sub-seafloor under high hydrostatic pressure and pollution in the hadal trenches. 

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\pagebreak
\section{Ryukyu Trench}
The statistical results (Fig. \ref{fig:R8}) on the modelling \ac{RT}\index[places]{Ryukyu Trench} show certain differences in the depth ranges of the \ac{RT} along the Ryukyu island arc\index[places]{Ryukyu island arc}. The depth and geometry of the slope vary considerably: maximal values of -5,500 to -5,700 m are notable for the northern part of the trench where 334 observation points are recorded, as shown on Fig. \ref{fig:R8} (A). With a minor difference, southern part demonstrates comparable values (329 samples) for the same depth range.

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=10cm]{Fig-R6.jpg}
	\caption{Ryukyu trench: cross-section profiles of the southern and northern segments}\label{map:R6}
\end{map}

However, more significant differences can be seen for the depths >6,000 m. Thus, the southern part of the trench (shown on Fig. \ref{fig:R8} (B) has only few values (9 samples) with depths >6,800m while the northern part shows 41, 59 and 23 records, with a maximal values reaching -7,460 m. Moderate to shallow depths with ranges from -3,000 to -5,000 m are comparable for both parts of the trench with values not exceeding 68 samples for the northern part and 42 samples for the southern, respectively. 

The forearc basin deeper than 2,000 m and shallower than 1,000 m \ac{BSL} shows more samples data in the northern part of the trench than in the south. In contrast, a wide terrace with depths shallower than 25 m is generally larger (161 samples) in the southern part of the trench. To conclude, the comparison between the two spatially different segments of the trench show that northern part of the trench has deeper depths comparing to the southern. This can also be noted on Fig. \ref{map:R1} where the deepest values of the central part are seen in the deep area extending seaward offshore from the Miyako Fault\index[places]{Miyako Fault} (Fig. \ref{map:R2}).

\begin{figure}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=13cm]{Fig-R7.jpg}
	\caption{Ryukyu Trench: models of the slope gradient trends for the profile curves}\label{fig:R7}
\end{figure}

As can be noticed on the comparison of Fig. \ref{map:R6} A and Fig. \ref{map:R6} B, the median stacked profile on the southern part (Fig. \ref{map:R6} A) demonstrates slightly more gentle slope shape with submarine terraces, while there are no clear on northern part of the forearc slope (Fig. \ref{map:R6} B). At the same time the depth of the forearc slope increases in the northern segment of the trench (Fig. \ref{map:R6} B). These changes of the slope geomorphology are also highlighted by the contour of the increased marine free-air gravity anomaly\index[concepts]{gravity!anomaly!marine free-air}, as a triangle shaped ‘island’ located at 126\degree E 24\degree N (Fig. \ref{map:R4}). 

Geometric approximation of the trend curves (Fig. \ref{fig:R7}) shows similarity between the segments while applying coarse linear model (Fig. \ref{fig:R7}: E and J; Fig. \ref{fig:R7}: D and I). Specifically, its shows modelling trend curves for general geomorphological transects and slope gradients using various mathematical approximations. However, the differences in the geomorphic variations become more notable at the polynomial functions (Fig. \ref{fig:R7}: C and H; Fig. \ref{fig:R7}: B and G).

\begin{figure}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=13cm]{Fig-R8.jpg}
	\caption{Ryukyu Trench: histograms of frequency of the depth distribution}\label{fig:R8}
\end{figure}

The geoid\index[concepts]{geoid} model shows geoidal undulation in the south-western part of the area east off Taiwan Island\index[places]{Taiwan Island} (Fig. \ref{map:R3}). Generally, the major part of the area has geoid values range of 25 to 35 mGal. The increased values (>35 to 85 mGal) can be seen on the south-eastern part in the Philippine Sea area, while lower values (15-25 mGal) bordering continental shelf stretch along the East China Sea and the lowest (<15 m Gal) mainly occupy land areas.

Various studies have been undertaking to analyze the complexity of the system of deep ocean trenches and reveal possible impact factors (recent studies \cite{Rijsingenvanetal2018}, \cite{Luoetal2017}, \cite{Yoshida2017}, \cite{Freymuthetal2015}, \cite{Ichinoetal2015}, \cite{CizkovaBina2015}, \cite{Boutelieretal2014}, \cite{Harrisetal2014}. Current analysis of the trench geomorphology and variability of the steepness indicate the importance of geological factors. Complex tectonic and geomorphic features of the trench was reviewed using a set of cross-section profiles with local geologic settings located in four tectonic plates. The results demonstrated dependences between the geomorphic factors and geology (slope angles and sediment thickness, respectively) on the profile morphology. 

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\pagebreak
\section{Palau Trench}
The results of the data analysis demonstrated spatial geomorphic unevenness of the \ac{PT}. The information and conceptual framework for the sediment thickness of the World Ocean published by \cite{Divins2003} was used in this research. Ranking geologic data has a step in geomorphic analysis where the levels of the slope degree are determined by the geospatial impact. Therefore, geomorphic variations typically necessitate an additional sub-task of the geological analysis of the study area. 

\begin{figure}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=12cm]{Fig-YPT7.jpg}
	\caption{Yap and Palau trenches: geomorphological modelling of the profiles \cite{Lemenkova2019106}}\label{fig:YPT7}
\end{figure}

Data distribution for the \ac{PT} has remarkable pattern: the amount of depths in range of -5000 m to -4000 m is large (737 samples at \ac{PT}). The same situation can be seen for the depth range at -5000 m to -6000 m: \ac{PT} has 152 samples. In general, \ac{PT} has more gentle slope both landwards and oceanwards. Topographic profiles of the \ac{PT} axis has U-shape. \ac{PT} has the majority of values higher and/or below -6000 m. \ac{PT} has more gentle geomorphic slope land- and oceanwards. Contours of the free-air gravity anomalies\index[concepts]{gravity!anomaly!marine free-air} (Fig. \ref{map:YPT3}) in general reflect bathymetric isolines (Fig. \ref{map:YPT1}). Comparing to the neighboring Mariana Trench, gravity field of the Yap and \ac{PT} are narrower (purple colored strip on the map). 

Free-air gravity\index[concepts]{gravity!anomaly!marine free-air} values reach positive above 100 to 500 mGal (red color) mostly over the area of Philippine Islands and small islands of the archipelago. Positive 70 to 100 mGal are notable over the shelf areas (orange color on the map). Gravity over the basin of the Philippine Sea generally fluctuates between -20 to 0 mGal range (light blue) to 0–20 mGal (cyan). The characteristics of the gravity field mirrors correlation between the gravity anomaly and geoid undulation\index[concepts]{geoid!undulation} as determined by \cite{Haxbyetal1983} for gravity anomalies from a grid of geoid undulations. The geoid model (Fig. \ref{map:YPT4}) visualizes subdued isolines in the Yap and \ac{PT}.

\begin{figure}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=12cm]{Fig-YPT8.jpg}
	\caption{Yap and Palau trenches: statistical histograms on bathymetry \cite{Lemenkova2019106}}\label{fig:YPT8}
\end{figure}

Trench formation is a very complex process that is known to be influenced by a variety of geological and tectonic factors. These may not adequately explain the process of trench deep formation, its further development and what is the impact in a variety of factors affecting hadal structure of the ocean trenches. Studies of the oceanic trenches based on the profiles cross-section have been undertaken in the past, e.g. \cite{Murray1945}, yet based on technical methods of that time. Until now, little attempts has been available on application of shell scripting towards modelling ocean trenches in marine geology, and this research contributed to this question.

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\pagebreak
\section{Yap Trench}
According to the statistical analysis (Fig. \ref{fig:YPT8}), the highest values for \ac{YT} are from - 4000 m to -4200 m (278 samples) with 19\% and 17\% of the total data pool, respectively. For the depths over -5000 m, the model of two trenches significantly changes: decreases for the \ac{PT} more clearly. 

\begin{figure}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=11cm]{Fig-YPT9.jpg}
	\caption{Yap trench: trend model of the slope gradient direction \cite{Lemenkova2019106}}\label{fig:YPT9}
\end{figure}

Data distribution for the \ac{YT} (605 samples): depth range at -5000 m to -6000 m: \ac{YT} has in total only 53 samples. In general, \ac{YT} has steeper slopes and generally deeper sample observation records. \ac{YT} has more deep values within range of -7000 m -- -6000 m: 68 samples, while \ac{PT} has only 34 samples for the same depths diapason. That means that \ac{YT} has dominating depths at deeper values. In general, \ac{YT} has steeper slopes and generally deeper sample observation records. Bare bottom of \ac{YT} may result from the thin sediment cover of the axis which proves correlation between the factors (geology, geomorphology, geophysics, tectonics, sedimentation) affecting trench structure and dynamics \cite{Yoshida2017}; \cite{Lemenkova201871}; \cite{Kimetal2009}. Topographic profiles of the \ac{YT} axis have very sharp V-shape. V-shaped trench generally indicates bare bottom of the \ac{YT} with very thin sediment cover of the axis.

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=12cm]{Fig-YPT6.jpg}
	\caption{Yap and Palau trenches: digitized cross-section profiles \cite{Lemenkova2019106}}\label{map:YPT6}
\end{map}

\ac{YT} has steeper slopes and deeper records. Topographic profiles of the axis of the \ac{YT} have sharp V-shape. \ac{YT} reaches -7000 m in its median curve of the profiles, while the \ac{PT} only -6200 m, that is the \ac{PT} is shallower. Second, \ac{YT} has distinctly more abrupt slope and steep gradient of the profiles oceanward (Fig. \ref{fig:YPT7}, B) comparing to the \ac{PT} (Fig. \ref{fig:YPT7}, A). \cite{Kobayashietal1997} earlier pointed that extensive slope failure is occurring on the landward slope of the \ac{PT} from which great amount of mass is wasted to the trench axis. Hence, if surface sediments or rocks are collapsed into the trench axis, the V-shaped bottom will be gradually buried to form a flat basin, unless subduction or the tectonic erosion proceeds. Submarine sediment avalanches occur easily and frequently, when dipping angles of the lower landward slope partly exceed slope failure limit. 

\begin{map}[H] 
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=13cm]{Fig-YPT5.jpg}
	\caption{Yap and Palau trenches: 3D mesh modelling \cite{Lemenkova2019106}}\label{map:YPT5}
\end{map}

With this in view, the comparison of the graduate slopes is important (example for \ac{YT}: Fig. \ref{fig:YPT9}). This can contribute to the explanation of the differences between two trenches in the geomorphic shape: as we can notice, inner slopes on the \ac{YT} are more steep and abrupt (Fig. \ref{fig:YPT7}, B) while the \ac{PT} (Fig. \ref{fig:YPT7}, A) has more gentle slope with sediment accumulation on the inner slope side. Fig. \ref{fig:YPT7} shows modelled profiles of the bathymetry of the \ac{PT} (A) and \ac{YT} (B) trenches. The average dip of the landward slope is recognized as about 29\degree for the \ac{YT} westwards 41\degree eastwards and while for the \ac{PT} it is 17\degree westwards and 18\degree eastwards, respectively. The topography of the \ac{PT} is more similar to an upside-down mound. Generally, \ac{YT} has abrupt shape with no marine terraces identified.

\pagebreak
\section{Mariana Trench}

Seafloor geomorphology of the \ac{MT} and of the \ac{PSB} were examined using high-resolution \ac{GEBCO}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!GEBCO} and \ac{ETOPO1}\index[concepts]{cartography!data!ETOPO1} topographic and gravity data grids in \ac{NetCDF} format, geologic vector layers and modeled profiles. 

\begin{map}[H]
	\centering
 		\includegraphics[width=12cm]{Fig-PM14.jpg}
	\caption{Cross-section transect profiles of the Mariana Trench}
\label{map:PM14}       
\end{map}

Profiles are visualized on Fig. \ref{map:PM14}. The statistical histograms for transects bordering the \ac{PSB} are visualized on Fig. \ref{fig:PM16}. The shape of the histogram varies by two trenches reflecting their geomorphology. The statistics on topography and interpolation approaches are shown on Fig. \ref{fig:PM16}. Histograms for the \ac{MT} demonstrated bimodal peak for depths -5,000-- -3,000 m with samples $<$10\%. In this interval, the \nth{1} peak is covering -4,500 to -5,000 m \ac{BSL} and the \nth{2} one is for the -3,800 to -3,000 m. Gradual decrease in data samples >5,000 m indicate a uniform way of data distribution for the depths range of -5,000-- -6,500 frequency 4--2\%, 285 samples. Depths at -6,200-- -10,800 m have a frequency $<$2\%. Shallower areas with depths $>$3000 m and $<$1,500 m \ac{BSL} have frequency <4\%, 400 samples. 

\begin{figure}[H]
	\centering
 		\includegraphics[width=11cm]{Fig-PM16.jpg}
	\caption{Histograms on depths frequency: Mariana and Philippine trenches}
\label{fig:PM16}       
\end{figure}

Gradient slopes of the trenches have markedly symmetric accurate 'V'-shape form in their deepest parts. Automatic digitizing of the perpendicular transects cross-sectioning \ac{MT} and \ac{PT}, demonstrated that \ac{MT} has steeper gradient slope on the northern (Fig. \ref{map:PM14}, below) flank on the archipelago's side (Fig. \ref{map:PM14}). Slope steepness on the left flank has a 33,10\degree\space at 0-15 km from the center from the vertical plummet. A segment on 15-25 km has a steepness of 45\degree, segment of 25-40 km -- 30,75\degree, a segment of 40-50 km -- 28\degree. On the right flank of the trench its gradient steepness varies as follows. The segment at distance 0-28 km has a steepness of 40,5\degree\space from the vertical plummet, 28-37 km from the start point has a 43,33\degree\space of steepness. It then becomes gradually becomes gentles to 30,43\degree\space until \nth{50} km. The segment at 50-90 km consists in the arc-shape gradual increase of the elevation which stabilizes after 100 km <-4000 \ac{BSL} (Fig. \ref{map:PM14}). 

The relief surrounding left flank slope of the trench presents uneven submarine terrain comparing to its right flank with more complex submarine relief and to the \ac{PT} which presents a more accurate and steep in its shape form. Results on geomorphic modelling of the \ac{MT} profiles revealed that the major depth samples of the trench are between -3000 -- -5000 m (Fig. \ref{fig:3-1-2}). Maximal depths reach up to -10,000 in the current dataset: profiles 20, 21, 22 crossing mostly \ac{PSP} (Fig. \ref{fig:3-1-2}). Decrease of depth is in profiles 23, 24, 25, crossing Caroline Plate. Profiles Nr. 23 and 24 have the deepest depth data range, \cite{Lemenkova201996}. Conversely, bathymetric profiles from 1 to 16 have gradual decrease in depths, which can be noted in outliers sample location. A slight increase in depths of the profiles Nr. 4-8 was also observed of the Fig. \ref{fig:3-1-2}.

\begin{figure}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=14cm]{Fig-3-1-2.jpg}
	\caption{Mariana Trench: graph of the 25 bathymetric profiles, QGIS \cite{Lemenkova2019105}}\label{fig:3-1-2}
\end{figure}

Changes in bathymetry may be explained by changed geological settings. Nevertheless, sediment thickness changes notably both within the trench by profiles and between tectonic plates. Since tectonic settings are not identical, the comparative analysis of data variations across tectonic plates was performed. The majority of samples of the profiles crosses Pacific Plate and Philippine Sea Plate, following my Mariana Plate, while Caroline Plate only covering a few points. Density distribution enables to compare overlapping and maximal aptitude of the density curves for the profiles and depth distribution by tectonic plates. Major trend of the trench gradient on the Pacific Plate has downward general line. The Philippine Plate, has a minimal peak by profiles \#14-21, then moving upwards. The highest values are for the Caroline Plate. Consequently, Mariana Plate has the highest density of depth distribution values, followed by the Philippine plate, then Pacific and Caroline, respectively.

From two multiple panel by groups (Fig. \ref{fig:8} and \ref{fig:9}) one can compare slope angles and depth distribution by tectonic plates, and patterns of these categories by plates. Summaries of variations of the local polynomial regression of the bathymetric depths of the measured samples are presented on Fig. 4 in \cite{Lemenkova2019105}. Upon a close examination of these summaries it can be observed that, with a mean depth value ranging from 5000 to of 3000 on the profiles, the widths of the confidence intervals are expanding more rapidly by the profiles 12 to 15 and 19 to 22 thus indicating on the large amplitude of the depths variations in this part of the \ac{MT}. The profile depth is being affected by local geographic settings caused by the location on the tectonic plates with varying geological conditions. On the other hand, profiles 22 to 25 crossing Caroline Plate \cite{Lemenkova2019105} suggests that absolute depth in this region change to become shallower than profiles crossing Philippine and Pacific Plates. This phenomenon has adverse implications for deep inter-connections between these factors.

Marine free-air gravity\index[concepts]{gravity!anomaly!marine free-air} anomaly in the \ac{PSP} area shows (Fig. \ref{map:PM7}) higher values ($>80$ mGal) of the gravity fields structure at the volcanic areas and Philippine archipelago. Geometric differences between trench shapes are explained by the context of the historic development and actual geomorphic and sedimentary processes affected their formation and structure. Current study presented comparative submarine geomorphic mapping and complex analysis of the geophysical and geological settings in the Philippine Sea basin area. 

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\paragraph{Bathymetric variations}
Data distribution\index[concepts]{data!distribution} are presented on Fig. \ref{fig:GP-3}. By profiles they ranged from -10.600m (maximal depth in this data set) to -3150m (the shallowest point in the data set). The maximum depths in the profiles were found in profiles Nr. 20, 21, 19, 14 (Fig. 3, right, sorted bathymetry), and the minimal depths (i.e. the shallowest) consist the first group, that is profiles 24, 23, 22 and 10 (Fig. 3, right, sorted bathymetry). The deepest part of the trench is located in the SW and central segments where the trench crosses Philippine and Pacific Plates. Moderate depths correlate with Mariana Plate and the majority of the Philippine Sea Plate. The depths ranges detected across the profiles of present study are also higher in southern part of the trench than in northern segment (Fig. \ref{fig:GP-3}). Central part of the \ac{MT} has variations in depth: the increasing depths towards S part were the depths are higher than in the N segments. By comparing the profiles Nr. 3, 4, 9, 12 from the Pandas array (Fig. \ref{fig:GP-3}, colored green) with profiles Nr. 18, 16, 15, 13 (orange on Fig. \ref{fig:GP-3}), the depths increase reaching its maximal depths in the following group (Profiles Nr. 20, 21, 19, 14, group blue, Fig. \ref{fig:GP-3}, right). 

\begin{figure}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=14cm,clip]{Fig-GP-3.jpg}
	\caption{Mariana Trench. Data distribution analysis: maximal depths by profiles, Python \cite{Lemenkova2019105}}\label{fig:GP-3}
\end{figure}

This step identified variations in the steepness in various segments of the trench. Slope steepness and depths were analyzed by libraries Matplotlib, Seaborn and Bokeh (e.g. \cite{Lemenkova201944}, \cite{Lemenkova201946}). The steepest part of the trench is located in NW, C and SW segments (profiles Nr. 3, 7, 9, 23, 24) where trench crosses Philippine and Pacific plates. This segment has the largest local depths and sharp abrupt changes. The highest depth gradient is at profile Nr. 21 at the distance of 10.600 m \ac{BSL}. Middle moderate slope\degree\space (Profiles Nr. 1, 11,  4, 5, 10) correlate with areas where trench crosses Mariana Plate. Fig. \ref{fig:GP-3} shows maximal depths by profiles, each profile has only one dot point on the plot, Fig. \ref{fig:3-1-2} illustrates the cross-section profiles. Profiles 19, 20, 21 and 22 had even geomorphic shape that decreased abruptly after sample 200. The unevenness of the profiles is explained by changed geologic conditions and tectonic plate (Philippine Sea to the Pacific, Mariana to the Philippine Plate). The groups of profiles 23, 24 and 25, (Fig. 9F, \cite{Lemenkova2019105}), have similar geomorphic shapes: the first halve of the profiles is relatively even while the S part is particular for the increased depths. For better visualization, profiles are colored individually with the shared X axes (Fig. 9, \cite{Lemenkova2019105}). 

Geomorphic segments of the Mariana Trench have relatively balanced elevations from -7.600 m (profile Nr. 11), -7.750 m (profile Nr. 4), -7.800 m (profile Nr. 5), -7.750 m (profile Nr. 10), to -6.200 m (profile Nr. 1). Depths ranges are shown on Fig. 10 \cite{Lemenkova2019105} (jitter plot). The profiles with the least slope degree (21, 22, 18, 20) are located in the S segments where trench crosses Caroline Plate. The depth values >-9600 m \ac{BSL} prevails on the bathymetric model for the deepest depth range, representing depths of the very steep slopes of the trench. Sediment sources are connected with trench tectonics where values of the sediment thickness are 140-120 decreasing to 50 mm. Sedimentation differs significantly by plates, due to the complex of the sediment processes and mechanisms. Among other are turbidity caused by gravity sliding, biochemical deposits, closeness of seismic zones, igneous volcanic areas. Besides, due to the funnelling effect, trench sediment deposits speed is faster and thickness is greater than abyssal basins.

\begin{figure}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=13cm,clip]{Fig-4-3-11.jpg}
	\caption{Mariana Trench. Correlation, k-means Clustering and Data Grouping, R \cite{Lemenkova201991}}\label{fig:GaCc}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Correlation and k-means clustering}
\emph{k}-means algorithm shows groups across all profiles, with the number of groups represented by variable. Several clusters were tested (2 to 7 (Fig. \ref{fig:GaCc1}), optical number is 5: cluster circle contained the number of samples and overlapping was minimal (Fig \ref{fig:GaCc1}). Correlation matrix is presented on Fig. 16 (\cite{Lemenkova201991}) showing crossing in the combination of factors. Comparison of the bi-factor shows pairwise correlation (Fig. 17, \cite{Lemenkova201991}). Comparative analysis (Fig. \ref{fig:GaCc1}) enabled to observe a marked influence on variables as bi-factors. In response to the decreasing sediment thickness the slope\degree\space goes in parallel; location of the volcanic igneous areas cause a cyclic repetition of the curve for the slope\degree. Igneous volcanic areas have correlation with the slope\degree\space and aspect\degree. Variable factors have distinct effect on the trench geomorphology: slope angle, tectonic plates, sediment thickness, aspect\degree\space and volcanic igneous areas, geographic location.	

\paragraph{Data partition and ranking}
Analysis of the angle steepness of the cross-section profiles along the trench revealed (Fig. \ref{fig:4-3-17}) that cross-section profiles form cluster groups with similar geomorphic properties divided into 5 groups. \nth{1} group: profiles 21, 22, 18 and 20, 'strong slope' tg\degree, ca. 0.05. \nth{2} group: profiles: 15, 19, 16, 17, 14, 2, class 'very strong slope', tg\degree angle of 0.057 to 0.058. \nth{3} group, 'extreme slope', profiles: 1, 11, 4, 5, 10 and 13, the average slope tg\degree, 0.060-0.070. \nth{4} group, 'steep slope', profiles: 25, 12, 6, 8, 3, slope tg\degree, 0.070-0.075. \nth{5} group, highest steepness, profiles: 9, 7, 23, 24, with average slope tg\degree\space up to 0.079. Although steepness is related to the slab subduction in this particular area, the nature of the slope expansion may be associated with other factors, e.g. topography, volcanism\index[concepts]{volcanism} and location. The data grouping has been created using libraries \{tidyverse\}\index[concepts]{programming language!R!tidyverse} and \{ggsignif\}\index[concepts]{programming language!R!ggsignif} for reordering and normalization of the steepness angle (Fig. \ref{fig:4-3-17}). Additional Chinese fonts (Fig. \ref{fig:GaCc1}) were activated through libraries \{extrafont\}\index[concepts]{programming language!R!extrafont} and \{extrafontdb\}\index[concepts]{programming language!R!extrafontdb}

\begin{figure}[H]
	\centering
		\includegraphics[width=13cm,clip]{Fig-4-3-12.jpg}
	\caption{Results of the \emph{k}-means clustering of the Mariana Trench with different \emph{k}-values, R \cite{Lemenkova201991}}\label{fig:GaCc1}
\end{figure}

5 classes were grouped: strong slope, very strong slope, extreme slope, steep slope, very steep slope. They show trench steepness according to the results of the cluster analysis. General trend has a scope of 0.03 (ca. 0.05-0.08) of the profiles steepness. Re-classification of the slope\degree\space across the trench and statistical normalization have crucial role in the analysis of the slope. Profiles nr. 23 and 24 have the highest slope steepness, located on the Philippine Plate. 2 large classes of the normalized degree angles were observed: \nth{1} below average\degree\space (profiles nr. 1, 2, 11, 14-19, 21-22) and \nth{2} above average\degree\space (all the rest). Steepness was shaped by the components of the igneous volcanic areas: profiles \# 20, 22, 23, 24 with notable amount of igneous volcanic observation points (>180) correlate with steepness\degree. 5 classes of the slope steepness are selected due to the changed geomorphic properties. Circular bar plots enabled to visualize depth distribution and slope angle values using R scripts, \cite{Lemenkova201991}.

\paragraph{Kernel density curves}
Mariana Plate has the highest \ac{KDE}\index[concepts]{algorithm!KDE} of depth distribution values, followed by the Philippine Plate, then Pacific and Caroline, respectively. \ac{KDE}\index[concepts]{algorithm!KDE} shown in a combined plot enabling to compare the overlapping and maximal aptitude of the density curves for the profiles and depth distribution by tectonic plates. Major trend of the trench\degree\space located on the Pacific Plate has downward general line trend. Philippine Plate has a minimal peak by profiles \#14-21 then moving upwards. The highest value for Caroline Plate has profile \#23, while maximal level for the Mariana Plate has profile \#7. Philippine Plate, has a minimal peak by profiles \#14-21 then moving upwards. The highest value for the Caroline Plate has profile \#23, while maximal level for Mariana Plate has profile \#7.
	
Composition charts (Fig. \ref{fig:8}), show comparison of the slope\degree\space by profiles. Basic concepts include interrelation of various factors (geological structure, bathymetric patters, tectonic plates location and subduction, spreading, transform fault, depths) affecting trench geomorphology. Comparison of the properties of the oceanic crust and ophiolite sections, sediment thickness and depth across profiles enabled to make a better understanding of the ties among geospatial factors influencing trench geomorphology. Tectonic plates, geometry (tg\degree), sediment thickness, geology (volcanic areas, earthquakes\index[concepts]{earthquake}), tectonics play crucial role in the geomorphology of the trench. Other factors include slope steepness\degree, angle aspect, bathymetry (depth at basement), means, median and minimal values, geographic location. A hierarchical treemap of impact factors generated using R code: \ref{R:39} is presented by code \cite{Lemenkova201808}, \cite{Lemenkova201871}. 

Mariana Trench is important integral feature of active continental margins in west Pacific Ocean. Its mainland and oceanward slopes have complicated steps of various shapes and sizes, caused by active tectonic and sedimentation. The steepness of the trench slopes averages in 4-5\degree. Tectonic plates have more steep\degree\space with average slopes of 10 to 15\degree, but their individual parts can be limited to steeper slopes as subjects to the gravitational flow system of the submarine canyons and valleys. Complex distribution of various geologic material on the adjacent abyssal plains contributes to the formation of the geomorphic features of the ocean bottom in the trench. Slope degree and amplitude contribute to sediment thickness, aspect\degree has lesser effect.
	
\paragraph{Spatial variations in trench morphology}
Quantified similarities in data distribution were assessed by correlation matrices and cluster analysis. Large gradients in variability occur within and among N and S part of the trench and in the E curve of its shape directed towards  transform lines near Magellan Seamounts\index[places]{Magellan Seamounts}\index[concepts]{seamounts} and Marcus-Wake seamount\index[places]{Marcus-Wake Seamounts}\index[concepts]{seamounts}. Geomorphology of the trench is dominated by following factors: closeness of the submarine volcanic areas, location of Philippine and Mariana tectonic plates, sediment thickness. Middle part of the trench (profiles: 14 up to 17), has very strong slopes, roughly equal proportions of sediment thickness. Variations in sediment thickness by profiles were defined as indirectly impacted from the changed slope\degree\space that gives conditions for sediment accumulations. If a certain segment of the trench is located near the active volcanic area, sedimentation rates increase. Profiles Nr. 1, 24, 25, 23 are notable for high sediment thickness on the Philippine Plate, profiles Nr. 2, 4, 6, 8 have the highest values for the Mariana Trench, profiles Nr. 3, 5, 8, 6 have the highest values of the sediment thickness for the Pacific Plate. Profiles located on the Caroline plate have the least values with only few samples. Five unique regions were compared, and impact factors affecting morphology analyzed by means of the statistical analysis.
